\begin{definition}[Multiplicity of a root]\label{polynomials:def:multiplicity}
Let \(f \in F[X]\) be nonzero and let \(\alpha \in F\) be a root of
\(f\). The \emph{multiplicity} \(\operatorname{mult}_\alpha(f)\) is the
largest integer \(m \ge 1\) with \((X - \alpha)^m \mid f\). A root of
multiplicity \(1\) is \emph{simple}; a root of multiplicity at least
\(2\) is \emph{repeated} (or \emph{multiple}).
\end{definition}

The largest such \(m\) exists: the powers \((X - \alpha)^m\) have
strictly increasing degrees, so only finitely many can divide the fixed
\(f\), and at least one does by the factor theorem. In an equivalent
characterisation, \(m = \operatorname{mult}_\alpha(f)\) holds exactly
when
\(f = (X - \alpha)^m g\) with \(g(\alpha) \neq 0\). Writing
\(f = (X-\alpha)^m g\) with \(m\) maximal forces
\(g(\alpha) \neq 0\), else the factor theorem would split a further
factor \(X - \alpha\) off \(g\); conversely, if
\(f = (X-\alpha)^m g\) with \(g(\alpha) \neq 0\), then
\((X-\alpha)^{m+1} \mid f\) would give
\((X-\alpha)^m g = (X-\alpha)^{m+1} h\), and cancelling
(\cref{polynomials:cor:cancellation}) \(g = (X-\alpha) h\), making
\(\alpha\) a root of \(g\).

The number of roots counted \emph{with} multiplicity is also bounded by
the degree; the proof uses the following lemma.

\begin{lemma}[Coprime factors multiply]\label{polynomials:lem:coprime-product}
Let \(f_1, \dots, f_r \in F[X]\) be pairwise coprime
(\(\gcd(f_i, f_j) = 1\) for \(i \neq j\)) and suppose each
\(f_i\) divides \(f\). Then \(f_1 f_2 \cdots f_r \mid f\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First let \(r = 2\), and write \(f = f_1 u = f_2 v\). B\'ezout
\eqref{polynomials:eq:bezout} gives \(s f_1 + t f_2 = 1\), so
\[
f \;=\; s f_1 f + t f_2 f
\;=\; s f_1 (f_2 v) + t f_2 (f_1 u)
\;=\; f_1 f_2\, (s v + t u),
\]
and \(f_1 f_2 \mid f\). For \(r > 2\), induct: by hypothesis
\(f_1 \cdots f_{r-1} \mid f\), and the product \(f_1 \cdots f_{r-1}\)
is coprime to \(f_r\)---a nonconstant common divisor would have a monic
irreducible factor (\cref{polynomials:thm:ufd}), which by Euclid's
lemma (\cref{polynomials:cor:euclid-lemma}(2)) would divide some
\(f_i\) with \(i < r\) as well as \(f_r\), contradicting
\(\gcd(f_i, f_r) = 1\). The case \(r = 2\) then applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Counting roots with multiplicity]
\label{polynomials:prop:roots-with-mult}
Let \(f \in F[X]\) be nonzero of degree \(d\), with all its distinct roots
\(\alpha_1, \dots, \alpha_r \in F\) of multiplicities
\(m_1, \dots, m_r\). Then
\[
f \;=\; (X - \alpha_1)^{m_1} \cdots (X - \alpha_r)^{m_r}\, g
\]
for some \(g \in F[X]\) with no roots in \(F\), and
\(\sum_i m_i \le d\). The number of roots counted with multiplicity is
thus at most \(d\), with equality if and only if \(f\) splits into
linear factors over \(F\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We first check that the powers \((X - \alpha_i)^{m_i}\) are pairwise
coprime. Each \(X - \alpha_i\) is monic irreducible (degree \(1\)), and
by unique factorisation the monic divisors of \((X - \alpha_i)^{m_i}\)
are exactly the powers \((X - \alpha_i)^{e}\) with
\(0 \le e \le m_i\). For \(i \neq j\) the only monic polynomial that is
simultaneously a power of \(X - \alpha_i\) and of \(X - \alpha_j\) is
\(1\), since \(X - \alpha_i = X - \alpha_j\) would force
\(\alpha_i = \alpha_j\); so
\(\gcd\bigl((X-\alpha_i)^{m_i}, (X-\alpha_j)^{m_j}\bigr) = 1\).

Each \((X - \alpha_i)^{m_i}\) divides \(f\) by the definition of
multiplicity, so \cref{polynomials:lem:coprime-product} gives
\(f = (X-\alpha_1)^{m_1} \cdots (X-\alpha_r)^{m_r}\, g\). Maximality of
each \(m_i\) gives \(g(\alpha_i) \neq 0\): a further factor
\(X - \alpha_i\) inside \(g\) would raise the multiplicity. Nor has
\(g\) any other root: a root \(\beta\) of \(g\) is a root of
\(f\), hence one of the \(\alpha_i\). Taking degrees with
\eqref{polynomials:eq:deg-prod},
\[
m_1 + \dots + m_r + \deg g \;=\; d,
\]
so \(\sum_i m_i \le d\), with equality precisely when \(\deg g = 0\),
that is, when \(f\) is a constant times a product of linear factors.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Formal derivative]\label{polynomials:def:derivative}
The \emph{formal derivative} is the map \(D\colon F[X] \to F[X]\)
defined on \(f = \sum_{i=0}^{n} a_i X^i\) by
\[
f' \;=\; Df \;=\; \sum_{i=1}^{n} i\, a_i\, X^{i-1}
\;=\; a_1 + 2a_2 X + \dots + n a_n X^{n-1},
\]
where the coefficient \(i\, a_i\) means \(a_i\) added to itself \(i\)
times in \(F\)---that is, \(a_i\) multiplied by the image of the
integer \(i\) under the canonical map \(\Z \to F\)
(\cref{ringsfields:def-char}). No limits are involved: the definition
is formal and algebraic, valid over any field, and is introduced for one
purpose, to detect repeated roots without appeal to analysis.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Rules of formal differentiation]
\label{polynomials:prop:deriv-rules}
For all \(f, g \in F[X]\), \(c \in F\), \(\alpha \in F\), and
\(m \ge 1\):
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textup{(Linearity.)} \((f + g)' = f' + g'\) and \((cf)' = c f'\).
\item \textup{(Product rule.)} \((fg)' = f'g + fg'\).
\item \textup{(Power rule.)}
  \(\bigl((X - \alpha)^m\bigr)' = m\, (X - \alpha)^{m-1}\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) Linearity is immediate from the coordinatewise definition of \(D\),
which is linear in the coefficients.

(2) Both sides are \emph{bilinear} in the pair \((f,g)\): by part (1),
each is linear in \(f\) with \(g\) fixed and linear in \(g\) with \(f\)
fixed. It therefore suffices to verify the identity on monomials
\(f = X^a\), \(g = X^b\), since every polynomial is a linear
combination of monomials and the general case follows by expanding
\(f = \sum_a u_a X^a\), \(g = \sum_b v_b X^b\) and applying the
monomial case termwise. On monomials,
\[
(X^a X^b)' = (X^{a+b})' = (a+b)\, X^{a+b-1}
= a X^{a-1} X^b + X^a\, b X^{b-1} = (X^a)' X^b + X^a (X^b)',
\]
with the convention that a term carrying the integer coefficient \(0\)
(the case \(a = 0\) or \(b = 0\)) is the zero polynomial.

(3) Induct on \(m\). The base is \((X - \alpha)' = 1\). For the step,
the product rule gives
\begin{align*}
\bigl((X-\alpha)^m\bigr)'
&= \bigl((X-\alpha)(X-\alpha)^{m-1}\bigr)' \\
&= (X-\alpha)^{m-1} + (X-\alpha)\,(m-1)(X-\alpha)^{m-2} \\
&= m\,(X-\alpha)^{m-1}. \qedhere
\end{align*}
\end{proof}

The proof of part (3) of the next theorem needs a repeated root in an
extension field not given in advance. The following lemma constructs
that extension by applying the quotient construction of
Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-quotient-Zn} to polynomial rings; its proof
transposes the argument that made \(\Z/p\Z\) a field
(\cref{ringsfields:cor-fpfield}) from \(\Z\) to \(F[X]\).

\begin{lemma}[Adjoining a root]\label{polynomials:lem:adjoin-root}
Let \(h \in F[X]\) be irreducible. Then the quotient ring
\(K = F[X]/(h)\) is a field; the map \(a \mapsto a + (h)\) embeds
\(F\) into \(K\) as a subfield, so that \(K\) is an extension of
\(F\); and the class \(\alpha = X + (h)\) is a root of \(h\) in
\(K\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The principal ideal \((h) = hF[X]\) is an ideal of the commutative
ring \(F[X]\) (Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-ideal}(2)), so \(K\) is a
commutative ring under the coset operations
(\cref{ringsfields:prop-quotient-well-defined}). Its identity is not
zero: \(1 + (h) = 0 + (h)\) would say \(h \mid 1\), impossible since a
factorisation \(1 = hk\) has degree \(\deg h + \deg k = 0\) by
\eqref{polynomials:eq:deg-prod}, while \(\deg h \ge 1\).

Every nonzero class is a unit. Let \(f + (h) \neq 0 + (h)\), i.e.\
\(h \nmid f\). The gcd \(\gcd(h, f)\) is then a monic divisor of the
irreducible \(h\) other than its monic associate, hence equals \(1\),
exactly as in the proof of \cref{polynomials:cor:euclid-lemma}(2);
B\'ezout's identity \eqref{polynomials:eq:bezout} supplies
\(s, t \in F[X]\) with \(sf + th = 1\), and reducing modulo \((h)\)
gives \((s + (h))(f + (h)) = 1 + (h)\). So \(K\) is a field.

The map \(\iota(a) = a + (h)\) on constants preserves sums, products,
and \(1\) directly from the coset operations of
\cref{ringsfields:def-quotient}, and it is injective: \(\iota(a) = 0\)
with \(a \neq 0\) would make \(h\) divide a nonzero constant, again
impossible by degrees. We identify \(F\) with its image, making \(K\)
an extension field of \(F\).

Finally, write \(h = \sum_i c_i X^i\) and compute in \(K\):
\[
h(\alpha)
= \sum_i \iota(c_i)\,\bigl(X + (h)\bigr)^{i}
= \Bigl(\sum_i c_i X^i\Bigr) + (h)
= h + (h) = 0 + (h),
\]
so \(\alpha\) is a root of \(h\) in \(K\).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Derivative test for repeated roots; separability]
\label{polynomials:thm:multiple-root}
Let \(f \in F[X]\) be nonzero and \(\alpha \in F\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item The element \(\alpha\) is a repeated root of \(f\)---a root of
  multiplicity at least \(2\)---if and only if
  \(f(\alpha) = 0\) and \(f'(\alpha) = 0\).
\item If \(f\) has a repeated root in \(F\), then
  \(\gcd(f, f') \neq 1\).
\item Call \(f\) \emph{separable} if it has no repeated root in any
  field containing \(F\). Then \(f\) is separable if and only if
  \(\gcd(f, f') = 1\) in \(F[X]\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(1) Let \(\alpha\) be a root of multiplicity \(m \ge 1\) and write
\(f = (X - \alpha)^m g\) with \(g(\alpha) \neq 0\). The product and
power rules give
\[
f' \;=\; m\,(X-\alpha)^{m-1} g + (X-\alpha)^m g'
\;=\; (X - \alpha)^{m-1}\,\bigl[\, m g + (X - \alpha)\, g' \,\bigr].
\]
If \(m = 1\), the first factor is \(1\) and evaluating at \(\alpha\)
gives \(f'(\alpha) = 1 \cdot g(\alpha) = g(\alpha) \neq 0\): a simple
root of \(f\) is not a root of \(f'\). If \(m \ge 2\), then
\((X - \alpha)^{m-1}\) divides \(f'\) and vanishes at \(\alpha\), so
\(f'(\alpha) = 0\). Together with the factor theorem for
\(f(\alpha) = 0\), this proves the equivalence.

(2) A repeated root \(\alpha \in F\) is a common root of \(f\) and
\(f'\) by (1), so the factor theorem puts the common factor
\(X - \alpha\) into both, and \((X - \alpha) \mid \gcd(f, f')\).

(3) The argument rests on the fact that \emph{gcds are stable under
field extension}: for an extension field \(K\) of \(F\)
(\cref{ringsfields:def-extension}), the Euclidean algorithm of
\cref{polynomials:thm:euclid} run on \(f, f' \in F[X]\) uses only field
operations on their coefficients, which lie in \(F\); run inside \(K[X]\)
on the same inputs it produces the identical quotients and remainders.
Hence the gcd computed in \(K[X]\) equals the gcd computed in \(F[X]\).

Suppose \(\gcd(f, f') = 1\) in \(F[X]\), and let \(K \supseteq F\) be
any extension. By stability, \(\gcd(f, f') = 1\) in \(K[X]\) as well,
so by (2) applied over \(K\), the polynomial \(f\) has no repeated root
in \(K\): \(f\) is separable.

Conversely, suppose \(d = \gcd(f, f')\) is nonconstant; we exhibit a
repeated root in some extension. Let \(h\) be a monic irreducible
factor of \(d\) (\cref{polynomials:thm:ufd}), and let
\(K = F[X]/(h)\) be the extension field supplied by
\cref{polynomials:lem:adjoin-root}, in which \(h\) has a root
\(\alpha\). The polynomial \(h\) divides \(d\), which divides both
\(f\) and \(f'\); evaluating the factorisations at \(\alpha\)
(\cref{polynomials:prop:eval-hom}) gives
\(f(\alpha) = f'(\alpha) = 0\). By (1), applied over \(K\), the
element \(\alpha\) is a repeated root of \(f\) in \(K\): \(f\) is not
separable.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal derivatives in positive characteristic]
\label{polynomials:rem:char-p}
The integer coefficient \(i\) in \(f' = \sum_i i\, a_i X^{i-1}\) is
interpreted \emph{in} \(F\), and may vanish there. Over a field of
characteristic \(p\)---where \(p = 0\) in \(F\), \(p\) prime, the
setting of \(\Fp\) and \(\Fq\)
(\cref{ringsfields:def-char})---the derivative of \(X^p\) is
\(p\, X^{p-1} = 0\). Thus \(f = X^p - 1\) over \(\Fp\) has \(f' = 0\)
and \(\gcd(f, f') = \gcd(f, 0) = f \neq 1\); correspondingly,
\[
X^p - 1 \;=\; (X - 1)^p \quad\text{in } \Fp[X],
\]
a factorisation the finite-fields section establishes through the
freshman's-dream identity \((a+b)^p = a^p + b^p\) of characteristic
\(p\) (\cref{finitefields:lem:freshman}, applied to \((X + (-1))^p\)
together with \((-1)^p = -1\) in every \(\Fp\)): a single root \(1\)
of multiplicity \(p\). Arguments over the finite fields of Orchard must
account for this characteristic-\(p\) behaviour.
\end{remark}
\subsection{Polynomials versus polynomial functions}
\label{polynomials:sub:formalfn}

This subsection determines when a polynomial may be identified with the
function it induces.

\begin{definition}[Polynomial function]\label{polynomials:def:polyfn}
Every \(f \in F[X]\) induces a \emph{polynomial function}
\[
\tilde{f}\colon F \to F,
\qquad
\alpha \longmapsto f(\alpha).
\]
The assignment \(f \mapsto \tilde{f}\) is a ring homomorphism from
\(F[X]\) to the ring \(F^F\) of \emph{all} functions \(F \to F\) under
pointwise operations
(\((u+v)(\alpha) = u(\alpha) + v(\alpha)\) and
\((uv)(\alpha) = u(\alpha)v(\alpha)\)), by
\cref{polynomials:prop:eval-hom} applied at every point.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[When formal equals functional]
\label{polynomials:prop:formal-vs-fn}
Let \(f, g \in F[X]\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item If \(F\) is infinite, then \(\tilde{f} = \tilde{g}\) as functions
  implies \(f = g\) as polynomials. Equivalently,
  \(f \mapsto \tilde{f}\) is injective, and polynomials over an
  infinite field may be identified with polynomial functions.
\item If \(F\) is finite, the map is \emph{not} injective. Over
  \(\Fp\), the nonzero polynomial \(X^p - X\) induces the zero
  function, since Fermat's little theorem
  (Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-euler-fermat}) gives
  \(\alpha^p = \alpha\) for every \(\alpha \in \Fp\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) Let \(h = f - g\), so that \(\tilde{h}\) is the zero function:
\(h\) vanishes at every point of the infinite set \(F\). Were \(h\)
nonzero of degree \(d\), it would have at most \(d\) roots
(\cref{polynomials:thm:root-bound}), yet it has infinitely many---a
contradiction. Hence \(h = 0\).

(2) Let \(F\) be finite, with \(q = \abs{F}\) elements, and consider
the \emph{vanishing-polynomial witness}
\[
w(X) \;=\; \prod_{\alpha \in F} (X - \alpha),
\]
a monic polynomial of degree \(q\)---in particular nonzero as a formal
object---which vanishes at every point of \(F\) by construction. Thus
\(\tilde{w} = \tilde{0}\) while \(w \neq 0\): the map
\(f \mapsto \tilde{f}\) identifies distinct polynomials. Over
\(F = \Fp\) the witness specialises to \(X^p - X\): by Fermat's little
theorem every element of \(\Fp\) is a root of the monic degree-\(p\)
polynomial \(X^p - X\), so
\cref{polynomials:prop:roots-with-mult} factors it as
\(\prod_{\alpha \in \Fp}(X - \alpha)\)---the \(p\) distinct linear
factors exhaust the degree---and \(X^p - X\) induces the zero function
despite having degree \(p \ge 1\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why polynomials are defined formally]
\label{polynomials:rem:formal-right}
Part (2) of \cref{polynomials:prop:formal-vs-fn} is the reason
polynomials are defined as coefficient sequences and not as functions.
Cryptography works over finite fields, where the functional view loses
information: the polynomials \(X\) and \(X^p\) induce the same function
on \(\Fp\), yet have degrees \(1\) and \(p\). The security of polynomial
commitment schemes rests on the \emph{degree}, a property of the formal
object invisible to the induced function over a finite field. A prover
commits to a low-degree \emph{formal} polynomial, and the verifier tests it by
\emph{evaluation}. The Schwartz--Zippel lemma quantifies the gap between
agreement at the tested points and identity to the claimed formal
polynomial.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The Schwartz--Zippel lemma}\label{polynomials:sub:sz}

To draw \(\alpha\) \emph{uniformly at random} from a finite nonempty set
\(S\) means to select each element with equal likelihood \(1/\abs{S}\);
the \emph{probability} of an event is then the fraction of elements of
\(S\) producing it, so that
\(\Pr[f(\alpha) = 0] = \abs{\{\alpha \in S : f(\alpha) = 0\}} /
\abs{S}\). The theorem needs only this counting case; probability is
developed later in the volume.

\begin{theorem}[Univariate Schwartz--Zippel]
\label{polynomials:thm:schwartz-zippel}
Let \(f \in F[X]\) be nonzero of degree at most \(d\), let
\(S \subseteq F\) be finite and nonempty, and let \(\alpha\) be drawn
uniformly at random from \(S\). Then
\begin{equation}\label{polynomials:eq:schwartz-zippel}
\Pr\bigl[f(\alpha) = 0\bigr] \;\le\; \frac{d}{\abs{S}} .
\end{equation}
More generally, if \(f, g \in F[X]\) are distinct, each of degree at
most \(d\), then
\(\Pr\bigl[f(\alpha) = g(\alpha)\bigr] \le d/\abs{S}\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \cref{polynomials:thm:root-bound}, the nonzero \(f\) has at most
\(d\) roots in \(F\), hence at most \(d\) in the subset \(S\). Under
the uniform draw,
\[
\Pr\bigl[f(\alpha) = 0\bigr]
= \frac{\abs{\{\alpha \in S : f(\alpha) = 0\}}}{\abs{S}}
\;\le\; \frac{d}{\abs{S}} .
\]
For the general form, apply this to \(h = f - g\), which is nonzero and
has degree at most \(d\) by \eqref{polynomials:eq:deg-sum}, noting that
\(f(\alpha) = g(\alpha)\) exactly when \(h(\alpha) = 0\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Polynomial identity testing]\label{polynomials:rem:sz-use}
Inequality \eqref{polynomials:eq:schwartz-zippel} is the probabilistic
refinement of \cref{polynomials:cor:agree-implies-equal}: a
\emph{single} random evaluation point detects unequal polynomials of
degree at most \(d\), except with probability at most \(d/\abs{S}\).
With \(\abs{S}\) exponentially large (\(S = \Fq\) with
\(q \approx 2^{254}\), the 255-bit fields underlying Orchard) and \(d\)
polynomially bounded, the failure probability is exponentially small,
hence \emph{negligible}, a term given its precise asymptotic meaning
later in the volume. The bound is the soundness argument for polynomial
identity testing, and hence for the polynomial commitment schemes and
arithmetisation checks of Halo~2, which verify a purported polynomial
identity by evaluating both sides at a random challenge and comparing
the results.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Multivariate generalisation]\label{polynomials:rem:multivariate}
The full Schwartz--Zippel lemma extends to polynomials in several
indeterminates \(f \in F[X_1, \dots, X_n]\): a nonzero polynomial of
total degree \(d\)---the largest sum of exponents
\(i_1 + \dots + i_n\) over the monomials with nonzero
coefficient---vanishes at a uniformly random point of \(S^n\) with
probability at most \(d/\abs{S}\). The univariate case proved here is
the base case of the multivariate inductive proof and the only version
the later volumes invoke directly. Where an identity in several random
challenges arises, for instance in a permutation argument drawing a pair
of challenges \((\beta, \gamma)\), the multivariate bound applies and
follows from the univariate lemma applied once per variable.
\end{remark}

Over a 255-bit field \(\Fq\), distinct polynomials \(f\) and \(g\) of
degree at most \(d\) agree on a uniformly random challenge
\(\alpha \in \Fq\) with probability at most
\(d/q \approx d \cdot 2^{-254}\), astronomically small even with \(d\)
in the millions.

\section{Finite fields}\label{sec:finitefields}

This section studies the finite fields, the fields of
\S\ref{sec:ringsfields} with finitely many elements. A verifier must divide
by every nonzero value, which the arithmetic of a 64-bit processor word,
modulo \(2^{64}\), does not permit: in \(\Z/2^{64}\Z\) no even class has an
inverse (Theorem~\ref{intmod:thm:unitiff}). The section gives two inversion
algorithms for \(\Fp\) and proves that every finite field has \(p^k\)
elements for a prime \(p\) (\cref{finitefields:thm:primepower}), so no field
has \(6\), \(10\), or \(12\) elements. Fields of size \(2^k\) exist for every
positive \(k\), but the field with \(2^{64}\) elements is not
\(\Z/2^{64}\Z\): it is the polynomial quotient of
\cref{finitefields:thm:existence}, with different addition and
multiplication. The section then defines the Frobenius map
\(x \mapsto x^p\) and proves that the multiplicative group \(\Fq^\times\) is
\emph{cyclic}, the powers of a single element running through every nonzero
value (\cref{finitefields:thm:cyclic}).
Cyclicity gives discrete logarithms their meaning and guarantees the roots of
unity on which the polynomial constructions of later sections depend.

\subsection{The prime field and two inversion routes}
\label{finitefields:sub:inversion}

Most constructions of the series are carried out over the field
\(\Fp = \Z/p\Z\) of \cref{ringsfields:cor-fpfield}, for a prime \(p\) of
roughly \(255\) bits. Its addition, subtraction, and multiplication are the
residue arithmetic of \S\ref{sec:intmod}; inversion requires an algorithm,
since a proof that an inverse exists (\S\ref{sec:ringsfields}) does not
compute it.

\begin{construction}[Inversion by the extended Euclidean algorithm]
\label{finitefields:con:inv-euclid}
Given \(a\) with \(0 < a < p\), run the extended Euclidean algorithm
(Theorem~\ref{intmod:thm:exteuclid}) on the pair \((a, p)\). Since \(p\) is
prime and \(p \nmid a\), the gcd is \(1\), and the algorithm outputs
integers \(u, v\) with \(au + pv = 1\). Reducing modulo \(p\) gives
\(au \equiv 1 \pmod{p}\), that is,
\[
a^{-1} = [u]_p \quad\text{in } \Fp .
\]
The cost is \(O(\log p)\) division steps
(Theorem~\ref{intmod:thm:exteuclid}). This is the standard general-purpose
method; a worked instance is Example~\ref{intmod:exa:modinverse}, which
computed \([17]_{43}^{-1} = [38]_{43}\) by exactly this route.
\end{construction}

The second method rests on Fermat's little theorem and on the following
fast exponentiation.

\begin{construction}[Square-and-multiply]\label{finitefields:con:sqmul}
Let \(a\) be an element of any structure with an associative multiplication,
and let \(e \ge 1\) have binary expansion \(e = \sum_i e_i 2^i\) with digits
\(e_i \in \{0,1\}\). Squaring repeatedly produces the values
\[
a^{2^0} = a, \qquad a^{2^{i+1}} = \bigl(a^{2^i}\bigr)^2 ,
\]
and multiplying together the factors indexed by the nonzero digits gives
\[
a^e = \prod_{i \,:\, e_i = 1} a^{2^i} .
\]
The computation uses \(\floor{\log_2 e}\) squarings and at most
\(\floor{\log_2 e}\) further multiplications---\(O(\log e)\) multiplications
in all, against the \(e - 1\) multiplications of the naive product
\(a \cdot a \cdots a\). For the \(255\)-bit exponents of later sections the
former is a few hundred multiplications and the latter a count beyond the
reach of any computer.
\end{construction}

\begin{remark}\label{finitefields:rem:dbladd}
Written additively, the same algorithm computes the multiple \(e \cdot P\)
of a group element \(P\) by repeated doubling and selective adding.
Under the name \emph{double-and-add} it is the algorithm for scalar
multiplication on elliptic curves (\cref{ellipticcurves:def-dbladd}).
\end{remark}

\begin{construction}[Inversion by Fermat's little theorem]
\label{finitefields:con:inv-fermat}
Let \(a \in \Fp\) be nonzero. Fermat's little theorem
(Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-euler-fermat}) gives \(a^{p-1} = 1\), hence
\(a \cdot a^{p-2} = 1\), and so
\[
a^{-1} = a^{p-2},
\]
computed by square-and-multiply (\cref{finitefields:con:sqmul}) with
exponent \(p - 2\), at a cost of \(O(\log p)\) modular multiplications
when \(p>2\). For \(p=2\), the only nonzero element is \(1\), whose
inverse is \(1\); the exponent is zero and no multiplication is needed.
\end{construction}

\begin{remark}[Constant-time inversion]\label{finitefields:rem:cttime}
The two methods have comparable asymptotic cost. In
\cref{finitefields:con:inv-fermat} the sequence of operations
performed---which steps square, which multiply---depends only on the binary
digits of the \emph{public} exponent \(p - 2\), never on the secret input
\(a\); the method is \emph{constant-time-friendly}. The extended Euclidean
method, by contrast, performs a sequence of divisions whose lengths and
quotients depend on \(a\) itself, so its running time and memory-access
pattern can leak information about \(a\) to an observer timing the
computation. Side-channel-resistant implementations therefore often prefer
the Fermat method despite its slightly larger constant factor.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Characteristic, prime subfield, and prime-power order}
\label{finitefields:sub:order}

This subsection determines the possible cardinalities of a finite field
through the characteristic of \cref{ringsfields:subsec-char}, defined by the
canonical homomorphism
\[
\iota\colon \Z \to F, \qquad \iota(n) = n \cdot 1_F ,
\]
whose kernel is an ideal \(n\Z\) of \(\Z\); the characteristic
\(\charf(F)\) is its nonnegative generator
(Definition~\ref{ringsfields:def-char}).

\begin{theorem}\label{finitefields:thm:finchar}
Every finite field \(F\) has prime characteristic: \(\charf(F) = p\) for
some prime \(p\). In particular \(\charf(\Fp) = p\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The map \(\iota\) cannot be injective, since its domain \(\Z\) is infinite
and its codomain \(F\) is finite; two distinct integers \(m < n\) must
satisfy \(\iota(m) = \iota(n)\), and then \(n - m\) is a nonzero element of
\(\ker\iota\). Hence \(\ker\iota = n\Z\) with \(n > 0\), that is,
\(\charf(F) > 0\); and the characteristic of a field, being nonzero, is
prime by Theorem~\ref{ringsfields:thm-char-prime}. For \(F = \Fp\) itself,
Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-char} computed \(\charf(\Z/p\Z) = p\) directly.
\end{proof}

Every field of characteristic \(p\) contains a canonical copy of \(\Fp\)
(Remark~\ref{ringsfields:rem-prime-subfield}), defined as follows.

\begin{definition}[Subfield; prime subfield]
\label{finitefields:def:primesubfield}
A \emph{subfield} of a field \(F\) is a subset \(K \subseteq F\) containing
\(0\) and \(1\) and closed under addition, negation, multiplication, and
inversion of nonzero elements; with the restricted operations, \(K\) is
itself a field. The \emph{prime subfield} of \(F\) is the intersection of
all subfields of \(F\)---equivalently, the smallest subfield of \(F\), and
equivalently again the subfield generated by \(1_F\).
\end{definition}

The intersection of any family of subfields is again a subfield, since each
defining closure property is preserved under intersection; the intersection
of \emph{all} subfields is contained in every subfield, hence is the
smallest one. That this smallest subfield is exactly what \(1_F\)
generates---the closure of \(\{1_F\}\) under the field operations---follows
from the classification below, whose proof exhibits the prime subfield
inside that closure.

\begin{proposition}[Classification of prime subfields]
\label{finitefields:prop:primesub}
Let \(F\) be a field. If \(\charf(F) = p > 0\), the prime subfield of \(F\)
is isomorphic to \(\Fp\). If \(\charf(F) = 0\), it is isomorphic to \(\Q\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Suppose first that \(\charf(F) = p > 0\), so that \(\ker\iota = p\Z\).
Define
\[
\bar\iota\colon \Fp = \Z/p\Z \to F, \qquad \bar\iota([a]_p) = a \cdot 1_F .
\]
A single equivalence shows the map well defined and injective:
\(a \cdot 1_F = b \cdot 1_F\) holds if and only if
\((a-b) \cdot 1_F = 0\), i.e.\ \(a - b \in \ker\iota = p\Z\), i.e.\
\([a]_p = [b]_p\). It is a ring homomorphism because \(\iota\) is one
(\cref{ringsfields:subsec-char}) and \(\bar\iota\) evaluates \(\iota\) on
representatives of residue classes. Its image
\(K = \{\, a \cdot 1_F : a \in \Z \,\}\) is therefore a subring of \(F\)
isomorphic to the field \(\Fp\), and \(K\) is a subfield: for
\([a]_p \neq [0]_p\) the computation
\(\bar\iota([a]_p)\,\bar\iota([a]_p^{-1}) = \bar\iota([1]_p) = 1_F\)
exhibits the inverse of each nonzero element of \(K\) inside \(K\).
Finally, \(K\) lies in every subfield: a subfield contains \(1_F\), hence
all its additive multiples \(a \cdot 1_F\) by closure under addition and
negation. So \(K\) is contained in the intersection of all subfields and
is itself a subfield, so \(K\) equals that intersection: the prime subfield
is \(K \cong \Fp\).

Now suppose \(\charf(F) = 0\), so that \(\iota\) is injective and
\(b \cdot 1_F \neq 0\) for every nonzero integer \(b\). Define
\[
j\colon \Q \to F, \qquad
j\!\left(\frac{a}{b}\right) = (a \cdot 1_F)(b \cdot 1_F)^{-1} .
\]
The map is well defined: if \(a/b = c/d\) with \(b, d \neq 0\), then
\(ad = bc\) in \(\Z\), so \((a \cdot 1_F)(d \cdot 1_F) =
(b \cdot 1_F)(c \cdot 1_F)\) by applying \(\iota\), and multiplying both
sides by \((b \cdot 1_F)^{-1}(d \cdot 1_F)^{-1}\) gives
\((a \cdot 1_F)(b \cdot 1_F)^{-1} = (c \cdot 1_F)(d \cdot 1_F)^{-1}\).
That \(j\) preserves sums, products, and \(1\) is the usual arithmetic of
fractions, transported by the homomorphism \(\iota\) and commutativity.
The map is injective: \(j(a/b) = 0\) forces \(a \cdot 1_F = 0\), hence
\(a = 0\). The image of \(j\) is thus a subfield of \(F\) isomorphic to
\(\Q\); and it lies in every subfield \(K\), since \(K\) contains \(1_F\),
hence every \(a \cdot 1_F\), hence---being closed under inversion and
multiplication---every \((a \cdot 1_F)(b \cdot 1_F)^{-1}\). As before the
image must equal the intersection of all subfields, and the prime subfield
is a copy of \(\Q\).
\end{proof}

The size constraint follows by counting over the prime subfield. Every
field \(F\) of characteristic \(p\) is a vector space over its prime
subfield \(\Fp\): vectors are the elements of \(F\), vector addition is the
field addition, and scalar multiplication \(c \cdot x\) for
\(c \in \Fp \subseteq F\) is the field multiplication, so the vector-space
axioms are instances of the ring axioms of \(F\). The proof uses two facts,
proved in \cref{sec:linalg} independently of the present theorem: a space
with a finite spanning set has a basis, and coordinates with respect to a
basis are unique.

\begin{theorem}[Finite fields have prime-power order]
\label{finitefields:thm:primepower}
Every finite field \(F\) has cardinality \(\abs{F} = p^k\) for some prime
\(p\) and integer \(k \ge 1\), where \(p = \charf(F)\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \cref{finitefields:thm:finchar} the characteristic of \(F\) is a prime
\(p\), and by \cref{finitefields:prop:primesub} the prime subfield of \(F\)
is a copy of \(\Fp\), with which we identify it. As explained above, \(F\)
is then a vector space over \(\Fp\). It is spanned by the finite set \(F\)
itself, so it has a finite basis \(e_1, \dots, e_k\); and \(k \ge 1\)
because \(1 \neq 0\) makes \(F\) nonzero. Every element of \(F\) is thus
\[
c_1 e_1 + c_2 e_2 + \dots + c_k e_k
\]
for a \emph{unique} tuple \((c_1, \dots, c_k)\) with each \(c_i \in \Fp\):
existence because the \(e_i\) span, uniqueness because coordinates with
respect to a basis are unique (\cref{sec:linalg}). The assignment
\((c_1, \dots, c_k) \mapsto \sum_i c_i e_i\) is therefore a bijection from
\(\Fp^{\,k}\) to \(F\), and counting tuples gives
\(\abs{F} = p \cdot p \cdots p = p^k\).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Existence and uniqueness of \(\Fq\)]
\label{finitefields:thm:existence}
For every prime power \(q = p^k\) there exists a field with exactly \(q\)
elements, and any two finite fields of the same cardinality are isomorphic.
One constructs such a field as the quotient \(\Fp[x]/(f)\) of the
polynomial ring by the ideal generated by an irreducible polynomial \(f\)
of degree \(k\)---the construction recorded in
Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-quotient-Zn}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}\label{finitefields:rem:whyunproved}
The fields on the critical path of Halo~2 and Orchard are \emph{prime}
fields, the case \(q = p\) with \(k = 1\), where the theorem is proved:
existence is \cref{ringsfields:cor-fpfield}, and uniqueness holds because a
field with \(p\) elements has order \(p^k = p\) with \(k = 1\) by
\cref{finitefields:thm:primepower}, so it equals its own prime subfield, a
copy of \(\Fp\) (\cref{finitefields:prop:primesub}). The general case
\(k \ge 2\) would require polynomial factorisation over field extensions; no
later result depends on it, so it is stated without proof.
\emph{Convention, fixed henceforth}: the letter \(q = p^k\) denotes a
prime power, and \(\Fq\) a field with \(q\) elements, of characteristic
\(p\); results are stated for general \(\Fq\) whenever the proof is
identical to the prime case, and for the applications to Halo~2 one may
take \(q = p\) throughout.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The Frobenius endomorphism}\label{finitefields:sub:frobenius}

The identity \((a+b)^p = a^p + b^p\), a common error over \(\Q\) and \(\R\),
where it is false for every \(p \ge 2\), is a theorem in characteristic
\(p\). Its proof expands \((a+b)^p\) by the binomial theorem.

\begin{lemma}[Binomial theorem; the coefficients count subsets]
\label{finitefields:lem:binomcount}
Let \(R\) be a commutative ring. For all \(a, b \in R\) and every
integer \(n \ge 0\),
\[
(a+b)^n \;=\; \sum_{k=0}^{n} \binom{n}{k}\, a^k b^{\,n-k} ,
\]
where the integer coefficient \(\binom{n}{k}\) acts as the additive
multiple \(\binom{n}{k} \cdot x = x + \dots + x\), equals the number of
\(k\)-element subsets of an \(n\)-element set, and satisfies
\[
\binom{n}{k} = \frac{n!}{k!\,(n-k)!} .
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Expand the product \((a+b)^n = (a+b) \cdots (a+b)\) by distributivity
without collecting terms: the result is a sum of \(2^n\) products,
one for each way of choosing, from every factor, either its \(a\) or
its \(b\). By commutativity such a product equals \(a^k b^{n-k}\)
exactly when \(a\) was chosen from \(k\) of the \(n\) factors, and it
is determined by the set of those \(k\) factors; so the coefficient of
\(a^k b^{n-k}\) is the number of \(k\)-element subsets of the \(n\)
factors, and collecting the terms gives the displayed sum.

For the formula, count the sequences of \(k\) distinct elements of an
\(n\)-element set in two ways. Choosing the entries in turn, there
are \(n\) choices for the first, \(n - 1\) for the second, and
\(n - k + 1\) for the \(k\)-th: \(n (n-1) \cdots (n-k+1) = n!/(n-k)!\)
sequences. Alternatively, each such sequence is a \(k\)-element
subset together with an ordering of it, and a \(k\)-element set has
\(k!\) orderings --- the same count with \(k\) in place of \(n\) ---
so there are \(k!\) sequences per subset, and the number of subsets
is \(n!/(k!\,(n-k)!)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Freshman's dream]\label{finitefields:lem:freshman}
Let \(R\) be a commutative ring of prime characteristic \(p\). Then for all
\(a, b \in R\),
\[
(a+b)^p = a^p + b^p ,
\]
and more generally \((a+b)^{p^n} = a^{p^n} + b^{p^n}\) for every
\(n \ge 1\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By the binomial theorem (\cref{finitefields:lem:binomcount}),
\[
(a+b)^p \;=\; \sum_{i=0}^{p} \binom{p}{i}\, a^i b^{\,p-i} ,
\]
where the integer coefficient \(\binom{p}{i}\) acts as the additive
multiple \(\binom{p}{i} \cdot x = x + \dots + x\). We claim that
\(p \mid \binom{p}{i}\) for \(0 < i < p\). From the factorial identity
of the same lemma
\[
\binom{p}{i}\; i!\,(p-i)! \;=\; p!
\]
the prime \(p\) divides the right-hand side. It does not divide
\(i!\,(p-i)!\): that product is a product of integers all smaller than
\(p\), and if \(p\) divided the product it would divide one of the factors
by Euclid's lemma for primes (Lemma~\ref{intmod:lem:primedivides}), which
is impossible. Applying Euclid's lemma to the left-hand side instead,
\(p\) must divide \(\binom{p}{i}\), say \(\binom{p}{i} = pm\).

Now let \(0 < i < p\) and consider the corresponding term. Writing
\(\iota(n) = n \cdot 1_R\) for the canonical homomorphism of
\cref{ringsfields:subsec-char}, the coefficient acts as multiplication by
\(\iota\bigl(\tbinom{p}{i}\bigr) = \iota(pm) = \iota(p)\,\iota(m)
= 0 \cdot \iota(m) = 0\), since \(\charf(R) = p\) means exactly
\(\iota(p) = p \cdot 1_R = 0\). Every middle term of the binomial
expansion therefore vanishes, and the terms \(i = p\) and \(i = 0\) leave
\((a+b)^p = a^p + b^p\).

The general case follows by induction on \(n\), the base case \(n = 1\)
being the identity just proved. For \(n \ge 2\), apply the base case to
the elements \(a^{p^{n-1}}\) and \(b^{p^{n-1}}\):
\[
(a+b)^{p^n}
= \bigl((a+b)^{p^{n-1}}\bigr)^{p}
= \bigl(a^{p^{n-1}} + b^{p^{n-1}}\bigr)^{p}
= a^{p^n} + b^{p^n} ,
\]
using the inductive hypothesis in the middle step.
\end{proof}

A homomorphism from a structure to itself is an \emph{endomorphism}. In
characteristic \(p\) the \(p\)-th power map is one: it preserves addition by
the lemma, and multiplication by commutativity.

\begin{definition}[Frobenius endomorphism]\label{finitefields:def:frobenius}
Let \(F\) be a field of characteristic \(p > 0\). The \emph{Frobenius map}
of \(F\) is
\[
\Frob\colon F \to F, \qquad \Frob(x) = x^p .
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{finitefields:prop:frobhom}
Let \(F\) be a field of characteristic \(p\). The Frobenius map is a ring
homomorphism \(F \to F\) fixing the prime subfield pointwise. If \(F\) is
finite, \(\Frob\) is an automorphism of \(F\)---a bijective ring
homomorphism of \(F\) onto itself.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Multiplicativity follows from commutativity and associativity alone:
\((xy)^p = x^p y^p\). Additivity is the freshman's dream
(Lemma~\ref{finitefields:lem:freshman}), and \(1^p = 1\); so \(\Frob\) is
a ring homomorphism.

Injectivity is an instance of a general fact used again later:
\emph{every ring homomorphism between fields is injective}. If
\(\phi\colon F \to F'\) had \(\phi(x) = 0\) for some \(x \neq 0\), then
\[
1 = \phi(1) = \phi(x^{-1} x) = \phi(x^{-1})\,\phi(x)
= \phi(x^{-1}) \cdot 0 = 0
\]
in \(F'\), contradicting \(1 \neq 0\) in a field. In particular \(\Frob\)
is injective.

The prime subfield is fixed pointwise. By
\cref{finitefields:prop:primesub} its elements are the values
\(\bar\iota([a]_p) = a \cdot 1_F\), and since \(\bar\iota\) is a ring
homomorphism,
\[
\Frob\bigl(\bar\iota([a]_p)\bigr)
= \bar\iota([a]_p)^p
= \bar\iota\bigl([a]_p^{\,p}\bigr)
= \bar\iota\bigl([a^p]_p\bigr)
= \bar\iota([a]_p) ,
\]
the last step because \(a^p \equiv a \pmod{p}\) for \emph{all} integers
\(a\) (Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-euler-fermat}).

Finally, let \(F\) be finite. An injective map from a finite set to itself
is surjective---the injectivity-implies-surjectivity argument of
\cref{ringsfields:subsec-fields}---so \(\Frob\) is a bijective ring
homomorphism of \(F\) onto itself, an automorphism.
\end{proof}

The iterates \(\Frob^n(x) = x^{p^n}\) are again homomorphisms, by the
general case of the freshman's dream. On \(\Fp\) the Frobenius map is the
identity; on larger fields of characteristic \(p\) it is not, and
\cref{finitefields:rem:cryptosummary} records its role in cryptography.

\subsection{Cyclicity of the multiplicative group}
\label{finitefields:sub:cyclic}

The multiplicative group \(\Fq^\times\) is a finite abelian group of order
\(q - 1\), since every nonzero element of a field is invertible. Finite
abelian groups need not be cyclic: the unit group \((\Z/8\Z)^\times\) of
Example~\ref{groups:exa-cyclic} has order \(4\) and no element of order
\(4\). The proof that \(\Fq^\times\) is cyclic is a counting argument of
Gauss, resting on an identity for the totient function \(\varphi\) and using
only its \emph{counting definition} (Definition~\ref{intmod:def:totient}):
\(\varphi(n)\) is the number of integers \(a\) with \(1 \le a \le n\) and
\(\gcd(a, n) = 1\).

\begin{lemma}[Gauss's divisor-sum identity]
\label{finitefields:lem:totient-sum}
For every integer \(n \ge 1\),
\[
\sum_{d \mid n} \varphi(d) = n ,
\]
the sum running over the positive divisors \(d\) of \(n\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Partition the set \(\{1, 2, \dots, n\}\) according to the value of
\(\gcd(a, n)\). For each \(a\) the gcd is a positive divisor \(d\) of
\(n\), so the classes
\[
C_d = \{\, a : 1 \le a \le n,\ \gcd(a, n) = d \,\}, \qquad d \mid n ,
\]
are disjoint and cover \(\{1, \dots, n\}\); hence
\(n = \sum_{d \mid n} \abs{C_d}\).

We claim \(\abs{C_d} = \varphi(n/d)\). A member of \(C_d\) is divisible by
\(d\), so it has the form \(a = db\) with \(1 \le b \le n/d\); we show
that, for such \(a\),
\[
\gcd(db, n) = d
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\gcd(b, n/d) = 1 .
\]
For the forward direction, suppose \(e = \gcd(b, n/d) > 1\); then \(de\)
divides both \(db = a\) and \(d \cdot (n/d) = n\), so \(de\) is a common
divisor exceeding \(d\) and \(\gcd(a, n) \ge de > d\). For the converse,
suppose \(\gcd(b, n/d) = 1\). The integer \(d\) is a common divisor of
\(a\) and \(n\), so \(d\) divides \(g = \gcd(a, n)\) by the
strongest-common-divisor property (Remark~\ref{intmod:rem:strongestgcd});
write \(g = dc\) with \(c \ge 1\). From \(dc \mid db\) we get
\(c \mid b\), and from \(dc \mid n = d(n/d)\) we get \(c \mid n/d\)
(cancel \(d\) in each divisibility); so \(c\) divides
\(\gcd(b, n/d) = 1\), forcing \(c = 1\) and \(g = d\).

The members of \(C_d\) are therefore exactly the integers \(db\) with
\(1 \le b \le n/d\) and \(\gcd(b, n/d) = 1\), and by the counting
definition of the totient (Definition~\ref{intmod:def:totient}) there are
precisely \(\varphi(n/d)\) of them. Summing,
\[
n = \sum_{d \mid n} \varphi\!\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)
  = \sum_{d \mid n} \varphi(d) ,
\]
the second equality because \(d \mapsto n/d\) is a bijection of the set of
positive divisors of \(n\) onto itself.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite subgroups of \(F^\times\) are cyclic]
\label{finitefields:thm:cyclic}
Let \(F\) be any field and let \(G \le F^\times\) be a finite subgroup of
the multiplicative group, of order \(N\). Then \(G\) is cyclic. In
particular, for a finite field \(\Fq\) the whole multiplicative group
\(\Fq^\times\) is cyclic of order \(q - 1\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For each divisor \(d \mid N\), let
\[
\psi(d) = \abs{\{\, a \in G : \ord(a) = d \,\}}
\]
count the elements of \(G\) of order exactly \(d\). Every element of the
finite group \(G\) has finite order dividing \(N = \abs{G}\)
(Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-order-elt-divides}), so the sets counted by the
\(\psi(d)\) partition \(G\) and
\begin{equation}\label{finitefields:eq:psisum}
\sum_{d \mid N} \psi(d) = N .
\end{equation}

We claim that \emph{for each} \(d \mid N\), either
\(\psi(d) = 0\) or \(\psi(d) = \varphi(d)\). Suppose \(\psi(d) > 0\) and
choose \(a \in G\) of order \(d\). The powers
\(a^0, a^1, \dots, a^{d-1}\) are \(d\) distinct elements of \(G\)
(Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-order-divides}(3)), and each satisfies
\[
\bigl(a^j\bigr)^d = \bigl(a^d\bigr)^j = 1^j = 1 ,
\]
so each is a root in \(F\) of the polynomial \(X^d - 1 \in F[X]\). That
polynomial has degree \(d\), so it has \emph{at most} \(d\) roots in \(F\)
(Theorem~\ref{polynomials:thm:root-bound}); the \(d\) distinct powers of
\(a\) therefore account for \emph{all} of its roots. Now take any
\(b \in G\) of order \(d\). Then \(b^d = 1\), so \(b\) is a root of
\(X^d - 1\) and hence \(b = a^j\) for some \(0 \le j < d\). By
Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-order-power},
\[
\ord\bigl(a^j\bigr) = \frac{d}{\gcd(d, j)} ,
\]
which equals \(d\) exactly when \(\gcd(j, d) = 1\). The elements of order
\(d\) in \(G\) are thus precisely the powers \(a^j\) with
\(0 \le j < d\) and \(\gcd(j, d) = 1\), and by the counting definition of
the totient there are \(\varphi(d)\) of them: \(\psi(d) = \varphi(d)\).
This proves the claim, and with it the inequality
\(\psi(d) \le \varphi(d)\) for every \(d \mid N\).

Summing the inequality over the divisors of \(N\) and comparing
\eqref{finitefields:eq:psisum} with Gauss's identity
(Lemma~\ref{finitefields:lem:totient-sum}),
\[
N = \sum_{d \mid N} \psi(d)
\;\le\; \sum_{d \mid N} \varphi(d) = N .
\]
The two ends are equal, so the inequality is an equality term by term:
\(\psi(d) = \varphi(d)\) for \emph{every} divisor \(d \mid N\). In
particular
\[
\psi(N) = \varphi(N) \ge 1 ,
\]
since \(a = 1\) always witnesses \(\gcd(1, N) = 1\) in the totient count.
Hence \(G\) contains an element \(g\) of order \(N\); its powers form a
subgroup of \(G\) with \(N\) distinct elements
(Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-order-divides}(3)), which must be all of
\(G\). So \(G = \langle g \rangle\) is cyclic.

For the final statement, let \(F = \Fq\) be finite. The multiplicative
group \(\Fq^\times\) has \(q - 1\) elements---every nonzero element of a
field is a unit---and is a finite subgroup of itself, so it is cyclic of
order \(q - 1\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Element-order census in cyclic groups]
\label{finitefields:cor:ordercensus}
A cyclic group \(G\) of order \(N\) contains, for each divisor
\(d \mid N\), exactly \(\varphi(d)\) elements of order \(d\), and no
elements of any other order. In particular \(G\) has exactly
\(\varphi(N)\) generators.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Write \(G = \langle g \rangle\) with \(\ord(g) = N\); the elements of
\(G\) are the powers \(g^j\), \(0 \le j < N\), each appearing once
(Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-order-divides}(3)). By
Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-order-power},
\(\ord(g^j) = N/\gcd(N, j)\), which is always a divisor of \(N\); and
\(\ord(g^j) = d\) if and only if \(\gcd(j, N) = N/d\). By the gcd
computation in the proof of Lemma~\ref{finitefields:lem:totient-sum}
(applied with \(n = N\) and divisor \(N/d\)), the exponents
\(j \in \{1, \dots, N\}\) with \(\gcd(j, N) = N/d\) are exactly
\(j = (N/d)\,t\) with \(1 \le t \le d\) and \(\gcd(t, d) = 1\), of which
there are \(\varphi(d)\) by Definition~\ref{intmod:def:totient}. The
generators are the elements of order \(N\), numbering \(\varphi(N)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Primitive root]\label{finitefields:def:primitive}
A generator of the cyclic group \(\Fq^\times\) is called a
\emph{primitive root} (or \emph{primitive element}) of \(\Fq\).
Equivalently, an element \(g \in \Fq^\times\) is primitive if and only if
\(\ord(g) = q - 1\).
\end{definition}

Primitive roots exist by \cref{finitefields:thm:cyclic}; by
\cref{finitefields:cor:ordercensus} there are exactly \(\varphi(q-1)\) of
them.

\begin{remark}\label{finitefields:rem:primitive-forward}
The group-theoretic term ``generator of \(\Fq^\times\)'' and the polynomial
term ``primitive \((q-1)\)-th root of unity''
(\cref{rootsofunity:def-primitive}) denote the same elements; the
identification is restated once roots of unity have been defined in general.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Primitive roots of \(\F_7\)]
\label{finitefields:ex:primitiveroot}
The group \(\F_7^\times\) has order \(6\). Testing \(g = 3\), the
successive powers modulo \(7\) are
\[
3^1 = 3, \quad 3^2 = 2, \quad 3^3 = 6, \quad
3^4 = 4, \quad 3^5 = 5, \quad 3^6 = 1 ,
\]
which run through all of \(\{1, \dots, 6\}\): the element \(3\) is a
primitive root of \(\F_7\). By contrast \(2\) is not: its powers are
\(2, 4, 1\), so \(\ord(2) = 3 < 6\). The complete set of primitive roots
consists of the powers \(3^j\) with \(\gcd(j, 6) = 1\)
(Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-order-power}), namely \(j = 1\) and \(j = 5\):
the primitive roots of \(\F_7\) are \(3^1 = 3\) and \(3^5 = 5\), and there
are \(\varphi(6) = 2\) of them, as the census predicts.
\end{example}

Cyclicity also determines the number of solutions of the equation
\(x^n = 1\) in a finite field, on which the evaluation domains of later
sections depend.

\begin{corollary}[Roots of unity in \(\Fq\)]
\label{finitefields:cor:rootsofunity}
Let \(\Fq\) be a finite field and \(n \ge 1\). The solutions of
\(x^n = 1\) in \(\Fq^\times\) number exactly \(\gcd(n, q-1)\), and they
form the cyclic subgroup of \(\Fq^\times\) of that order. In particular,
when \(n \mid q - 1\) the equation has exactly \(n\) solutions.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Fix a primitive root \(g\) (\cref{finitefields:thm:cyclic}) and write each
element of \(\Fq^\times\) uniquely as \(x = g^j\) with
\(0 \le j \le q - 2\) (Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-order-divides}(3)).
Then \(x^n = g^{jn}\), and \(g^{jn} = 1\) holds if and only if
\((q-1) \mid jn\) (Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-order-divides}(1)). Set
\(d = \gcd(n, q-1)\) and write \(q - 1 = dm\), \(n = dn'\) with
\(\gcd(m, n') = 1\). Then
\[
(q-1) \mid jn
\iff dm \mid j\,dn'
\iff m \mid jn'
\iff m \mid j ,
\]
the last step by Euclid's lemma
(Proposition~\ref{intmod:prop:euclidlemma}(1), with
\(\gcd(m, n') = 1\)). The qualifying exponents in \(\{0, \dots, q-2\}\)
are therefore the multiples of \(m = (q-1)/d\), namely \(j = tm\) for
\(t = 0, 1, \dots, d - 1\): exactly \(d\) of them. The corresponding
solutions \(x = (g^m)^t\) are exactly the powers of \(g^m\), which by
Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-order-power} has order
\((q-1)/\gcd(q-1, m) = (q-1)/m = d\); so the solution set is the cyclic
subgroup \(\langle g^m \rangle\) of order \(d = \gcd(n, q-1)\). When
\(n \mid q - 1\) we have \(d = n\), giving exactly \(n\) solutions.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Two-adicity and proof-system fields]
\label{finitefields:rem:twoadicity}
The corollary makes a curve such as Pallas or Vesta suitable for Halo~2:
the field size \(q\) is chosen so that \(q - 1\) is
divisible by a large power of \(2\), and the corollary then
\emph{guarantees} a multiplicative subgroup of \(2\)-power order. That
subgroup is the evaluation domain of the number-theoretic transform, on
which efficient polynomial arithmetic rests. The construction of these
domains and the transform itself are treated in \cref{sec:rootsofunity};
the curves are introduced in \cref{sec:ellipticcurves}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Cyclicity and Frobenius in cryptography]
\label{finitefields:rem:cryptosummary}
The cyclicity of \(\Fq^\times\)
(\cref{finitefields:thm:cyclic}) underlies the discrete-logarithm
problem---every nonzero element has the form \(g^j\), the problem is to
recover \(j\), and the presumed hardness of doing so is a foundational
assumption of later volumes---and, through
\cref{finitefields:cor:rootsofunity}, guarantees the existence of the
roots of unity used in polynomial commitment schemes. The Frobenius
endomorphism (\cref{finitefields:def:frobenius}) induces, on an
elliptic curve over \(\Fp\), an endomorphism of the curve whose
characteristic polynomial \(T^2 - tT + p\) encodes the number of curve
points as \(N = p + 1 - t\), where \(t\) is the Hasse trace; the order of
the cryptographic group is thus determined by the field-theoretic
Frobenius. Both connections are taken up in
\cref{sec:ellipticcurves}.
\end{remark}

\section{Number theory for cryptography}\label{sec:numbertheory}

This section treats the number theory behind two requirements of
elliptic-curve cryptography. A point \((x, y)\) of a curve
\(y^2 = x^3 + ax + b\) (\cref{sec:ellipticcurves}) is determined by
\(x\) up to the sign of \(y\), so wire formats send \(x\) with one bit
selecting the sign, halving the transmission; decompression must decide
whether \(x^3 + ax + b\) is a square modulo \(p\) and, if it is,
\emph{compute} a square root, in the time of a few modular
exponentiations on numbers hundreds of bits long. Elliptic-curve
cryptography also publishes powers \(g^x\) of a known group element
while the exponent \(x\) is a secret key, so exponentiation must be
efficient and its inversion infeasible. The section proves the Chinese
Remainder Theorem and Euler's criterion, gives square-root algorithms
for \(p \equiv 3 \pmod 4\)
(Proposition~\ref{numbertheory:prop:sqrt-3mod4}) and in general
(Theorem~\ref{numbertheory:thm:tonelli-shanks}), and proves the
Pohlig--Hellman reduction of a discrete logarithm to discrete logarithms
in subgroups of prime order, whence the prime group orders of Halo~2 and
Orchard (Corollary~\ref{numbertheory:cor:prime-order}).

Throughout, \(n\) and \(m\) denote positive integers and \(p\) denotes a
prime. Statements proved in \cref{sec:intmod}, \cref{sec:groups} and
\cref{sec:finitefields} (the cyclicity theorem) reappear in arithmetic
form, with proofs by citation.

\subsection{Euler's totient function and the Chinese Remainder Theorem}
\label{numbertheory:sub:totient}

A class \([a]_n \in \Z/n\Z\) is a unit if and only if \(\gcd(a, n) = 1\)
(Theorem~\ref{intmod:thm:unitiff}), the units form a finite abelian
group \((\Z/n\Z)^\times\) (Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-unitgroup}), and
Euler's totient \(\varphi(n)\) counts them:
\(\varphi(n) = \abs{(\Z/n\Z)^\times}\)
(Definition~\ref{intmod:def:totient}). In particular
\(\varphi(p) = p - 1\) for a prime \(p\)
(Example~\ref{intmod:exa:totient}), so \(\Fp^\times\) has order
\(p - 1\). Through the orders of elliptic-curve groups, the totient
enters the parameter choices of Halo~2 and Orchard. Since
\cref{sec:intmod} computes \(\varphi(n)\) only by enumeration, this
subsection derives a formula from its values on prime powers and its
multiplicativity on coprime factors.

\begin{proposition}[Totient of a prime power]
\label{numbertheory:prop:totient-prime-power}
For a prime \(p\) and an integer \(k \ge 1\),
\[
\varphi(p^k) = p^k - p^{k-1} = p^k\Bigl(1 - \frac{1}{p}\Bigr).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Among \(1, \dots, p^k\), we claim the integers \emph{not} coprime to
\(p^k\) are exactly the multiples of \(p\). If \(p \mid a\) then
\(p \mid \gcd(a, p^k)\), so the gcd exceeds \(1\). Conversely, if
\(g = \gcd(a, p^k) > 1\), then \(g\) has a prime factor \(\ell\); from
\(\ell \mid g \mid p^k\) and Euclid's lemma for primes
(Lemma~\ref{intmod:lem:primedivides}) we get \(\ell = p\), and
\(\ell \mid g \mid a\) gives \(p \mid a\). The multiples of \(p\) in the
range are \(p, 2p, \dots, p^{k-1} \cdot p\), of which there are
\(p^{k-1}\); subtracting them from the \(p^k\) integers in the range
leaves \(\varphi(p^k) = p^k - p^{k-1}\).
\end{proof}

Multiplicativity follows from the Chinese Remainder Theorem, which the
discrete-logarithm subsection uses again. The theorem is stated in the
language of rings (\cref{sec:ringsfields}), for the product ring
\(R \times S\) with componentwise operations
(Example~\ref{ringsfields:exa-rings}); a bijective ring homomorphism
(Definition~\ref{ringsfields:def-ringhom}) is a \emph{ring isomorphism},
as for groups (Definition~\ref{groups:def-iso}).

\begin{theorem}[Chinese Remainder Theorem]\label{numbertheory:thm:crt}
If \(\gcd(m, n) = 1\), then the map
\[
\Z/mn\Z \longrightarrow \Z/m\Z \times \Z/n\Z,
\qquad
[a]_{mn} \longmapsto \bigl([a]_m,\, [a]_n\bigr)
\]
is a ring isomorphism. It restricts to a group isomorphism
\[
(\Z/mn\Z)^\times \;\cong\; (\Z/m\Z)^\times \times (\Z/n\Z)^\times .
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The map is well defined: if \([a]_{mn} = [a']_{mn}\) then
\(mn \mid a - a'\), hence \(m \mid a - a'\) and \(n \mid a - a'\), so the
two components agree. It is a ring homomorphism because each component
is: reduction modulo \(m\) (and modulo \(n\)) sends sums to sums,
products to products, and \(1\) to \(1\)
(Theorem~\ref{ringsfields:thm-znring}), and the product ring's
operations are componentwise.

For injectivity it suffices, since the map is in particular a
homomorphism of additive groups, to check that the kernel is trivial
(Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-kernel-injective}). Suppose
\([a]_m = [0]_m\) and \([a]_n = [0]_n\), that is, \(m \mid a\) and
\(n \mid a\). Since \(\gcd(m, n) = 1\), Euclid's lemma
(Proposition~\ref{intmod:prop:euclidlemma}(2)) gives \(mn \mid a\), i.e.\
\([a]_{mn} = [0]_{mn}\).

Both sides have exactly \(mn\) elements
(Theorem~\ref{ringsfields:thm-znring} for each factor), and an injective
map between finite sets of equal size is bijective: the \(mn\) distinct
inputs have \(mn\) distinct images, which must exhaust the codomain.
Hence the map is a ring isomorphism.

For the multiplicative statement, a ring isomorphism \(\phi\) matches
units with units: if \(uv = 1\) then \(\phi(u)\phi(v) = \phi(1) = 1\),
and the same applies to \(\phi^{-1}\). The units of the product ring are
exactly the pairs of units, \((u, v)^{-1} = (u^{-1}, v^{-1})\) existing
precisely when both components are invertible. Restricting \(\phi\) to
unit groups therefore gives a bijection
\((\Z/mn\Z)^\times \to (\Z/m\Z)^\times \times (\Z/n\Z)^\times\) that
respects multiplication: a group isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Multiplicativity and the product formula]
\label{numbertheory:cor:totient-mult}
If \(\gcd(m, n) = 1\) then \(\varphi(mn) = \varphi(m)\,\varphi(n)\).
Hence if \(n = \prod_i p_i^{k_i}\) is the prime factorisation of \(n\),
then
\[
\varphi(n)
= \prod_i \bigl(p_i^{k_i} - p_i^{k_i - 1}\bigr)
= n \prod_{p \mid n} \Bigl(1 - \frac{1}{p}\Bigr),
\]
the last product running over the distinct primes dividing \(n\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The unit-group isomorphism of Theorem~\ref{numbertheory:thm:crt} is in
particular a bijection, and the cardinality of a product of two sets is
the product of the cardinalities, so
\(\varphi(mn) = \varphi(m)\,\varphi(n)\).

For the product formula, the prime powers \(p_i^{k_i}\) are pairwise
coprime: a common divisor exceeding \(1\) of two of them would have a
prime factor dividing both, which by Euclid's lemma for primes
(Lemma~\ref{intmod:lem:primedivides}) would equal both \(p_i\) and
\(p_j\). Moreover each \(p_i^{k_i}\) is coprime to the product of the
others, by repeated application of
Proposition~\ref{intmod:prop:euclidlemma}(3). Induction on the number of
factors therefore extends multiplicativity to
\(\varphi(n) = \prod_i \varphi\bigl(p_i^{k_i}\bigr)\), and
Proposition~\ref{numbertheory:prop:totient-prime-power} evaluates each
factor:
\[
\varphi(n)
= \prod_i \bigl(p_i^{k_i} - p_i^{k_i-1}\bigr)
= \prod_i p_i^{k_i}\Bigl(1 - \frac{1}{p_i}\Bigr)
= n \prod_{p \mid n}\Bigl(1 - \frac{1}{p}\Bigr). \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

For example, the product formula gives
\(\varphi(12) = 12\bigl(1 - \tfrac12\bigr)\bigl(1 - \tfrac13\bigr) = 4\),
in agreement with the direct enumeration \(\{1, 5, 7, 11\}\) of
Example~\ref{intmod:exa:totient}. Gauss's divisor-sum identity
\(\sum_{d \mid n} \varphi(d) = n\) is
Lemma~\ref{finitefields:lem:totient-sum}.

\subsection{Fermat's little theorem and Euler's theorem}
\label{numbertheory:sub:fermat-euler}

Both congruences are Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-euler-fermat}, proved
from Lagrange's theorem.

\begin{theorem}[Euler's theorem]\label{numbertheory:thm:euler}
If \(\gcd(a, n) = 1\), then
\[
a^{\varphi(n)} \equiv 1 \pmod{n}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The hypothesis says that \([a]_n\) is a unit
(Theorem~\ref{intmod:thm:unitiff}), i.e.\ an element of the finite group
\((\Z/n\Z)^\times\), whose order is \(\varphi(n)\)
(Definition~\ref{intmod:def:totient}). By
Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-aG-e}---every element \(g\) of a finite group
of order \(N\) satisfies \(g^N = e\), a consequence of Lagrange's
theorem---we get \([a]_n^{\varphi(n)} = [1]_n\), which is the stated
congruence. This is Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-euler-fermat} verbatim.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Fermat's little theorem]\label{numbertheory:cor:fermat}
Let \(p\) be prime. If \(p \nmid a\), then
\(a^{p-1} \equiv 1 \pmod{p}\). Moreover
\(a^{p} \equiv a \pmod{p}\) for \emph{every} integer \(a\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For \(p \nmid a\) we have \(\gcd(a, p) = 1\), so
Theorem~\ref{numbertheory:thm:euler} with \(n = p\) and
\(\varphi(p) = p - 1\) (Example~\ref{intmod:exa:totient}) gives the first
congruence; multiplying it by \(a\) gives \(a^p \equiv a \pmod p\). When
\(p \mid a\) both \(a^p\) and \(a\) are congruent to \(0\), so the second
congruence holds for all \(a\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Cryptographic use of Euler and Fermat]
\label{numbertheory:rem:fermat-rsa}
Euler's theorem underlies RSA: if \(ed \equiv 1 \pmod{\varphi(n)}\),
say \(ed = 1 + k\varphi(n)\), then
\[
(a^e)^d = a^{1 + k\varphi(n)}
= a \cdot \bigl(a^{\varphi(n)}\bigr)^{k} \equiv a \pmod{n}
\]
whenever \(\gcd(a, n) = 1\), so \(e\) can serve as a public encryption
exponent and \(d\) as its private inverse. Orchard's elliptic-curve
cryptography instead uses Fermat's little theorem in the field \(\Fp\):
every nonzero \(x\) satisfies \(x^{p-1} = 1\), so inversion is computable
as \(x^{-1} = x^{p-2}\) (\cref{finitefields:con:inv-fermat}).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Multiplicative order and primitive roots}
\label{numbertheory:sub:order}

\begin{definition}[Multiplicative order]\label{numbertheory:def:ord}
Let \(\gcd(a, n) = 1\). The \emph{multiplicative order} of \(a\) modulo
\(n\), written \(\ord_n(a)\), is the least positive integer \(k\) with
\(a^k \equiv 1 \pmod{n}\). Such a \(k\) exists because
\(a^{\varphi(n)} \equiv 1\) by Theorem~\ref{numbertheory:thm:euler}.
\end{definition}

The quantity \(\ord_n(a)\) is exactly the order of \([a]_n\) as an
element of the group \((\Z/n\Z)^\times\), so the order theory of
\cref{sec:groups} applies verbatim: \(a^m \equiv 1 \pmod{n}\) if and
only if \(\ord_n(a) \mid m\)
(Proposition~\ref{groups:prop-order-divides}(1))---in particular
\(\ord_n(a) \mid \varphi(n)\), by Euler's theorem---and
\[
\ord_n\bigl(a^j\bigr) = \frac{\ord_n(a)}{\gcd\bigl(j, \ord_n(a)\bigr)}
\]
(Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-order-power}).

\begin{definition}[Primitive root]\label{numbertheory:def:primitive-root}
A \emph{primitive root} modulo \(n\) is an element \(g\) with
\(\ord_n(g) = \varphi(n)\); equivalently, \(g\) generates
\((\Z/n\Z)^\times\) as a cyclic group, so that
\(\{g^0, g^1, \dots, g^{\varphi(n)-1}\}\) lists all the units.
\end{definition}

Primitive roots need not exist for every modulus. The unit group
\((\Z/8\Z)^\times = \{1, 3, 5, 7\}\) has order \(\varphi(8) = 4\), yet
every one of its elements squares to \(1\)
(Example~\ref{groups:exa-cyclic}): the element orders are \(1\) for the
identity and \(2\) for each of \(3\), \(5\), \(7\), so no element has
order \(4\) and no primitive root modulo \(8\) exists. The unit group is
isomorphic to the Klein four-group of
Example~\ref{groups:exa-trivial}: every element is its own inverse, and
the product of two distinct non-identity elements \(a\) and \(b\) is
neither \(e\) (which would force \(b = a^{-1} = a\)) nor \(a\) nor \(b\)
(which would force \(b = e\) or \(a = e\)), so it is the third. The case
that cryptography requires is \(n = p\) prime, where the multiplicative
group is always cyclic.

\begin{corollary}[\(\Fp^\times\) is cyclic]\label{numbertheory:cor:fp-cyclic}
For every prime \(p\), the group \(\Fp^\times = (\Z/p\Z)^\times\) is
cyclic of order \(p - 1\). In particular primitive roots modulo \(p\)
exist, and there are exactly \(\varphi(p-1)\) of them;
Example~\ref{finitefields:ex:primitiveroot} exhibits the two primitive
roots \(3\) and \(5\) of \(\F_7\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The group \(\Fp^\times\) is a finite subgroup, of order \(p - 1\), of
the multiplicative group of the field \(\Fp\), hence cyclic by
Theorem~\ref{finitefields:thm:cyclic}. A generator \(g\) has order
\(p - 1\), and the powers \(g^k\) that again generate are those with
\(\gcd(k, p-1) = 1\) (Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-order-power}), numbering
\(\varphi(p-1)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Cyclicity and prime-order groups]
\label{numbertheory:rem:cyclic-subgroup}
Cyclicity is the reason the prime-order groups of elliptic-curve
cryptography behave exactly like \(\Z/q\Z\) under addition once a
generator is fixed: a cyclic group of order \(q\) is isomorphic to
\((\Z/q\Z, +)\) via \(g^k \leftrightarrow k\)
(Theorem~\ref{groups:thm-cyclic-classification}). The full
classification, stated without proof and not used later, is that
primitive roots modulo \(n\) exist exactly when
\(n \in \{1, 2, 4, p^k, 2p^k\}\) for an odd prime \(p\).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Quadratic residues and the Euler criterion}
\label{numbertheory:sub:qr}

The squares modulo a prime decide whether a curve point with a given
\(x\)-coordinate exists, the first decompression task.

\begin{definition}[Quadratic residue]\label{numbertheory:def:qr}
Let \(p\) be an odd prime and \(a\) an integer with \(p \nmid a\). We
say \(a\) is a \emph{quadratic residue} modulo \(p\) (a QR) if the
congruence \(x^2 \equiv a \pmod{p}\) has a solution, and a
\emph{quadratic non-residue} (a QNR) otherwise.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Exactly half are residues]
\label{numbertheory:prop:half-qr}
For an odd prime \(p\), exactly \(\tfrac{p-1}{2}\) of the nonzero
residues modulo \(p\) are quadratic residues and \(\tfrac{p-1}{2}\) are
non-residues. The squaring map \(x \mapsto x^2\) is exactly two-to-one
on \(\Fp^\times\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Consider \(\sigma \colon \Fp^\times \to \Fp^\times\),
\(\sigma(x) = x^2\); its image is exactly the set of quadratic residues.
For \(x, y \in \Fp^\times\),
\[
\sigma(x) = \sigma(y)
\iff x^2 - y^2 = 0
\iff (x - y)(x + y) = 0
\iff y = \pm x,
\]
the middle equivalence because a field has no zero divisors
(Proposition~\ref{ringsfields:prop-field-domain}). Since \(p\) is odd,
\(x \neq -x\) for \(x \neq 0\) (otherwise \(2x = 0\) with \(2\) and
\(x\) nonzero), so every fibre of \(\sigma\) over its image has exactly
two elements. Counting, \(\abs{\img \sigma} = \tfrac{p-1}{2}\), and the
complement---the non-residues---has the same size.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Euler's criterion]\label{numbertheory:thm:euler-criterion}
Let \(p\) be an odd prime and \(p \nmid a\). Then
\[
a^{\frac{p-1}{2}} \equiv
\begin{cases}
\phantom{-}1 \pmod{p} & \text{if } a \text{ is a quadratic residue,}\\[2pt]
-1 \pmod{p} & \text{if } a \text{ is a quadratic non-residue.}
\end{cases}
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First, the value is always \(\pm 1\). Fermat's little theorem
(Corollary~\ref{numbertheory:cor:fermat}) gives
\(\bigl(a^{(p-1)/2}\bigr)^2 = a^{p-1} \equiv 1\), and the only square
roots of \(1\) modulo a prime are \(\pm 1\): from
\(x^2 \equiv 1\) we get \(p \mid (x-1)(x+1)\), so
\(p \mid x - 1\) or \(p \mid x + 1\) by Euclid's lemma for primes
(Lemma~\ref{intmod:lem:primedivides}), i.e.\ \(x \equiv \pm 1\). Hence
\(a^{(p-1)/2} \equiv \pm 1 \pmod{p}\).

If \(a\) is a residue, say \(a \equiv x^2\) with \(p \nmid x\), then
\(a^{(p-1)/2} \equiv x^{p-1} \equiv 1\), again by Fermat. Thus every one
of the \(\tfrac{p-1}{2}\) quadratic residues
(Proposition~\ref{numbertheory:prop:half-qr}) is a root of the
polynomial \(t^{(p-1)/2} - 1\) over \(\Fp\). That polynomial has degree
\(\tfrac{p-1}{2}\), so it has \emph{at most} \(\tfrac{p-1}{2}\) roots
(Theorem~\ref{polynomials:thm:root-bound}); the residues therefore
account for all of them. A non-residue \(a\) is consequently not a root,
yet still satisfies \(a^{(p-1)/2} \equiv \pm 1\); the remaining
possibility is \(a^{(p-1)/2} \equiv -1\).
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Legendre symbol]\label{numbertheory:def:legendre}
For an odd prime \(p\) and an integer \(a\), the \emph{Legendre symbol}
is
\[
\legendre{a}{p} =
\begin{cases}
\phantom{-}0 & \text{if } p \mid a,\\
\phantom{-}1 & \text{if } a \text{ is a quadratic residue modulo } p,\\
-1 & \text{if } a \text{ is a quadratic non-residue modulo } p.
\end{cases}
\]
\end{definition}

Euler's criterion (Theorem~\ref{numbertheory:thm:euler-criterion}) then
reads, uniformly,
\[
\legendre{a}{p} \equiv a^{\frac{p-1}{2}} \pmod{p},
\]
so the symbol is computable by a single modular exponentiation, at a cost
of \(O(\log p)\) modular multiplications by square-and-multiply
(\cref{finitefields:con:sqmul}). The criterion also shows that the
symbol is \emph{completely multiplicative} in \(a\):
\[
\legendre{ab}{p} = \legendre{a}{p}\legendre{b}{p},
\qquad\text{since}\qquad
(ab)^{\frac{p-1}{2}} = a^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\, b^{\frac{p-1}{2}} .
\]

\begin{example}[\(13\) is a non-square in both Pasta fields]
\label{numbertheory:ex:pasta-nonsquare}
Orchard uses the Pasta hash-to-curve construction, a procedure, treated
in later volumes, that maps arbitrary byte strings to curve points and
requires a fixed non-square constant in the base field. The construction
fixes \(Z = -13\) as a verifiably non-square constant in both base
fields. Let \(p\) and \(q\) be the Pasta primes of
\cref{sec:ellipticcurves}; both satisfy
\(p \equiv q \equiv 1 \pmod{4}\). The verification below works modulo
\(p\); the computation modulo \(q\) is identical.

First, \(-1\) is a square modulo any prime \(\equiv 1 \pmod 4\): the
exponent \(\tfrac{p-1}{2}\) is even, so Euler's criterion gives
\[
\legendre{-1}{p} \equiv (-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}} = 1 .
\]
By complete multiplicativity,
\[
\legendre{-13}{p} = \legendre{-1}{p}\legendre{13}{p}
= \legendre{13}{p},
\]
so \(-13\) is a non-square precisely when \(13\) is. Whether \(13\) is a
square is settled by one Euler-criterion exponentiation in each field, a
computation any computer-algebra system reproduces in milliseconds:
\[
13^{\frac{p-1}{2}} \equiv p - 1 \equiv -1 \pmod{p},
\qquad
13^{\frac{q-1}{2}} \equiv q - 1 \equiv -1 \pmod{q},
\]
whence \(\legendre{13}{p} = \legendre{13}{q} = -1\). Thus \(13\), and
with it \(Z = -13\), is a quadratic non-residue modulo both Pasta
primes, as the hash-to-curve construction requires.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[\(5\) is a non-square in the Pallas base field]
\label{numbertheory:ex:pasta-five}
% Source: .wip/rewrite/math-guide/compute/pasta_five_nonsquare.py
%   Pallas p: 5^((p-1)/2) = p - 1 = -1, so 5 is a non-square in F_p
%   Vesta q: 5^((q-1)/2) = q - 1 = -1, so 5 is a non-square in F_q
%   -5 is a non-cube in F_p: no Pallas point has y = 0
The same test decides a second constant on which later volumes rely,
in this case the constant term of the curve equation. Pallas is the curve
\(y^2 = x^3 + 5\) over \(\Fp\) (\cref{sec:ellipticcurves}), and the
arithmetic circuits of Halo~2 encode its point at infinity as the
coordinate pair \((0, 0)\) (\cref{ellipticcurves:con-complete}). The
encoding is unambiguous only if no genuine point has \(x\)-coordinate
\(0\), i.e.\ only if \(y^2 = 5\) has no solution in \(\Fp\): the question
is whether \(5\) is a square in the \emph{base} field \(\Fp\) of Pallas,
not in its scalar field \(\Fq\). Euler's criterion decides it in one
exponentiation:
\[
5^{\frac{p-1}{2}} \equiv p - 1 \equiv -1 \pmod{p},
\]
so \(\legendre{5}{p} = -1\). The constant \(5\) is a quadratic
non-residue modulo the Pallas base-field prime, and no point of Pallas
has \(x = 0\). (The same computation modulo \(q\) gives
\(5^{(q-1)/2} \equiv -1 \pmod{q}\), so Vesta has no such point either.)
That no point has \(y = 0\) is a different fact---\(-5\) is not a cube
in \(\Fp\)---which \cref{ellipticcurves:rem-pasta-odd} reads off from
the oddness of the group order; the two together are the hypotheses of
\cref{ellipticcurves:prop-complete}.
\end{example}

\subsection{Square roots modulo a prime}\label{numbertheory:sub:sqrt}

Decompression must also \emph{produce} an \(x\) with \(x^2 \equiv a\)
for a residue \(a\), recovering \(y\) from \(y^2\); the cost depends on
\(p \bmod 4\).

\begin{proposition}[The \(p \equiv 3 \pmod 4\) shortcut]
\label{numbertheory:prop:sqrt-3mod4}
Let \(p \equiv 3 \pmod 4\) be prime and let \(a\) be a quadratic residue
modulo \(p\) with \(p \nmid a\). Then
\[
x \equiv a^{\frac{p+1}{4}} \pmod{p}
\]
is a square root of \(a\), and the two square roots of \(a\) are
\(\pm x\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(p \equiv 3 \pmod 4\), the quantity \(\tfrac{p+1}{4}\) is a
positive integer. Compute
\[
x^2 = a^{\frac{p+1}{2}} = a^{\frac{p-1}{2}} \cdot a
\equiv \legendre{a}{p} \cdot a = a \pmod{p},
\]
using Euler's criterion
(Theorem~\ref{numbertheory:thm:euler-criterion}) and the hypothesis that
\(a\) is a residue, so \(\legendre{a}{p} = 1\). The roots come in the
pair \(\pm x\) by the two-to-one property of squaring
(Proposition~\ref{numbertheory:prop:half-qr}).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Cost and the Pasta primes]\label{numbertheory:rem:pasta-3mod4}
Primes \(p \equiv 3 \pmod 4\) are often favoured for this
single-exponentiation shortcut. For \(p \equiv 5 \pmod 8\) a closed-form
variant (Atkin's formula) exists. In general the cost of square-root
extraction grows with the \(2\)-adic valuation \(s\) of \(p - 1\):
writing \(p - 1 = 2^s d\) with \(d\) odd, the Tonelli--Shanks algorithm
below performs up to \(s\) corrective iterations. The Pasta primes have
\(s = 32\), chosen for FFT-friendliness (\cref{rootsofunity:rem-twoadic}),
so their implementations use Tonelli--Shanks.
\end{remark}

For \(p \equiv 1 \pmod 4\), write \(p - 1 = 2^s d\) with \(d\) odd and
\(s \ge 2\). The algorithm works in the subgroup of \(\Fp^\times\) of
order \(2^s\), which contains the obstruction to the
single-exponentiation shortcut, and removes that obstruction one power
of two at a time. The subgroup is unique since \(\Fp^\times\) is cyclic
(Corollary~\ref{numbertheory:cor:fp-cyclic} and
Theorem~\ref{groups:thm-cyclic-subgroups}(3)).

\begin{theorem}[Tonelli--Shanks]\label{numbertheory:thm:tonelli-shanks}
Let \(p\) be an odd prime, \(p - 1 = 2^s d\) with \(d\) odd, and let
\(a\) be a quadratic residue with \(p \nmid a\). Given any quadratic
non-residue \(z\) modulo \(p\), one can compute a square root of \(a\)
modulo \(p\) using \(O(s^2 + \log p)\) modular multiplications.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write \(H \le \Fp^\times\) for the unique cyclic subgroup of order
\(2^s\). Set \(c = z^d\). We claim \(c\) generates \(H\). By Euler's
criterion (Theorem~\ref{numbertheory:thm:euler-criterion})
\(z^{(p-1)/2} \equiv -1\), so
\[
c^{\,2^{s-1}} = z^{\,2^{s-1} d} = z^{\frac{p-1}{2}} \equiv -1 \neq 1,
\qquad
c^{\,2^{s}} = z^{\,p-1} \equiv 1
\]
by Fermat (Corollary~\ref{numbertheory:cor:fermat}); hence
\(\ord_p(c)\) divides \(2^s\) but not \(2^{s-1}\), forcing
\(\ord_p(c) = 2^s\) and \(\langle c \rangle = H\).

Initialise
\[
x = a^{\frac{d+1}{2}}, \qquad t = a^{d}, \qquad m = s
\]
(the exponent \(\tfrac{d+1}{2}\) is an integer because \(d\) is odd).
We maintain three invariants:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(x^2 = a\,t\);
\item the current \(c\) has order exactly \(2^m\);
\item the order of \(t\) divides \(2^{m-1}\).
\end{enumerate}
Initially (1) holds since \(x^2 = a^{d+1} = a \cdot a^d\); (2) was
proved above; and (3) holds because
\(t^{\,2^{s-1}} = a^{(p-1)/2} \equiv 1\) by Euler's criterion
(Theorem~\ref{numbertheory:thm:euler-criterion}), \(a\) being a
residue.

Each iteration proceeds as follows. If \(t = 1\), then \(x^2 = a\) by
(1) and the algorithm terminates. Otherwise let \(2^i = \ord_p(t)\),
found as the least \(i \ge 1\) with \(t^{\,2^i} = 1\) by repeatedly
squaring \(t\); invariant (3) gives
\(1 \le i \le m - 1\). Set
\[
b = c^{\,2^{m-i-1}},
\]
and update
\[
x \leftarrow x b, \qquad t \leftarrow t b^2, \qquad c \leftarrow b^2,
\qquad m \leftarrow i .
\]
The invariants are preserved. For (1),
\((xb)^2 = x^2 b^2 = a\,t\,b^2\), which is \(a\) times the new \(t\).
For (2), the old \(c\) has order \(2^m\), so
\(\ord_p(b) = 2^m/\gcd\bigl(2^m, 2^{m-i-1}\bigr) = 2^{i+1}\)
(Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-order-power}), and the new \(c = b^2\) has
order \(2^i\)---exactly \(2\) to the new \(m\). For (3), both the old
\(t\) and \(b^2\) have order exactly \(2^i\), so \(t^{\,2^{i-1}}\) and
\((b^2)^{\,2^{i-1}}\) each have order \(2\); the only element of order
\(2\) in \(\Fp^\times\) is \(-1\), because the square roots of \(1\)
are \(\pm 1\) (as in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{numbertheory:thm:euler-criterion}); hence
\[
\bigl(t\,b^2\bigr)^{2^{i-1}}
= t^{\,2^{i-1}} \bigl(b^2\bigr)^{2^{i-1}}
= (-1)(-1) = 1,
\]
so the new \(t\) has order dividing \(2^{i-1}\), which is \(2^{m-1}\)
for the new \(m\).

Each iteration strictly decreases \(m\), from \(s\) through a
descending chain of nonnegative integers, so after at most \(s\)
iterations the order of \(t\) reaches \(2^0 = 1\), i.e.\ \(t = 1\), and
\(x\) is the desired root. For the cost: locating \(i\) and computing
\(b\) each take at most \(m \le s\) squarings, so the loop costs
\(O(s)\) multiplications per iteration and \(O(s^2)\) in total; the
three initial exponentiations, with exponents smaller than \(p\), cost
\(O(\log p)\) multiplications each by square-and-multiply
(\cref{finitefields:con:sqmul}).
\end{proof}

Finding the non-residue \(z\) is the only randomised step in this
section. A uniformly random element of \(\Fp^\times\) is a non-residue
with probability exactly \(\tfrac12\)
(Proposition~\ref{numbertheory:prop:half-qr}), and each candidate is
checked by one Euler-criterion exponentiation
(Definition~\ref{numbertheory:def:legendre}), i.e.\ \(O(\log p)\)
modular multiplications, so a few random trials find \(z\) with high
probability. Implementations hard-code one non-residue per field,
verified once, as Example~\ref{numbertheory:ex:pasta-nonsquare} verifies
\(Z = -13\). This volume uses no deterministic polynomial-time method
for finding a non-residue.

\begin{remark}[Which root, and detecting failure]
\label{numbertheory:rem:sqrt-uniqueness}
The square roots of a residue come in a pair \(\pm x\)
(Proposition~\ref{numbertheory:prop:half-qr}), so a \emph{canonical}
root is fixed by convention---for instance ``choose the
root whose least nonnegative representative is even'', as
elliptic-curve point encodings do; the compressed point's extra bit
selects between the two. Both algorithms detect a non-residue \(a\).
The shortcut of Proposition~\ref{numbertheory:prop:sqrt-3mod4} still
outputs a candidate \(a^{(p+1)/4}\), which fails the check
\(x^2 \equiv a\) at the cost of one squaring. The variable-time
Tonelli--Shanks algorithm cannot complete its update: Euler's
criterion gives \(a^{(p-1)/2} \equiv -1\), so \(t = a^d\) satisfies
\(t^{2^{s-1}} \equiv -1\) and has order exactly \(2^s\), and the first
search for the least \(i \ge 1\) with \(t^{2^i} = 1\) already
returns \(i = m\), which invariant (3) of the proof of
Theorem~\ref{numbertheory:thm:tonelli-shanks} forbids and for which
the update exponent \(2^{m-i-1}\) is undefined. The algorithm can
therefore report ``not a residue'' as soon as \(i = m\) occurs, or
verify \(a^{(p-1)/2} \equiv 1\) up front, at the same \(O(\log p)\) cost
as the initial exponentiations.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The discrete logarithm problem}\label{numbertheory:sub:dlog}

The statements below concern a cyclic group written multiplicatively.
In Orchard the group is the group of points of an elliptic curve, of
large prime order and written additively; every statement transfers by
renaming the operation.

\begin{definition}[Discrete logarithm problem]\label{numbertheory:def:dlp}
Let \(G = \langle g \rangle\) be a finite cyclic group of order \(N\)
with generator \(g\). Given \(h \in G\), the \emph{discrete logarithm}
of \(h\) to base \(g\), written \(\log_g h\), is the unique residue
\(x \in \Z/N\Z\) with \(g^x = h\). The \emph{discrete logarithm
problem} (DLP) is to compute \(x\) from \((G, g, h)\).
\end{definition}

Existence and uniqueness of \(\log_g h\) are the content of the
classification of cyclic groups: the map \(x \mapsto g^x\) is a group
isomorphism \((\Z/N\Z, +) \xrightarrow{\;\sim\;} G\)
(Theorem~\ref{groups:thm-cyclic-classification}, whose map \(\bar\phi\)
this is), so it has a well-defined inverse, and \(\log_g\) is that
inverse. Computing \(g^x\) from \(x\) takes \(O(\log N)\) group
operations by square-and-multiply (\cref{finitefields:con:sqmul});
computing \(x\) from \(g^x\) is the DLP, for which no efficient
algorithm is known in well-chosen groups. This asymmetry is the
one-wayness on which elliptic-curve cryptography rests.

\begin{remark}[Generic complexity and group choice]
\label{numbertheory:rem:dlp-generic}
Two \emph{generic} algorithms solve the DLP in any group of order
\(N\), using only the group operation. \emph{Baby-step
giant-step} is deterministic: set \(m = \ceil{\sqrt N}\) and write the
unknown as \(x = im + j\) with \(0 \le i, j < m\); tabulate the ``baby
steps'' \(g^j\) for all \(j\), then walk the ``giant steps''
\(h (g^{-m})^i\) for \(i = 0, 1, 2, \dots\) until one equals a
tabulated \(g^j\), at which point \(h g^{-im} = g^j\) gives
\(x = im + j\). Both the table and the walk are \(O(\sqrt N)\), so the
cost is \(O(\sqrt N)\) time \emph{and} space. \emph{Pollard's rho}
(not developed here) is a randomised alternative with the same
\(O(\sqrt N)\) expected running time---on a heuristic analysis, made
explicit in \cref{sec:ellipticcurves}---but only \(O(1)\) space, and
is the algorithm used in practical attacks. Conversely, Shoup's
theorem shows that \(\Omega(\sqrt N)\) group operations are
\emph{necessary} for any generic algorithm when \(N\) is prime, so for
generic attacks the square-root cost is exact. Reaching a
