Both maps are linear and act between spaces of the same finite
dimension \(n\), so verifying one composition would suffice (a
one-sided inverse makes \(\mathrm{ev}_H\) injective, hence surjective by
rank--nullity, \cref{linalg:thm-rank-nullity}); we verify both. For
\(v \in \F^n\), the polynomial \(\mathrm{int}_H(v) = \sum_i v_i L_i\) has
value \(\sum_i v_i L_i(\omega^j) = v_j\) at \(\omega^j\) by
\cref{rootsofunity:prp-lagrangeprop}, so
\(\mathrm{ev}_H(\mathrm{int}_H(v)) = v\). For \(f \in \F[X]_{<n}\), the
polynomial \(\mathrm{int}_H(\mathrm{ev}_H(f)) = \sum_i f(\omega^i) L_i\)
is, by the uniqueness clause of \cref{rootsofunity:prp-lagrangeprop},
the unique degree-\(<n\) polynomial taking the values \(f(\omega^i)\) on
\(H\)---which is \(f\) itself. Hence the two maps are inverse; in
particular \(\mathrm{ev}_H\) is bijective and linear, i.e.\ an
isomorphism (\cref{linalg:def-linear-map}).
\end{proof}

In coordinates, \(\mathrm{ev}_H\) is given by a matrix, which identifies
evaluation on \(H\) with the Fourier transform. Matrix notation is fixed
as follows: an
\emph{\(n \times n\) matrix} over \(\F\) is a doubly indexed family
\(A = (A_{ik})_{0 \le i, k \le n-1}\) of elements of \(\F\), acting on a
vector \(c \in \F^n\) by \((Ac)_i = \sum_k A_{ik} c_k\)---the general
form, written out through the standard basis
(Example~\ref{linalg:exa-bases}), of a linear map \(\F^n \to \F^n\). The
product of two matrices is defined so that \((AB)\,c = A(Bc)\), which
forces \((AB)_{ij} = \sum_k A_{ik} B_{kj}\); the \emph{identity matrix}
\(I\), with entries \(\delta_{ik}\), acts as the identity map; and \(A\)
is \emph{invertible} if some matrix \(A^{-1}\) satisfies
\(A^{-1} A = A A^{-1} = I\).

\begin{definition}[Vandermonde / DFT matrix]\label{rootsofunity:def-vander}
Let \(f(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} c_k X^k\) have coefficient vector
\(c = (c_0, \dots, c_{n-1})\). Then \(\mathrm{ev}_H(f)\) has \(i\)-th
entry \(f(\omega^i) = \sum_k c_k\, \omega^{ik}\). Thus, relative to the
monomial basis on \(\F[X]_{<n}\) and the standard basis on \(\F^n\), the
\emph{Vandermonde matrix}
\[
V = \bigl(\omega^{ik}\bigr)_{0 \le i, k \le n-1} =
\begin{pmatrix}
1 & 1 & 1 & \cdots & 1\\
1 & \omega & \omega^2 & \cdots & \omega^{n-1}\\
1 & \omega^2 & \omega^4 & \cdots & \omega^{2(n-1)}\\
\vdots & & & & \vdots\\
1 & \omega^{n-1} & \omega^{2(n-1)} & \cdots & \omega^{(n-1)^2}
\end{pmatrix}
\]
---rows indexed by the evaluation point \(\omega^i\), columns by the
power \(k\)---represents \(\mathrm{ev}_H\), so that
\(\mathrm{ev}_H(f) = Vc\). We call \(V\) the (discrete Fourier)
transform matrix on \(H\).
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Invertibility and the inverse transform]
\label{rootsofunity:thm-vanderinv}
The matrix \(V\) of \cref{rootsofunity:def-vander} is invertible, with
\[
(V^{-1})_{k,i} = \frac{1}{n}\, \omega^{-ik}.
\]
Equivalently, \(V^{-1} = \tfrac{1}{n} \overline{V}\), where
\(\overline{V} = (\omega^{-ik})_{i,k}\) is the transform matrix built
from \(\omega^{-1}\) (itself a primitive \(n\)-th root of unity).
Consequently the coefficient and value representations are related by
the mutually inverse formulas
\[
c_k = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=0}^{n-1} f(\omega^i)\, \omega^{-ik},
\qquad
f(\omega^i) = \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} c_k\, \omega^{ik}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First, \(\omega^{-1} = \omega^{n-1}\) has order \(n\) by
Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-order-power} (with \(\gcd(n-1, n) = 1\)), so
\(\overline{V}\) is again a matrix of the shape in
\cref{rootsofunity:def-vander}. We show
\(\overline{V} V = nI\). The \((k, k')\) entry of \(\overline{V} V\) is
\[
\sum_{i=0}^{n-1} \omega^{-ik}\, \omega^{ik'}
= \sum_{i=0}^{n-1} \omega^{i(k'-k)}.
\]
By \cref{rootsofunity:prp-sumroots} this sum is \(n\) if
\(n \mid (k' - k)\) and \(0\) otherwise; for
\(k, k' \in \{0, \dots, n-1\}\) we have
\(n \mid (k' - k) \iff k = k'\). Hence \(\overline{V} V = nI\), and the
same computation with the roles of the two factors exchanged gives
\(V \overline{V} = nI\). Since \(n \ne 0\) in \(\F\)
(\cref{rootsofunity:thm-vanishing}), we may divide by \(n\):
\(V^{-1} = \tfrac{1}{n} \overline{V}\). The component formulas write
out the matrix identities \(\mathrm{ev}_H(f) = Vc\) and
\(c = V^{-1}\, \mathrm{ev}_H(f)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rootsofunity:rem-isodict}
\Cref{rootsofunity:thm-evaliso} and \cref{rootsofunity:thm-vanderinv}
state one isomorphism in abstract and in matrix form. Since the inverse of
\(V\) is, up to the scalar \(1/n\), the same matrix with \(\omega\)
replaced by \(\omega^{-1}\), the fast inverse transform of
\cref{rootsofunity:thm-fftcost} costs as much as the forward one.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The number-theoretic transform and convolution}
\label{rootsofunity:sub-ntt}

The map \(\mathrm{ev}_H\), read as the matrix \(V\), is the discrete
Fourier transform over a finite field rather than over \(\C\); since it
involves no analytic structure (no complex exponentials, no
convergence), it is called the \emph{number-theoretic transform}.

\begin{definition}[Number-theoretic transform]\label{rootsofunity:def-ntt}
Let \(\omega\) be a primitive \(n\)-th root of unity in \(\F\). The
\emph{number-theoretic transform} (NTT) sends the coefficient vector
of a polynomial to its value vector on \(H\): it is the evaluation map
\(\mathrm{ev}_H\) written in coordinates. Explicitly, the NTT of
\(a = (a_0, \dots, a_{n-1}) \in \F^n\) is
\(\hat{a} = (\hat{a}_0, \dots, \hat{a}_{n-1})\) with
\[
\hat{a}_j = \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} a_k\, \omega^{jk},
\qquad j = 0, \dots, n-1,
\]
each entry being the value at \(\omega^j\) of the polynomial with
coefficients \(a\). The \emph{inverse number-theoretic transform} is
interpolation---the recovery of the coefficients from the values---and
by \cref{rootsofunity:thm-vanderinv} it too is a single explicit sum:
\[
a_k = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{j=0}^{n-1} \hat{a}_j\, \omega^{-jk}.
\]
\end{definition}

The NTT has the convolution property of the classical DFT, the basis of
fast polynomial multiplication. The coefficients of a product are a
\emph{convolution}: with
\(f = \sum_i a_i X^i\) and \(g = \sum_j b_j X^j\), the coefficient of
\(X^k\) in \(fg\) is \(\sum_{i+j=k} a_i b_j\)
(\cref{polynomials:def:operations}). The \emph{cyclic} variant folds
indices modulo \(n\): for \(a, b \in \F^n\), the cyclic convolution
\(a * b \in \F^n\) has entries
\[
(a * b)_k \;=\; \sum_{\substack{0 \le i, j \le n-1\\
i + j \equiv k \pmod{n}}} a_i\, b_j,
\qquad k = 0, \dots, n-1.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Convolution theorem]\label{rootsofunity:thm-convolution}
For \(f, g \in \F[X]_{<n}\) with \(\deg f + \deg g < n\), let \(c = fg\) be
their product (of degree \(< n\)). Then for every \(h \in H\),
\[
c(h) = f(h)\, g(h),
\]
i.e.\ pointwise multiplication of value vectors corresponds to
polynomial multiplication. Equivalently, writing \(a, b \in \F^n\) for
the coefficient vectors of \(f, g\) (padded with zeros up to length
\(n\)),
\[
\widehat{a * b}_j = \hat{a}_j\, \hat{b}_j
\qquad (j = 0, \dots, n-1),
\]
so the NTT turns convolution into entrywise multiplication.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The first identity is the statement that evaluation at \(h\) is a ring
homomorphism \(\F[X] \to \F\)
(\cref{polynomials:prop:eval-hom}): \((fg)(h) = f(h)\, g(h)\). Since
\(\deg(fg) < n\), interpolation recovers the product \(c = fg\) from its
values on the \(n\)-point set \(H\)
(\cref{rootsofunity:prp-lagrangeprop}), so the entrywise product of the
value vectors uniquely determines \(c\).

The convolution statement is proved in three steps: (i) multiplication in
\(\F[X]/(X^n - 1)\) \emph{is} cyclic convolution of coefficient
vectors; (ii) evaluation on \(H\) is a ring isomorphism from that
quotient onto \(\F^n\) with entrywise operations; (iii) the degree
bound rules out wrap-around, so the cyclic product is the ordinary one.
For (i), work in the quotient ring
\(\F[X]/(X^n - 1)\) (\cref{ringsfields:def-quotient} and
\cref{ringsfields:prop-quotient-well-defined}, applied to the ideal of
multiples of \(X^n - 1\), \cref{ringsfields:def-ideal}). Every class has
a unique representative of degree \(< n\), by division with remainder
(\cref{polynomials:thm:division}); and multiplying two such
representatives and reducing replaces each \(X^k\) with \(k \ge n\) by
\(X^{k-n}\), since \(X^n \equiv 1\). The coefficient of \(X^k\) in the
reduced product of the representatives with coefficient vectors \(a\)
and \(b\) is therefore \(\sum_{i+j \equiv k \ (\mathrm{mod}\ n)} a_i
b_j = (a * b)_k\): multiplication in \(\F[X]/(X^n - 1)\) \emph{is}
cyclic convolution of coefficient vectors.

Next consider the map \([p] \mapsto \mathrm{ev}_H(p)\) from
\(\F[X]/(X^n - 1)\) to \(\F^n\), where \(\F^n\) is a commutative ring
under entrywise addition and multiplication (the ring laws hold in each
coordinate because they hold in \(\F\)), with multiplicative identity
\((1, \dots, 1)\). The map is well defined: two representatives of a
class differ by a multiple of \(X^n - 1\), which vanishes on \(H\)
(\cref{rootsofunity:thm-vanishing}). It is a ring homomorphism
(\cref{ringsfields:def-ringhom}), because evaluation at each point of
\(H\) is one (\cref{polynomials:prop:eval-hom}) and the operations on
\(\F^n\) are entrywise. And it is bijective: classes correspond to their
degree-\(<n\) representatives, on which \(\mathrm{ev}_H\) is a bijection
by \cref{rootsofunity:thm-evaliso}. Hence it is a ring isomorphism
\(\F[X]/(X^n - 1) \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ } \F^n\), and it sends the
product of the classes of \(f\) and \(g\)---coefficient vector
\(a * b\)---to the entrywise product of \(\hat{a}\) and \(\hat{b}\).
This is the displayed identity. Finally, when
\(\deg f + \deg g < n\), the ordinary product \(fg\) already has degree
\(< n\), so no folding occurs, the cyclic and ordinary products
coincide, and the statement computes \(fg\) itself.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Computational significance]\label{rootsofunity:rem-whyntt}
\Cref{rootsofunity:thm-convolution} reduces multiplying two
degree-\(<n/2\) polynomials---a quadratic-cost operation by the
schoolbook method---to: transform both (NTT), multiply the value
vectors entrywise (\(n\) multiplications), and transform back (inverse
NTT). If the NTT runs in \(O(n \log n)\) operations, the whole
multiplication costs \(O(n \log n)\). The fast Fourier transform,
constructed below, meets that hypothesis.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[The convolution theorem in \(\F_{17}\)]
\label{rootsofunity:exp-ntt17}
% Source: .wip/rewrite/math-guide/compute/ntt_f17.py
%   domain H = [1, 4, 16, 13]; a^ = [3, 9, 16, 10]; b^ = [4, 7, 2, 16]
%   entrywise = [12, 12, 15, 7]; inverse NTT = [3, 7, 2, 0]
In \(\F_{17}\), the element \(\omega = 4\) has
\(\omega^2 = 16 = -1\), so it is a primitive \(4\)-th root of unity
with domain \(H = \{1, 4, 16, 13\}\). To multiply \(f = 1 + 2X\)
and \(g = 3 + X\), pad both coefficient vectors to length \(4\) and
transform: \(\hat{a} = (3, 9, 16, 10)\) and
\(\hat{b} = (4, 7, 2, 16)\), the values of \(f\) and of \(g\) on
\(H\). Multiplying entrywise gives \((12, 12, 15, 7)\), the values
of \(fg\) on \(H\), and the inverse transform returns
\((3, 7, 2, 0)\): the coefficients of \(fg = 3 + 7X + 2X^2\),
agreeing with the schoolbook product. At \(n = 4\) nothing is saved;
the example exhibits the three-step procedure, which saves work once the
transforms themselves cost \(O(n \log n)\)
(\S\ref{rootsofunity:sub-fft}).
\end{example}

\subsection{The fast Fourier transform}\label{rootsofunity:sub-fft}

Computing \(\hat{a} = Va\) directly from
\cref{rootsofunity:def-vander} costs \(\Theta(n^2)\) field
multiplications: each of the \(n\) outputs is a sum of \(n\) products.
The fast Fourier transform (FFT)---here a number-theoretic
FFT---computes the same vector in \(O(n \log n)\) operations by a
divide-and-conquer recursion, provided \(n\) is a power of two or, more
generally, smooth (\cref{rootsofunity:rem-twoadic}).

\begin{theorem}[Cooley--Tukey radix-2 step]\label{rootsofunity:thm-fft}
Suppose \(n = 2m\) and \(\omega\) is a primitive \(n\)-th root of unity
in \(\F\). Given \(f(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} a_k X^k\), split its
coefficients by parity into
\[
f_{\mathrm{even}}(Y) = \sum_{k=0}^{m-1} a_{2k}\, Y^k,
\qquad
f_{\mathrm{odd}}(Y) = \sum_{k=0}^{m-1} a_{2k+1}\, Y^k,
\]
so that
\(f(X) = f_{\mathrm{even}}(X^2) + X\, f_{\mathrm{odd}}(X^2)\). Then
\(\eta := \omega^2\) is a primitive \(m\)-th root of unity, and for
\(j = 0, \dots, m-1\),
\begin{align}
f(\omega^{j}) &= f_{\mathrm{even}}(\eta^{j})
  + \omega^{j}\, f_{\mathrm{odd}}(\eta^{j}),
  \label{rootsofunity:eq-fftbutter1}\\
f(\omega^{j+m}) &= f_{\mathrm{even}}(\eta^{j})
  - \omega^{j}\, f_{\mathrm{odd}}(\eta^{j}).
  \label{rootsofunity:eq-fftbutter2}
\end{align}
Thus the length-\(n\) NTT of \(a\) reduces to two length-\(m\) NTTs (of
the even and odd coefficient subsequences, over the domain
\(\langle \eta \rangle\)) plus \(O(n)\) extra operations.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The identity
\(f(X) = f_{\mathrm{even}}(X^2) + X f_{\mathrm{odd}}(X^2)\) regroups
the sum \(\sum_k a_k X^k\) into even-index and odd-index terms; in the
odd terms one factor of \(X\) is pulled out, leaving powers \(X^{2k}\).
That \(\eta = \omega^2\) is a primitive \(m\)-th root of unity follows
from \cref{rootsofunity:lem-orderdivides} and the order-of-a-power
formula (Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-order-power}):
\(\ord(\omega^2) = n/\gcd(2, n) = n/2 = m\), using \(2 \mid n\).

It remains to evaluate. For \eqref{rootsofunity:eq-fftbutter1}, put
\(X = \omega^j\); then \(X^2 = \omega^{2j} = \eta^j\), so
\(f(\omega^j) = f_{\mathrm{even}}(\eta^j) + \omega^j
f_{\mathrm{odd}}(\eta^j)\). For \eqref{rootsofunity:eq-fftbutter2}, put
\(X = \omega^{j+m}\); then
\(X^2 = \omega^{2j+2m} = \omega^{2j}\, \omega^{n} = \eta^j \cdot 1 =
\eta^j\) (since \(2m = n\) and \(\omega^n = 1\)), while the linear
factor is \(\omega^{j+m} = \omega^j\, \omega^m = -\omega^j\). Here
\(\omega^m = -1\), because \(\omega^m\) has order
\(n/\gcd(m, n) = n/m = 2\)
(Corollary~\ref{groups:cor-order-power}), and \(-1\) is the unique
element of order \(2\) in a field: \(x^2 = 1\) with \(x \ne 1\) forces
\((x-1)(x+1) = 0\) and so \(x = -1\); and \(-1 \ne 1\), since
\(\charf \F \nmid n\) (\cref{rootsofunity:thm-vanishing}) with \(n\)
even forces \(\charf \F \ne 2\). Substituting,
\(f(\omega^{j+m}) = f_{\mathrm{even}}(\eta^j) - \omega^j
f_{\mathrm{odd}}(\eta^j)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The butterfly]\label{rootsofunity:rem-butterfly}
Equations
\eqref{rootsofunity:eq-fftbutter1}--\eqref{rootsofunity:eq-fftbutter2}
constitute the \emph{butterfly}: from the two subtransform outputs
\(E_j = f_{\mathrm{even}}(\eta^j)\) and
\(O_j = f_{\mathrm{odd}}(\eta^j)\) one forms the \emph{twiddle}
\(t_j = \omega^j O_j\) and reads off the two outputs \(E_j + t_j\) and
\(E_j - t_j\). In a diagram, \(E_j\) and \(O_j\) are two nodes on the
left, each joined to both outputs on the right, the two
\(E_j\)-edges with weight \(1\) and the two \(O_j\)-edges with weights
\(+\omega^j\) and \(-\omega^j\); the pair of crossing edges forms the
\(\times\) after which the operation is named. Each butterfly costs one
multiplication (the twiddle) and two additions, and there are \(n/2\) of
them per level.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[\(O(n \log n)\) cost of the FFT]\label{rootsofunity:thm-fftcost}
Let \(n = 2^t\) and let \(T(n)\) be the number of field additions and
multiplications the radix-2 recursion of \cref{rootsofunity:thm-fft}
uses to compute a length-\(n\) NTT. Then
\[
T(n) = 2\, T(n/2) + cn \quad (n > 1),
\qquad T(1) = O(1),
\]
for a constant \(c\), and therefore \(T(n) = O(n \log n)\). The inverse
NTT has the same cost, since it is the forward NTT with \(\omega\)
replaced by \(\omega^{-1}\), followed by scaling each entry by \(1/n\)
(\cref{rootsofunity:thm-vanderinv}).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The recursion is exactly \cref{rootsofunity:thm-fft}: a length-\(n\)
transform calls two length-\(n/2\) transforms (on the even and odd
coefficient subsequences) and combines them with \(n/2\) butterflies of
\(O(1)\) operations each, for the \(cn\) overhead. Unrolling, with
\(n = 2^t\),
\[
T(2^t) = 2\, T(2^{t-1}) + c\, 2^t.
\]
Divide by \(2^t\) and set \(S(t) = T(2^t)/2^t\): then
\(S(t) = S(t-1) + c\), so \(S(t) = S(0) + ct = O(1) + ct\), and
\(T(2^t) = 2^t S(t) = O(2^t) + ct\, 2^t = O(t\, 2^t)\). With
\(t = \log_2 n\) this is \(T(n) = O(n \log n)\). (Equivalently: the
recursion tree has \(\log_2 n\) levels, each performing \(\Theta(n)\)
total work across all subproblems at that level.) The inverse-transform
claim is \cref{rootsofunity:thm-vanderinv}: the inverse is the same
Cooley--Tukey recursion with \(\omega^{-1}\) in place of \(\omega\)
(also a primitive \(n\)-th root of unity), plus a final pass of \(n\)
divisions by \(n\), all within \(O(n \log n)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Mixed radix and smoothness]\label{rootsofunity:rem-mixedradix}
Fast transforms apply when \(n\) is smooth and the field contains a
primitive \(n\)-th root of unity, that is, \(n \mid q - 1\)
(\cref{rootsofunity:thm-existence}); for a power-of-two \(n\) dividing
\(q - 1\), every level of the recursion is a radix-2 step.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[A length-\(4\) transform]\label{rootsofunity:exp-fft4}
Take \(n = 4\) with primitive \(4\)-th root \(\omega\) (so
\(\omega^2 = -1\) and \(\omega^4 = 1\), and \(\eta = \omega^2 = -1\) is
the primitive \(2\)nd root). For
\(f = a_0 + a_1 X + a_2 X^2 + a_3 X^3\), the two length-\(2\)
subtransforms over the domain \(\{1, -1\}\)---each itself a single
butterfly, with twiddle \(\eta^0 = 1\)---are
\[
E = (a_0 + a_2,\; a_0 - a_2),
\qquad
O = (a_1 + a_3,\; a_1 - a_3),
\]
and the butterflies give
\[
\hat{a}_0 = E_0 + \omega^0 O_0, \quad
\hat{a}_2 = E_0 - \omega^0 O_0, \quad
\hat{a}_1 = E_1 + \omega^1 O_1, \quad
\hat{a}_3 = E_1 - \omega^1 O_1.
\]
(Outputs \(j\) and \(j + m\) are paired, as in
\eqref{rootsofunity:eq-fftbutter1}--\eqref{rootsofunity:eq-fftbutter2}.)
This uses \(2\) subtransforms of \(2\) additions each plus \(2\)
butterflies of \(2\) additions each (\(n/2 = 2\) per level,
\cref{rootsofunity:rem-butterfly})---together \(2\) twiddle
multiplications and \(8\) additions---versus \(16\) coefficient-products
and \(12\) additions for the direct \(4 \times 4\) matrix multiply; the
difference grows with \(n\).
\end{example}

\centering

\subsection{The role of smooth multiplicative domains in polynomial
proof systems}\label{rootsofunity:sub-proofsystems}

This subsection states how the preceding results are used in Halo~2,
the proof system of Orchard, which encodes a computation as polynomial
identities on the evaluation domain \(H = \mu_n\): the \(n\)-step table
described at the start of this section is indexed by the \(n\) points of
\(H\).

\begin{remark}[The roles played by \(H\), \(Z_H\), the Lagrange basis,
and the FFT]\label{rootsofunity:rem-whyproofsystems}
\leavevmode
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Witnesses as evaluations on \(H\).} A prover lays out the
  trace of a computation as the values of one or more polynomials on
  the \(n\) points of \(H\). By \cref{rootsofunity:thm-evaliso} this is
  the same data as a polynomial of degree \(< n\); the prover may move
  freely between the value (Lagrange) representation, suited to
  stating constraints row by row, and the coefficient representation,
  suited to committing and to low-degree testing. The change of
  representation is the (inverse) NTT.
\item \textbf{Constraints as divisibility by \(Z_H\).} ``Constraint
  polynomial \(C\) holds on every row'' means \(C\) vanishes on all of
  \(H\), which by \cref{rootsofunity:thm-vanishing} means exactly that
  \(Z_H = X^n - 1\) divides \(C\). The prover demonstrates this by
  exhibiting a quotient \(t\) with \(C(X) = t(X)\,(X^n - 1)\). The
  closed form \(Z_H(X) = X^n - 1\), evaluated by one exponentiation,
  makes this check cheap at a random point.
  \Cref{rootsofunity:prp-coset} extends the same technique to cosets
  \(\gamma H\), used to separate quotient and remainder domains.
\item \textbf{Lagrange selectors.} The Lagrange polynomials \(L_i\) of
  \cref{rootsofunity:def-lagrange} are \emph{selectors}: \(L_i\) is
  \(1\) on row \(i\) and \(0\) elsewhere
  (\cref{rootsofunity:prp-lagrangeprop}), so boundary and instance
  constraints (e.g.\ ``the first row equals the public input'') become
  \(L_0 \cdot (\dots)\). The closed form and the barycentric formula
  (\cref{rootsofunity:thm-lagrangeclosed},
  \cref{rootsofunity:rem-barycentric}) let the verifier evaluate these
  at a random challenge in \(O(n)\) or even \(O(\log n)\) field
  operations rather than reconstructing polynomials.
\item \textbf{Group structure for permutations and shifts.} Because
  \(H\) is a cyclic \emph{group}
  (\cref{rootsofunity:def-domain}), multiplication by \(\omega\) is the
  cyclic ``next-row'' shift \(\omega^i \mapsto \omega^{i+1}\).
  Constraints relating a row to its neighbour become \(f(\omega X)\)
  versus \(f(X)\); permutation arguments, on which Halo~2's copy
  constraints are built, rest on the action of the group on itself and on
  its cosets. An arbitrary set of \(n\) points has none of this structure.
\item \textbf{Smoothness for speed.} Provers manipulate polynomials of
  size \(n\) (often after enlarging the domain by a small constant
  factor for low-degree testing). Transforms between representations,
  multiplications and coset evaluations are computed by NTTs, which by
  \cref{rootsofunity:thm-fftcost} cost \(O(n \log n)\) instead of
  \(O(n^2)\) (at the sizes used in practice, the difference between
  feasible and infeasible computation), provided \(n\) is smooth and
  \(\Fq\) contains a primitive \(n\)-th root of unity. By
  \cref{rootsofunity:thm-existence} that requires \(n \mid q - 1\); by
  \cref{rootsofunity:rem-twoadic} this is why high-two-adicity primes
  are chosen for the scalar fields of the Pallas and Vesta curves
  (\cref{sec:ellipticcurves}).
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\section{Elliptic curves}\label{sec:ellipticcurves}

This section constructs elliptic-curve groups, the standard source of
groups of roughly \(2^{255}\) elements for which no discrete-logarithm
attack faster than the generic \(O(\sqrt{N})\) attacks of
\cref{numbertheory:rem:dlp-generic} is known. The multiplicative groups
\(\Fp^\times\) lack this property: the \emph{index-calculus} algorithms
exploit the factorisation of the integers behind the residues and run in
subexponential time, so \(p\) must have \emph{thousands} of bits to push
the cheapest known attack beyond \(2^{128}\) operations. An \emph{elliptic
curve} is the solution set of a cubic equation in two variables together
with one additional point, the ``point at infinity''. The section proves
that these points form an abelian group under a geometric rule
(\cref{ellipticcurves:thm-grouplaw}, associativity in sketch), derives
explicit addition formulas, including complete ones valid for every input
pair, bounds the group order by Hasse's theorem
(\cref{ellipticcurves:thm-hasse}), and states the elliptic-curve discrete
logarithm problem.

Throughout, \(K\) is a field whose characteristic
(\cref{ringsfields:def-char}) is neither \(2\) nor \(3\); in the
applications \(K\) is a prime field \(\Fp\) with \(p\) large. The running
example is the pair of \emph{Pasta curves}
\[
  E_p : y^2 = x^3 + 5 \quad\text{over } \Fp, \qquad
  E_q : y^2 = x^3 + 5 \quad\text{over } \Fq,
\]
where \(p\) and \(q\) are the two \(255\)-bit primes of
\cref{finitefields:rem:twoadicity} and
\cref{numbertheory:rem:pasta-3mod4},
\begin{align*}
  p &= 2^{254} + 0x224698fc094cf91b992d30ed00000001,\\
  q &= 2^{254} + 0x224698fc0994a8dd8c46eb2100000001 .
\end{align*}
The primes and the curve equations are the deployed constants, specified
in \href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#pallasandvesta}{\S5.4.9.6}
(``Pallas and Vesta'') of the Zcash protocol specification. The curve
\(E_p\) is called \emph{Pallas} and \(E_q\) \emph{Vesta}. By design they
form a \emph{cycle}: the number of \(\Fp\)-points of Pallas equals \(q\),
and the number of \(\Fq\)-points of Vesta equals \(p\). A toy curve over
\(\F_{11}\) is treated in parallel for hand computation.

\subsection{Weierstrass equations}\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-weierstrass}

\begin{definition}[Short Weierstrass equation]
\label{ellipticcurves:def-weierstrass}
Let \(K\) be a field of characteristic \(\neq 2, 3\), and let
\(a, b \in K\). The \emph{short Weierstrass equation} with coefficients
\(a, b\) is the equation
\begin{equation}\label{ellipticcurves:eq-weierstrass}
  y^2 = x^3 + a x + b .
\end{equation}
The elements \(a\) and \(b\) are the \emph{Weierstrass coefficients}.
\end{definition}

Over a field of characteristic \(\neq 2, 3\), an invertible affine
change of variables reduces the most general cubic of this shape,
\(y^2 + a_1 xy + a_3 y = x^3 + a_2 x^2 + a_4 x + a_6\), to the
form~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-weierstrass}: completing the square in
\(y\) (possible since \(2 \neq 0\)) removes the \(xy\) and \(y\) terms
but changes the quadratic coefficient to \(a_2+a_1^2/4\); translating
with the substitution \(x=X-(a_2+a_1^2/4)/3\) (possible since
\(3 \neq 0\)) removes the \(X^2\) term. Characteristics \(2\) and \(3\)
are excluded for this reason: those cases require the longer Weierstrass
form, and they do not arise for the Pasta curves, which are defined over
large prime fields.

Cubics whose graph has a self-intersection or a cusp are excluded,
because the group law defined below fails at such a singular point.

\begin{definition}[Discriminant and nonsingularity]
\label{ellipticcurves:def-discriminant}
The \emph{discriminant} of the short Weierstrass
equation~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-weierstrass} is
\begin{equation}\label{ellipticcurves:eq-discriminant}
  \Delta = -16\,(4 a^3 + 27 b^2) \in K .
\end{equation}
The equation, and the cubic \(f(x) = x^3 + ax + b\), is
\emph{nonsingular} (or \emph{smooth}) if \(\Delta \neq 0\),
equivalently \(4 a^3 + 27 b^2 \neq 0\). (The factor \(-16\) is a
convention inherited from the general Weierstrass form; over the fields
considered here it is nonzero and does not affect the condition.)
\end{definition}

The following lemma expresses \(4a^3 + 27b^2\) through the roots of a
\emph{depressed cubic} (a cubic with no square term); the smoothness
criterion is derived from it.

\begin{lemma}[Discriminant of a depressed cubic]
\label{ellipticcurves:lem-cubicdisc}
Let \(f(x) = x^3 + ax + b = (x - r_1)(x - r_2)(x - r_3)\) with
\(r_1, r_2, r_3\) in some field containing \(K\). Then
\[
  \prod_{i < j} (r_i - r_j)^2 = -(4a^3 + 27 b^2) .
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Expanding \((x-r_1)(x-r_2)(x-r_3)\) and comparing coefficients
with \(x^3 + ax + b\) gives Vieta's relations for the depressed cubic:
\[
  r_1 + r_2 + r_3 = 0, \qquad
  r_1 r_2 + r_1 r_3 + r_2 r_3 = a, \qquad
  r_1 r_2 r_3 = -b .
\]
Differentiating \(f(x) = \prod_i (x - r_i)\) by the product rule and
evaluating at a root annihilates every term but one:
\(f'(r_i) = \prod_{j \neq i} (r_i - r_j)\). Multiplying the three
evaluations pairs each factor \((r_i - r_j)\) with \((r_j - r_i)\):
\[
  \prod_{i} f'(r_i)
  = \prod_{i < j} (r_i - r_j)(r_j - r_i)
  = (-1)^3 \prod_{i < j} (r_i - r_j)^2 .
\]
It remains to compute \(\prod_i f'(r_i) = \prod_i (3 r_i^2 + a)\).
Expanding the product and collecting the elementary symmetric functions
of the squares \(r_i^2\),
\[
  \prod_i (3 r_i^2 + a)
  = 27 \prod_i r_i^2
  + 9a \sum_{i<j} r_i^2 r_j^2
  + 3a^2 \sum_i r_i^2 + a^3 .
\]
Vieta's relations evaluate each symmetric function: with
\(e_1 = 0\), \(e_2 = a\), \(e_3 = -b\),
\[
  \sum_i r_i^2 = e_1^2 - 2e_2 = -2a, \qquad
  \sum_{i<j} r_i^2 r_j^2 = e_2^2 - 2 e_1 e_3 = a^2, \qquad
  \prod_i r_i^2 = e_3^2 = b^2 .
\]
Substituting,
\(\prod_i f'(r_i) = 27 b^2 + 9a^3 - 6a^3 + a^3 = 4a^3 + 27b^2\),
and the two displays together give
\(\prod_{i<j}(r_i - r_j)^2 = -(4a^3 + 27b^2)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Equivalent conditions for smoothness]\label{ellipticcurves:prop-smooth}
Let \(f(x) = x^3 + ax + b \in K[x]\). The following are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item The quantity \(4a^3 + 27 b^2\) of
    \cref{ellipticcurves:def-discriminant} is nonzero.
  \item The cubic \(f\) has no repeated root in the algebraic closure
    \(\overline{K}\)---the smallest extension field of \(K\) in which
    every nonconstant polynomial over \(K\) factors into linear factors.
  \item The polynomials \(f\) and \(f'\) are coprime in
    \(\overline{K}[x]\).
  \item There is no point \((x_0, y_0) \in \overline{K}^2\) satisfying
    the curve equation \(y^2 = f(x)\) together with the two
    partial-derivative equations \(2y = 0\) and \(f'(x) = 0\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Equivalence \((1)\Leftrightarrow(2)\): over \(\overline{K}\) the monic
cubic factors as \(f = (x-r_1)(x-r_2)(x-r_3)\), and
\cref{ellipticcurves:lem-cubicdisc} gives
\(\prod_{i<j}(r_i-r_j)^2 = -(4a^3+27b^2)\). The left side vanishes
exactly when two roots coincide, so \(f\) has a repeated root if and
only if \(4a^3 + 27b^2 = 0\).

Equivalence \((2)\Leftrightarrow(3)\): this is the derivative test of
\cref{polynomials:thm:multiple-root}, applied over \(\overline{K}\)
where \(f\) splits: a repeated root of \(f\) is a common root of \(f\)
and \(f'\), hence a common factor \((x - \alpha)\), and conversely a
nontrivial \(\gcd(f, f')\) has a root in \(\overline{K}\) which is then
a repeated root of \(f\).

Equivalence \((4)\Leftrightarrow(2)\): a singular point of the affine
curve \(y^2 = f(x)\) is a point where both partial derivatives of
\(g(x,y) = y^2 - f(x)\) vanish, namely
\(\partial g/\partial y = 2y = 0\) and
\(\partial g/\partial x = -f'(x) = 0\). Since
\(\charf(K) \neq 2\), the first equation forces \(y_0 = 0\); the curve
equation then gives \(f(x_0) = 0\), which combined with
\(f'(x_0) = 0\) says \(x_0\) is a repeated root of \(f\)
(\cref{polynomials:thm:multiple-root}(1)). Conversely a repeated root
\(x_0\) of \(f\) yields the singular point \((x_0, 0)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Elliptic curve and rational points]
\label{ellipticcurves:def-curve}
An \emph{elliptic curve} over \(K\) is a short Weierstrass
equation~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-weierstrass} with nonzero
discriminant, \(4a^3 + 27b^2 \neq 0\). For any field extension
\(L \supseteq K\), the set of \emph{\(L\)-rational affine points} is
\[
  E^{\mathrm{aff}}(L)
  = \{\, (x,y) \in L \times L : y^2 = x^3 + ax + b \,\},
\]
and the set of \emph{\(L\)-rational points} is
\[
  E(L) = E^{\mathrm{aff}}(L) \cup \{\mathcal{O}\},
\]
where \(\mathcal{O}\) is a single formal symbol called the \emph{point
at infinity}. When \(L = K\) one writes \(E\) for the curve and
\(E(K)\) for its rational points.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[The Pasta curves are smooth]
\label{ellipticcurves:rem-pasta-smooth}
For the Pasta curves \(a = 0\) and \(b = 5\), so
\(4a^3 + 27b^2 = 27 \cdot 25 = 675 = 3^3 \cdot 5^2\). The curve is
therefore nonsingular whenever the characteristic does not divide
\(675\), i.e.\ whenever the prime is neither \(3\) nor \(5\); the Pasta
primes are far larger than \(5\), so the condition holds. More generally,
any curve
\(y^2 = x^3 + b\) with \(b \neq 0\) is nonsingular away from
characteristics \(2\) and \(3\).
\end{remark}

\subsection{The point at infinity, geometrically}
\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-infinity}

The point \(\mathcal{O}\) is the identity element of the group law
defined below. To exhibit it as a point of the curve, embed the affine
plane in the \emph{projective plane}. A
point of the projective plane \(\mathbb{P}^2(K)\) is an
equivalence class \([X : Y : Z]\) of nonzero triples
\((X, Y, Z) \in K^3 \setminus \{0\}\), where
\((X,Y,Z) \sim (\lambda X, \lambda Y, \lambda Z)\) for every
\(\lambda \in K^{\times}\). The affine points \((x, y)\) embed as
\([x : y : 1]\); the points with \(Z = 0\) form the \emph{line at
infinity}, the extra ``directions''. Homogenising the Weierstrass
equation~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-weierstrass} by setting
\(x = X/Z\), \(y = Y/Z\) and clearing denominators gives the
\emph{projective Weierstrass equation}
\begin{equation}\label{ellipticcurves:eq-projective}
  Y^2 Z = X^3 + a X Z^2 + b Z^3 .
\end{equation}
Setting \(Z = 0\) in~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-projective} forces
\(X^3 = 0\), hence \(X = 0\), leaving the \emph{single} projective
point \([0 : 1 : 0]\). This is the point at infinity \(\mathcal{O}\).
It is the point at infinity in the vertical direction, and every vertical
line \(x = \text{const}\) passes through it.

\subsection{The chord-and-tangent group law: geometry}
\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-grouplaw-geom}

% FIGURE (fig:ellipticcurves-chordtangent): chord-and-tangent addition
% and doubling on a real Weierstrass curve --- a chord through P and Q
% meeting the curve at R', reflected to P+Q; and a tangent at P meeting
% the curve at R', reflected to [2]P. Dedicated figures pass.

The geometric definition of addition rests on one fact: a line meets a
cubic curve in exactly three points, counted with multiplicity, once the
point at infinity is included.

\begin{definition}[The group law, geometric form]
\label{ellipticcurves:def-grouplaw-geom}
Let \(P, Q \in E(K)\). Define \(P + Q\) as follows.
\begin{itemize}
  \item If \(P = \mathcal{O}\), set \(P + Q = Q\); if
    \(Q = \mathcal{O}\), set \(P + Q = P\).
  \item Otherwise \(P = (x_1, y_1)\) and \(Q = (x_2, y_2)\) are affine.
    Draw the line \(\ell\) through \(P\) and \(Q\); if \(P = Q\), let
    \(\ell\) be the tangent line to the curve at \(P\). The line
    \(\ell\) meets the curve in a third point \(R'\), counted with
    multiplicity, where a vertical \(\ell\) has third point
    \(\mathcal{O}\). Define \(P + Q\) to be the \emph{reflection of
    \(R'\) across the \(x\)-axis}: if \(R' = (x_3, -y_3)\) then
    \(P + Q = (x_3, y_3)\), and if \(R' = \mathcal{O}\) then
    \(P + Q = \mathcal{O}\).
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

The reflection turns the rule that \emph{the three intersection points of
any line with the curve add up to \(\mathcal{O}\)} into a group law with
identity \(\mathcal{O}\): reflection across the \(x\)-axis sends an
affine point \((x, y)\) to \((x, -y)\), its additive inverse by the next
subsection, and fixes \(\mathcal{O}\). The rule is stated here as a
heuristic; every use of it below is discharged by explicit computation
with the formulas of \S\ref{ellipticcurves:subsec-formulas}, which obtain
the third intersection point from Vieta's relations, as in
\cref{ellipticcurves:lem-cubicdisc}.

\subsection{Explicit affine formulas}\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-formulas}

Fix an elliptic curve \(E : y^2 = x^3 + ax + b\) over \(K\), and let
\(P = (x_1, y_1)\) and \(Q = (x_2, y_2)\) denote affine points.

\begin{definition}[Negation]\label{ellipticcurves:def-negation}
Define
\[
  -\mathcal{O} = \mathcal{O}, \qquad -(x_1, y_1) = (x_1, -y_1) .
\]
The reflected point \((x_1, -y_1)\) is again on the curve, since the
curve equation depends on \(y\) only through \(y^2\). Geometrically,
the line through \(P\) and \(-P\) is the vertical line \(x = x_1\),
which meets the curve at \(P\), \(-P\), and \(\mathcal{O}\).
\end{definition}

\begin{construction}[Chord addition]\label{ellipticcurves:con-chord}
Suppose \(P, Q \neq \mathcal{O}\) and \(x_1 \neq x_2\). The line
through \(P\) and \(Q\) is \(y = \lambda x + \mu\) with
\[
  \lambda = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}, \qquad
  \mu = y_1 - \lambda x_1 .
\]
Substituting the line into the curve equation,
\((\lambda x + \mu)^2 = x^3 + ax + b\), and collecting terms gives
\[
  g(x) := x^3 - \lambda^2 x^2 + (a - 2\lambda\mu)\, x + (b - \mu^2)
  = 0 .
\]
Both \(x_1\) and \(x_2\) are roots of the monic cubic \(g\): each
point lies on the line and on the curve, so
\(g(x_i) = f(x_i) - (\lambda x_i + \mu)^2 = f(x_i) - y_i^2 = 0\).
Since \(x_1 \neq x_2\), factoring out \((x - x_1)(x - x_2)\)
(\cref{polynomials:thm:factor}, applied twice) leaves a monic linear
factor \(x - x_3\) with \(x_3 \in K\): the \(x\)-coordinate of the
third intersection point \(R'\). By Vieta, the sum of the roots equals
the negative of the \(x^2\)-coefficient---so
\(x_1 + x_2 + x_3 = \lambda^2\), which determines the third root without
factoring. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{ellipticcurves:eq-add}
  x_3 = \lambda^2 - x_1 - x_2, \qquad
  y_3 = \lambda\,(x_1 - x_3) - y_1 ,
\end{equation}
and \(P + Q = (x_3, y_3)\). The \(y\)-formula already incorporates the
reflection across the \(x\)-axis: the third intersection point is
\((x_3, \lambda x_3 + \mu)\), and reflecting gives
\(y_3 = -(\lambda x_3 + \mu)
= -(\lambda x_3 + y_1 - \lambda x_1)
= \lambda(x_1 - x_3) - y_1\).
\end{construction}

\begin{construction}[Tangent doubling]\label{ellipticcurves:con-tangent}
Suppose \(P = Q = (x_1, y_1)\) with \(y_1 \neq 0\). Implicit
differentiation of \(y^2 = x^3 + ax + b\) yields the tangent slope:
from \(2y \, dy = (3x^2 + a)\, dx\),
\[
  \lambda = \frac{3 x_1^2 + a}{2 y_1} .
\]
With \(\mu = y_1 - \lambda x_1\), substituting \(y = \lambda x + \mu\)
into the curve equation produces the same monic cubic
\(g(x) = x^3 + ax + b - (\lambda x + \mu)^2\) as in
\cref{ellipticcurves:con-chord}, and here \(x_1\) is a \emph{double}
root. The statement that ``tangency counts the intersection twice'' is
verified directly: \(g(x_1) = f(x_1) - y_1^2 = 0\), and
\[
  g'(x_1) = 3x_1^2 + a - 2\lambda\,(\lambda x_1 + \mu)
          = 3x_1^2 + a - 2\lambda y_1 = 0
\]
by the choice of \(\lambda\), so \(x_1\) is a repeated root of \(g\)
(\cref{polynomials:thm:multiple-root}(1)). Factoring
\((x - x_1)^2\) out of \(g\) leaves a monic linear factor
\(x - x_3\) with \(x_3 \in K\), and Vieta now reads
\(x_1 + x_1 + x_3 = \lambda^2\), hence
\begin{equation}\label{ellipticcurves:eq-double}
  x_3 = \lambda^2 - 2 x_1, \qquad
  y_3 = \lambda\,(x_1 - x_3) - y_1, \qquad
  [2]P = (x_3, y_3) .
\end{equation}
\end{construction}

% FIGURE (fig:ellipticcurves-vertical): the vertical-line cases escaping
% to the point at infinity --- the line through P and -P, and the
% vertical tangent at a point with y = 0, each with third point O.
% Dedicated figures pass.

The remaining cases are exactly those in which the line is vertical.
\begin{itemize}
  \item If \(P = \mathcal{O}\), then \(P + Q = Q\); symmetrically if
    \(Q = \mathcal{O}\).
  \item If \(x_1 = x_2\) but \(y_1 = -y_2\) (so \(Q = -P\), including
    the subcase \(y_1 = y_2 = 0\)), the line is vertical, the third
    point is \(\mathcal{O}\), and \(P + Q = \mathcal{O}\).
  \item If \(P = Q\) and \(y_1 = 0\), the tangent is vertical (the
    slope formula in \cref{ellipticcurves:con-tangent} has zero
    denominator), so \([2]P = \mathcal{O}\). These are exactly the
    points of order two, studied in
    \cref{ellipticcurves:subsec-torsion}.
\end{itemize}
Every case is covered: either an input is \(\mathcal{O}\); or both
inputs are affine with \(x_1 \neq x_2\)
(use~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-add}); or both are affine with
\(x_1 = x_2\), in which case either \(y_1 = -y_2\) (result
\(\mathcal{O}\)) or \(y_1 = y_2 \neq 0\)
(use~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-double}).

\begin{proposition}[Closure]\label{ellipticcurves:prop-closure}
For all \(P, Q \in E(K)\), the point \(P + Q\) computed by the case
analysis above lies in \(E(K)\). In particular \(E(K)\) is closed
under \(+\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If either input is \(\mathcal{O}\), the result is the other input,
which lies in \(E(K)\) by hypothesis; if the result is \(\mathcal{O}\),
it lies in \(E(K)\) by definition. Otherwise \((x_3, y_3)\) is produced
by~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-add} or~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-double},
and in both constructions \(x_3\) was exhibited as a root of the cubic
\(g(x) = x^3 + ax + b - (\lambda x + \mu)^2\)---as the third root
beside \(x_1, x_2\) in \cref{ellipticcurves:con-chord}, and beside the
double root \(x_1\) in \cref{ellipticcurves:con-tangent}. The equation
\(g(x_3) = 0\) states precisely that the third intersection point
\((x_3, \lambda x_3 + \mu) = (x_3, -y_3)\) satisfies the curve
equation; and since the curve equation is invariant under
\(y \mapsto -y\), the reflected point \((x_3, y_3)\) is on the curve
too, so
\(P + Q \in E^{\mathrm{aff}}(K) \subseteq E(K)\). For rationality: all
coordinates are rational expressions in
\(x_1, x_2, y_1, y_2, a\) whose denominators are \(x_2 - x_1\) or
\(2 y_1\), nonzero in the cases where each formula is invoked; so
\(x_3, y_3 \in K\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Complete versus incomplete addition]
\label{ellipticcurves:rem-complete}
The chord formula~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-add} is \emph{incomplete}:
it fails whenever \(x_1 = x_2\)---the doubling and inverse cases---and
an implementation must branch into the case analysis above.
\emph{Complete} addition formulas, valid for \emph{all} input pairs
including \(P = Q\) and \(P = -Q\), exist for the curves used here, at
the cost of more field operations per addition;
\cref{ellipticcurves:con-complete} exhibits the deployed set and
\cref{ellipticcurves:prop-complete} proves it complete. Later volumes
depend on the distinction. Halo~2's elliptic-curve gadgets provide
complete addition and use incomplete gates only where their callers
justify the exclusions. The Sinsemilla hash of Orchard deliberately uses
the \emph{incomplete} formulas nondeterministically: an exceptional case does
not violate the constraints but leaves the affected output unconstrained
(the prover may choose the intermediate slope freely), which the
specification models by letting the hash return \(\bot\) and weakening
the proved statement accordingly; its Merkle path validity check may pass
on the resulting sentinel values. Security follows not from the proof
system but from
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#thmsinsemillaex}{Theorem~5.4.4}
of the Zcash protocol specification: any input reaching an exceptional
case of the incomplete addition yields a nontrivial discrete-logarithm
relation among the Sinsemilla generators, so under discrete-log hardness
on Pallas (\cref{ellipticcurves:rem-ecdlp-hardness}) such inputs are
infeasible to find, for honest and adversarial provers alike.
\end{remark}

A circuit cannot branch. A proof system checks the group law by a fixed
list of polynomial identities in the input, output, and auxiliary cells of
a row, evaluated on every row alike, so each branch of the case analysis
appears as a factor that vanishes exactly when the branch is not taken.
The deployed gate uses five auxiliary cells, four of them
``inverse-or-zero'' hints that let a polynomial test a field element for
zero.

\begin{construction}[Complete addition as deployed]
\label{ellipticcurves:con-complete}
Let \(E : y^2 = x^3 + b\) over \(\Fp\) with \(p > 3\), and encode the
point at infinity as the pair \((0, 0)\), so that every element of
\(E(\Fp)\) is a pair of field elements. For \(z \in \Fp\) let
\(\operatorname{inv}_0(z)\) be \(z^{-1}\) if \(z \neq 0\) and \(0\) if
\(z = 0\). For encoded inputs \(P = (x_p, y_p)\) and
\(Q = (x_q, y_q)\) the prover fills five auxiliary cells,
\begin{gather*}
  \alpha = \operatorname{inv}_0(x_q - x_p), \qquad
  \beta = \operatorname{inv}_0(x_p), \qquad
  \gamma = \operatorname{inv}_0(x_q), \qquad
  \delta =
  \begin{cases}
    \operatorname{inv}_0(y_q + y_p) & \text{if } x_q = x_p,\\
    0 & \text{otherwise},
  \end{cases}\\[4pt]
  \lambda =
  \begin{cases}
    (y_q - y_p)\,\alpha & \text{if } x_q \neq x_p,\\
    3x_p^2\,(2y_p)^{-1} & \text{if } x_q = x_p \text{ and } y_p \neq 0,\\
    0 & \text{otherwise},
  \end{cases}
\end{gather*}
and an output pair \((x_r, y_r)\): it is \(Q\) if \(x_p = 0\); else
\(P\) if \(x_q = 0\); else \((0, 0)\) if \(x_q = x_p\) and
\(y_q = -y_p\); else \(x_r = \lambda^2 - x_p - x_q\) and
\(y_r = \lambda(x_p - x_r) - y_p\). The verifier accepts the row iff the
twelve identities
\begin{align*}
  (x_q - x_p)\bigl((x_q - x_p)\lambda - (y_q - y_p)\bigr) &= 0,
    \tag{C1}\\
  \bigl(1 - (x_q - x_p)\alpha\bigr)\bigl(2y_p\lambda - 3x_p^2\bigr) &= 0,
    \tag{C2}\\
  x_p x_q (x_q - x_p)\,(\lambda^2 - x_p - x_q - x_r) &= 0,
    \tag{C3a}\\
  x_p x_q (x_q - x_p)\,\bigl(\lambda(x_p - x_r) - y_p - y_r\bigr) &= 0,
    \tag{C3b}\\
  x_p x_q (y_q + y_p)\,(\lambda^2 - x_p - x_q - x_r) &= 0,
    \tag{C3c}\\
  x_p x_q (y_q + y_p)\,\bigl(\lambda(x_p - x_r) - y_p - y_r\bigr) &= 0,
    \tag{C3d}\\
  (1 - x_p\beta)\,(x_r - x_q) &= 0,
    \tag{C4a}\\
  (1 - x_p\beta)\,(y_r - y_q) &= 0,
    \tag{C4b}\\
  (1 - x_q\gamma)\,(x_r - x_p) &= 0,
    \tag{C5a}\\
  (1 - x_q\gamma)\,(y_r - y_p) &= 0,
    \tag{C5b}\\
  \bigl(1 - (x_q - x_p)\alpha - (y_q + y_p)\delta\bigr)\,x_r &= 0,
    \tag{C6a}\\
  \bigl(1 - (x_q - x_p)\alpha - (y_q + y_p)\delta\bigr)\,y_r &= 0
    \tag{C6b}
\end{align*}
hold in \(\Fp\). These are, in this order, the twelve constraints of the
gate \texttt{complete addition} in the \texttt{halo2\_gadgets} crate, each
carrying there one further factor, a per-row cell equal to \(1\) on the
rows where the gate applies and \(0\) elsewhere, which switches the list
on or off; the crate assigns the cells as above, and the circuit field is
the Pallas base field \(\Fp\).
\end{construction}

\begin{proposition}[The gate is complete and sound]
\label{ellipticcurves:prop-complete}
Suppose no affine point of \(E(\Fp)\) has \(x = 0\) and none has
\(y = 0\)---for \(y^2 = x^3 + b\), that \(b\) is a non-square and \(-b\)
a non-cube in \(\Fp\). Then for all \(P, Q \in E(\Fp)\), encoded as in
\cref{ellipticcurves:con-complete}:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item (\emph{completeness}) the prover's cells satisfy
    (C1)--(C6b), and the output pair encodes \(P + Q\);
  \item (\emph{soundness}) every
    \((\lambda, \alpha, \beta, \gamma, \delta, x_r, y_r) \in \Fp^7\)
    satisfying (C1)--(C6b) has \((x_r, y_r)\) equal to the encoding of
    \(P + Q\).
\end{enumerate}
This is what \emph{complete} means for a gate: the twelve identities are
satisfiable for every input pair---doublings, inverse pairs, and the
identity included---and the output they admit is unique, so they are
the graph of the total function \(+ \colon E(\Fp) \times E(\Fp) \to
E(\Fp)\), with no case left to a branch outside the constraint system.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Under the hypothesis an encoded pair has \(x = 0\) iff it is
\(\mathcal{O}\), and two affine points with the same \(x\)-coordinate
have \(y_q = \pm y_p\) with \(y_p \neq 0\), so they are equal or inverse
and not both. Every case uses one observation: for \(z \in \Fp\) and
any cell value \(h\), the factor \(1 - zh\) equals \(1\) when \(z = 0\),
whatever \(h\) is, while for \(z \neq 0\) the honest choice
\(h = z^{-1}\) makes it \(0\). A constraint of the shape
\((1 - zh)\,t = 0\) therefore forces \(t = 0\) exactly when \(z = 0\)
and is discharged by the honest hint otherwise. Five cases exhaust the
input pairs.

\emph{Case \(P = \mathcal{O}\).} Then \(x_p = 0\), so (C4a)--(C4b) force
\((x_r, y_r) = (x_q, y_q)\), the encoding of \(\mathcal{O} + Q = Q\);
that is soundness. The prover outputs \(Q\) by the first clause, and its
cells satisfy the rest: (C1) reads \(x_q(x_q\lambda - y_q) = 0\) and
holds for \(\lambda = y_q\,\operatorname{inv}_0(x_q)\); (C2) and
(C3a)--(C3d) carry a factor \(x_p\) or \(2y_p\lambda - 3x_p^2\), both
zero; (C5a)--(C5b) are discharged by \(\gamma = x_q^{-1}\) if
\(Q \neq \mathcal{O}\), and read \((x_r, y_r) = (0, 0) = (x_p, y_p)\) if
\(Q = \mathcal{O}\); and (C6a)--(C6b) have first factor
\(1 - x_q\alpha - 0\), which is \(0\) for \(\alpha = x_q^{-1}\) when
\(Q \neq \mathcal{O}\), while for \(Q = \mathcal{O}\) the other factor
\(x_r = y_r = 0\) vanishes.

\emph{Case \(Q = \mathcal{O}\), \(P \neq \mathcal{O}\).} Then
\(x_q = 0 \neq x_p\), so (C5a)--(C5b) force \((x_r, y_r) = (x_p, y_p)\),
the encoding of \(P\): soundness. The prover outputs \(P\) by the second
clause, with \(\alpha = (-x_p)^{-1}\) and
\(\lambda = (-y_p)\alpha = y_p/x_p\); then (C1) reads
\((-x_p)(-x_p\lambda + y_p) = 0\) and holds; (C2) and (C6a)--(C6b) carry
the factor \(1 - (x_q - x_p)\alpha = 0\), the latter with
\(\delta = 0\); (C3a)--(C3d) carry \(x_q = 0\); and (C4a)--(C4b) are
discharged by \(\beta = x_p^{-1}\).

\emph{Case \(P, Q\) affine with \(x_q \neq x_p\).} Constraint (C1)
forces \(\lambda\) to be the chord slope \((y_q - y_p)/(x_q - x_p)\),
and since \(x_p x_q (x_q - x_p) \neq 0\), (C3a)--(C3b) force
\((x_r, y_r)\) to be the chord formula~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-add}---the
encoding of \(P + Q\), which is affine because \(x_p \neq x_q\). That is
soundness. The prover's \(\lambda\) is that slope and its output that
formula, by the fourth clause; (C3c)--(C3d) contain the same two
vanishing factors; (C2), (C4a)--(C4b), (C5a)--(C5b) are discharged by
the honest \(\alpha\), \(\beta\), \(\gamma\); and (C6a)--(C6b) have
first factor \(1 - 1 - 0 = 0\), since \(\delta = 0\).

\emph{Case \(Q = -P\), \(P\) affine.} Here \(x_q = x_p\) and
\(y_q + y_p = 0\), so both hint-bearing products in (C6a)--(C6b) are
\(0\) whatever \(\alpha\) and \(\delta\) are, and the constraints force
\(x_r = y_r = 0\): the encoding of \(P + (-P) = \mathcal{O}\), which is
soundness. The prover outputs \((0, 0)\) by the third clause; (C1) and
(C3a)--(C3b) carry \(x_q - x_p = 0\), and (C3c)--(C3d) carry
\(y_q + y_p = 0\); (C2), whose first factor is now \(1\), needs
\(2y_p\lambda = 3x_p^2\), which the honest
\(\lambda = 3x_p^2(2y_p)^{-1}\) supplies (\(y_p \neq 0\)); and
(C4a)--(C5b) are discharged by \(\beta = \gamma = x_p^{-1}\).

\emph{Case \(Q = P\) affine.} Again \(x_q = x_p\) fixes
\((x_q - x_p)\alpha = 0\), so (C2) forces \(\lambda\) to be the tangent
slope \(3x_p^2/(2y_p)\), and since
\(x_p x_q (y_q + y_p) = 2x_p^2 y_p \neq 0\), (C3c)--(C3d) force
\((x_r, y_r)\) to be the doubling
formula~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-double}---the encoding of \([2]P\),
affine because \(y_p \neq 0\). That is soundness. The prover's
\(\lambda\) is that slope and its output that formula, by the fourth
clause; (C1) and (C3a)--(C3b) carry \(x_q - x_p = 0\); (C4a)--(C5b) are
discharged by \(\beta = \gamma = x_p^{-1}\); and (C6a)--(C6b) have first
factor \(1 - 0 - 2y_p\delta = 0\) for the honest
\(\delta = (2y_p)^{-1}\).
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[The gate on the toy curve]
\label{ellipticcurves:exa-toy-complete}
% Source: .wip/rewrite/math-guide/compute/complete_add_toy.py
%   admissible S = O + 8 affine points; all 81 pairs exact
%     incl. 8 doublings, 8 inverse pairs, 17 with O
%     [2](5,3): lambda = 7, delta = 2, R = (6, 1) = (6, 1)
%     (5,3)+(5,8): lambda = 7 (tangent slope), delta = 0, R = (0, 0)
%     O+(5,3): lambda = 5, alpha = 9, beta = 0, R = (5, 3)
%   (0,4)+(5,3): gate outputs (5,3) -- x = 0 read as O; true sum (10,9)
%   (0,4)+(0,7): unsatisfiable (C4 forces R = Q, C5 forces R = P)
%   (8,0)+(8,0): unsatisfiable (C2 forces 3*64 = 0); true sum O
Pallas satisfies both hypotheses of \cref{ellipticcurves:prop-complete}:
\(5\) is a non-square in \(\Fp\) (\cref{numbertheory:ex:pasta-five})
and \(-5\) is not a cube (\cref{ellipticcurves:rem-pasta-odd}). The toy
curve \(y^2 = x^3 + 5\) over \(\F_{11}\) of \cref{ellipticcurves:exa-toy}
satisfies neither---\(5 = 4^2\) gives the points \((0, \pm 4)\), and
\((8, 0)\) has order \(2\)---so on it the gate can be examined both on
inputs satisfying the hypotheses and on inputs violating them. For each
input pair considered below, a script determines every output pair
\((x_r, y_r)\) that \emph{some} assignment of the five auxiliary cells in
\(\F_{11}\) makes the twelve identities accept---the search over
\(\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \delta\) factored by which identities mention
each: \(\beta\) only (C4), \(\gamma\) only (C5), \(\alpha\) and
\(\delta\) only (C2) and (C6)---so soundness is checked against all
provers, not only the honest one. On the nine points \(\mathcal{O}\),
\((4, \pm 5)\), \((5, \pm 3)\), \((6, \pm 1)\), \((10, \pm 2)\) with
\(x, y \neq 0\), all \(81\) ordered pairs---eight doublings, eight
inverse pairs, seventeen involving \(\mathcal{O}\)---admit exactly one
output, the encoding of the true sum. Three of them, worked: for
\([2](5, 3)\) the honest cells are
\(\lambda = 3 \cdot 5^2 \cdot 6^{-1} = 9 \cdot 2 = 7\) and
\(\delta = 6^{-1} = 2\), giving
\((x_r, y_r) = (49 - 10,\ 7(5 - 6) - 3) = (6, 1)\), the value
of~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-double}; for the inverse pair
\((5, 3) + (5, 8)\) the prover supplies the same \(\lambda = 7\) but
\(\delta = \operatorname{inv}_0(11) = 0\), and (C6a)--(C6b) force
\((0, 0)\); for \(\mathcal{O} + (5, 3)\) the cells are
\(\alpha = 5^{-1} = 9\), \(\lambda = 3 \cdot 9 = 5\), \(\beta = 0\), and
(C4a)--(C4b) force \((5, 3)\). Off the admissible set the gate fails as
the proof predicts. With \(P = (0, 4)\) and \(Q = (5, 3)\) the identities
admit only the output \((5, 3)\)---the coordinate \(x_p = 0\) is read as
\(\mathcal{O}\)---whereas \(P + Q = (10, 9)\)
(\cref{ellipticcurves:exa-toy}); the pair \((0, 4) + (0, 7)\) admits no
output at all, (C4b) demanding \(y_r = 7\) and (C5b) \(y_r = 4\); and
\((8, 0) + (8, 0)\) admits none either, since (C2) reduces to
\(3 \cdot 8^2 = 0\), false in \(\F_{11}\). On Pallas none of the three
inputs exists, and the gate is complete.
\end{example}

\subsection{Identity, inverses, commutativity, and associativity}
\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-axioms}

\begin{theorem}[Group law]\label{ellipticcurves:thm-grouplaw}
Let \(E\) be an elliptic curve over a field \(K\). The set \(E(K)\)
with the chord-and-tangent operation \(+\) of
\cref{ellipticcurves:def-grouplaw-geom} is an abelian group with
identity \(\mathcal{O}\).
\end{theorem}

Closure is \cref{ellipticcurves:prop-closure}. The next proposition
establishes every remaining axiom except associativity.

\begin{proposition}[Identity, inverses, commutativity]
\label{ellipticcurves:prop-id-inv}
The operation \(+\) on \(E(K)\) has the following properties.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item The point \(\mathcal{O}\) is a two-sided identity:
    \(P + \mathcal{O} = \mathcal{O} + P = P\) for all \(P\).
  \item Every \(P\) has the two-sided inverse \(-P\) of
    \cref{ellipticcurves:def-negation}: \(P + (-P) = \mathcal{O}\).
  \item The operation is commutative: \(P + Q = Q + P\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Item (1) is the first clause of \cref{ellipticcurves:def-grouplaw-geom}.
For item (2), let \(P = (x, y)\) be affine. If \(y \neq 0\), the line
through \(P\) and \(-P = (x, -y)\) is vertical, so its third point is
\(\mathcal{O}\) and \(P + (-P) = \mathcal{O}\) by the vertical-line case;
if \(y = 0\), then \(P = -P\) and \([2]P = \mathcal{O}\) directly by the
vertical-tangent case; and
\(\mathcal{O} + (-\mathcal{O}) = \mathcal{O} + \mathcal{O} =
\mathcal{O}\). For item (3), the chord through \(P\) and \(Q\) does not
depend on the order in which the two points are named, and neither does
the tangent in the doubling case; algebraically, the
formulas~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-add} are symmetric under the swap
\((x_1, y_1) \leftrightarrow (x_2, y_2)\)---the slope \(\lambda\) is
unchanged, and \(x_3 = \lambda^2 - x_1 - x_2\) is symmetric in
\(x_1, x_2\).
\end{proof}

The remaining axiom is \emph{associativity},
\((P + Q) + R = P + (Q + R)\). A direct verification through the explicit
formulas divides into many coordinate cases, each a lengthy
rational-function identity; we sketch the conceptual proof instead.

\begin{proof}[Proof of associativity (sketch)]
The proof uses the classical \emph{Cayley--Bacharach theorem} for plane
cubics: if two cubics with no common component meet in nine points counted
with intersection multiplicity, then any third cubic passing through eight
of them with the required multiplicities also passes through the ninth. To
prove \((P + Q) + R = P + (Q + R)\), one writes both sides via the
chord-and-tangent construction and arranges the relevant intersections among
the curve \(E\) and two degenerate cubics, each a product of three suitably
chosen lines. Cayley--Bacharach forces the two candidate sums to coincide;
its intersection-multiplicity formulation includes tangencies and coincident
points. Replacing ``reflect across the \(x\)-axis'' by ``the three
points of any line sum to \(\mathcal{O}\)'' makes the bookkeeping
uniform.

A complete, case-free treatment identifies \(E(K)\) with the
degree-zero part \(\mathrm{Pic}^0(E)\) of the divisor class group of
the curve via the map \(P \mapsto [P] - [\mathcal{O}]\), a bijection
that intertwines the two operations; the group law on
\(\mathrm{Pic}^0(E)\) is manifestly associative, because it is
inherited from addition of divisors modulo principal divisors. Either
route yields a complete proof; both lie beyond the elementary scope of
this volume, so we take associativity as established.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[{The notation \([n]P\)}]\label{ellipticcurves:rem-notation}
For \(n \in \Z\) and \(P \in E(K)\), we write \([n]P\) for the
\(n\)-fold sum \(P + \cdots + P\), with \([0]P = \mathcal{O}\) and
\([-n]P = -([n]P)\). This makes the abelian group \(E(K)\) a
\(\Z\)-module: integers act on points. The map \(n \mapsto [n]P\) is
the \emph{scalar multiplication} of
\cref{ellipticcurves:def-dbladd}---not any coordinate-wise product.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Projective and Jacobian coordinates}
\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-coords}

The affine formulas~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-add}
and~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-double} each require a field
\emph{inversion}, by \(x_2 - x_1\) or \(2y_1\), which in a large prime
field costs roughly as much as dozens of multiplications, whether computed
by the extended Euclidean algorithm (\cref{finitefields:con:inv-euclid})
or as \(z^{p-2}\) by Fermat (\cref{finitefields:con:inv-fermat}). Over a
long chain of group operations, as in scalar multiplication, it is cheaper
to carry points in a redundant coordinate system whose group law uses only
additions, subtractions, and multiplications, and to invert once at the
end.

\begin{remark}[Inversion-free coordinate systems]
\label{ellipticcurves:rem-coords}
Two redundant systems serve this purpose. In \emph{(homogeneous)
projective coordinates}, a triple \([X : Y : Z]\) with \(Z \neq 0\)
represents the affine point \((X/Z, Y/Z)\) on the projective
curve~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-projective}, with
\(\mathcal{O} = [0 : 1 : 0]\). In \emph{Jacobian coordinates}, a triple
\((X : Y : Z)\) with \(Z \neq 0\) represents \((X/Z^2, Y/Z^3)\), under
the equivalence
\((X, Y, Z) \sim (\lambda^2 X, \lambda^3 Y, \lambda Z)\); the curve
equation becomes \(Y^2 = X^3 + a X Z^4 + b Z^6\). In either system
addition and doubling need no divisions, at the cost of more
multiplications per operation; the final recovery of affine coordinates
computes \(Z^{-1}\) once, then the needed powers of it by multiplication.
Jacobian coordinates usually yield the faster doubling and are the
standard choice in cryptographic libraries; the \texttt{pasta\_curves}
crate used by Halo~2 carries its curve points in them. For curves
\(y^2 = x^3 + b\) with \(a = 0\), the term \(a Z^4\) in the Jacobian
doubling formulas vanishes, which lowers the cost of doubling; this is
one reason the Pasta curves take \(a = 0\).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Scalar multiplication and double-and-add}
\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-dbladd}

\emph{Scalar multiplication} computes, for a point \(P\) and an integer
\(n \geq 0\),
\[
  [n]P = \underbrace{P + P + \cdots + P}_{n\ \text{times}} .
\]
Computing this by \(n - 1\) successive additions is infeasible when
\(n\) has hundreds of bits. Double-and-add uses the binary expansion of
\(n\) instead; it is the additive form of square-and-multiply
(\cref{finitefields:con:sqmul}), anticipated in
\cref{finitefields:rem:dbladd}.

\begin{definition}[Double-and-add]\label{ellipticcurves:def-dbladd}
If \(n=0\), return \(\mathcal O\). Otherwise write
\(n = \sum_{i=0}^{k-1} n_i 2^i\) with digits
\(n_i \in \{0, 1\}\) and top digit \(n_{k-1} = 1\). The
\emph{left-to-right double-and-add} algorithm computes \([n]P\) as
follows: set \(R \leftarrow P\); for \(i = k-2\) down to \(0\), set
\(R \leftarrow [2]R\), and if \(n_i = 1\) set
\(R \leftarrow R + P\); return \(R\).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{ellipticcurves:prop-dbladd}
For \(n\geq1\), double-and-add returns \([n]P\) using exactly \(k - 1\)
doublings and at most \(k - 1\) additions, where
\(k = \floor{\log_2 n} + 1\) is the bit length of \(n\). For \(n=0\),
it returns \(\mathcal O\) without a group operation.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The case \(n=0\) is immediate. Let \(n\geq1\), and let \(R_j\) denote the
value of \(R\) after bits \(n_{k-1}, \ldots, n_j\) have been incorporated.
We claim \(R_j = [\floor{n/2^j}]P\), by downward induction on \(j\). At
initialisation, \(R_{k-1}=P=[\floor{n/2^{k-1}}]P\) because the top bit is
\(1\).
Assuming \(R_{j+1} = [\floor{n/2^{j+1}}]P\), the next iteration
computes
\[
  [2]R_{j+1} + [n_j]P
  = [\,2\floor{n/2^{j+1}} + n_j\,]P
  = [\floor{n/2^{j}}]P ,
\]
where the last step is the digit identity
\(2\floor{n/2^{j+1}} + n_j = \floor{n/2^j}\): stripping the last bit of
\(\floor{n/2^j}\) and appending it again recovers the number. At \(j = 0\)
the invariant reads \(R_0 = [n]P\), which is what the algorithm
returns. For the counts, the loop has exactly \(k-1\) iterations, each
performing one doubling and at most one addition.
\end{proof}

Scalar multiplication by an \(n\)-bit scalar therefore costs \(O(n)\)
group operations---\(O(n)\) field operations up to constant factors, or
\(O(n \log^2 p)\) bit operations with schoolbook field arithmetic.
Elliptic-curve cryptography rests on the contrast between this
\emph{forward} map and the apparent hardness of inverting it
(\S\ref{ellipticcurves:subsec-ecdlp}).

\subsection{The group structure of
\texorpdfstring{\(E(\Fp)\)}{E(Fp)}}
\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-structure}

Let \(K = \Fp\) with \(p\) prime, \(p > 3\). Each of the \(p\) values
of \(x\) admits at most two values of \(y\)
(\cref{polynomials:thm:root-bound}, applied to \(y^2 - f(x)\) as a
polynomial in \(y\)), so with \(\mathcal{O}\) there are at most
\(2p + 1\) points, and by \cref{ellipticcurves:thm-grouplaw} the set
\(E(\Fp)\) is a \emph{finite abelian group}. Hasse's theorem bounds its
order.

\begin{theorem}[Hasse]\label{ellipticcurves:thm-hasse}
Let \(E\) be an elliptic curve over \(\Fp\). Write
\[
  \#E(\Fp) = p + 1 - t,
\]
which \emph{defines} the integer \(t\), called the \emph{trace of
Frobenius}. Then
\[
  \abs{t} \leq 2\sqrt{p} ;
\]
equivalently, \(\#E(\Fp)\) lies in the \emph{Hasse interval}
\([\,p + 1 - 2\sqrt{p},\; p + 1 + 2\sqrt{p}\,]\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof (sketch)]
The count has an exact character-sum expression. For fixed \(x\), the
number of \(y \in \Fp\) with \(y^2 = f(x)\) is
\(1 + \legendre{f(x)}{p}\) when \(f(x) \neq 0\)---two solutions for a
nonzero square, none for a non-square
(\cref{numbertheory:prop:half-qr})---and exactly \(1 = 1 +
\legendre{0}{p}\) when \(f(x) = 0\), using the Legendre symbol of
\cref{numbertheory:def:legendre} with the convention
\(\legendre{0}{p} = 0\). Summing over \(x\),
\[
  \#E^{\mathrm{aff}}(\Fp)
  = \sum_{x \in \Fp}\left(1 + \legendre{x^3 + ax + b}{p}\right)
  = p + \sum_{x \in \Fp}\legendre{x^3 + ax + b}{p},
\]
so \(t = -\sum_{x}\legendre{x^3 + ax + b}{p}\). Heuristically, as \(x\)
ranges over \(\Fp\) the value \(f(x)\) is a nonzero square about half
the time (two points), a non-square about half the time (none), and
zero occasionally (one point); the \(p\) summands \(\pm 1\) behave like
a random walk, and Hasse's bound \(\abs{t} \le 2\sqrt{p}\) is exactly
square-root cancellation in this character sum.

The conceptual proof realises \(t\) as the trace of the \(p\)-power
\emph{Frobenius endomorphism} \(\pi \colon (x, y) \mapsto (x^p, y^p)\)
of the curve---the curve-level counterpart of the field automorphism of
\cref{finitefields:def:frobenius}. One shows that \(\pi\) satisfies the
characteristic equation \(\pi^2 - [t]\pi + [p] = 0\) in the
endomorphism ring of \(E\), and that the associated quadratic form (the
degree) is positive definite; the discriminant condition
\(t^2 - 4p \leq 0\) for the complex roots of the characteristic
polynomial then yields \(\abs{t} \leq 2\sqrt{p}\). The full argument
requires the theory of isogenies and the Weil pairing, beyond the
present scope.
\end{proof}

By Hasse's theorem the relative deviation of the order from \(p\) is of
order \(p^{-1/2}\), so the bound guarantees without a brute-force count
that the group has about \(p\) elements, about \(255\) bits for the Pasta
curves. The bound also underlies the point-counting algorithms
(Schoof--Elkies--Atkin) that compute the \emph{exact} order in polynomial
time, by which curve designers certify their group orders.

Cryptographic use requires \(E(\Fp)\) to contain a large subgroup of
\emph{prime} order. The general structure theory of \(E(\Fp)\) is not
needed in this series: the Pasta curves are designed so that
\(\#E(\Fp)\) is itself prime, and a group of prime order is cyclic with no
proper nontrivial subgroups (\cref{groups:cor-prime-cyclic}).

\subsection{Torsion, the cofactor, and prime-order subgroups}
\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-torsion}

\begin{definition}[Order of a point and torsion]
\label{ellipticcurves:def-torsion}
The \emph{order} of a point \(P \in E(\Fp)\) is its order as a group
element (\cref{groups:def-order-elt}): the least integer \(m \geq 1\)
with \([m]P = \mathcal{O}\). It exists and divides \(\#E(\Fp)\), by
Lagrange's theorem (\cref{groups:thm-lagrange} via
\cref{groups:cor-order-elt-divides}). For an integer \(m \geq 1\), the
\emph{\(m\)-torsion subgroup} is
\[
  E[m] = \{\, P \in E(\overline{\Fp}) : [m]P = \mathcal{O} \,\},
\]
the points over the algebraic closure killed by \(m\); its rational
part is \(E(\Fp)[m] = E[m] \cap E(\Fp)\).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Cofactor]\label{ellipticcurves:def-cofactor}
Suppose \(\#E(\Fp) = h \cdot r\), where \(r\) is the (large) prime to
be used and \(h\) is the remaining factor. The integer \(h\) is the
\emph{cofactor}, and \(r\) is the order of the \emph{prime-order
subgroup}: a point \(P\) of order \(r\) generates a cyclic subgroup
\(\langle P \rangle \cong \Z/r\Z\), and cryptographic protocols operate
in this subgroup.
\end{definition}

A nontrivial cofactor \(h > 1\) is undesirable: it admits
\emph{small-subgroup} points, of order dividing \(h\), which can leak
information or break protocol assumptions---the small-subgroup attacks
of \cref{numbertheory:rem:cofactor}. Implementations therefore either
\emph{clear the cofactor}, multiplying incoming points by \(h\), or use
prime-order curves with \(h = 1\). The Pasta curves have cofactor
\(1\): the orders \(\#E_p(\Fp) = q\) and \(\#E_q(\Fq) = p\) are
themselves prime, so \(E_p(\Fp) \cong \Z/q\Z\) and
\(E_q(\Fq) \cong \Z/p\Z\), and no cofactor clearing is needed.

\begin{proposition}[Two-torsion]\label{ellipticcurves:prop-twotorsion}
The affine points of order \(2\) in \(E(\Fp)\) are exactly the points
\((x_0, 0)\) with \(f(x_0) = x_0^3 + a x_0 + b = 0\). Hence \(E(\Fp)\)
has \(0\), \(1\), or \(3\) points of order \(2\) according as \(f\) has
\(0\), \(1\), or \(3\) roots in \(\Fp\); and \(\#E(\Fp)\) is even if
and only if \(f\) has a root in \(\Fp\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A point \(P = (x, y)\) has order \(2\) iff \(P = -P\) and
\(P \neq \mathcal{O}\), i.e.\ \(y = -y\), i.e.\ \(y = 0\) (since
\(\charf(\Fp) \neq 2\)); the curve equation then forces \(f(x) = 0\).
Conversely each root \(x_0\) of \(f\) in \(\Fp\) gives the order-\(2\)
point \((x_0, 0)\).

Next, the number \(N\) of roots of \(f\) in \(\Fp\) can only be \(0\),
\(1\), or \(3\): two roots force a third. Suppose \(f\) has distinct
roots \(r_1 \neq r_2\) in \(\Fp\). The factor theorem
(\cref{polynomials:thm:factor}) gives \(f = (x - r_1)h\); evaluating
at \(r_2\) yields \(0 = (r_2 - r_1)\,h(r_2)\), so \(h(r_2) = 0\), and
a second application factors the monic cubic completely,
\(f = (x - r_1)(x - r_2)(x - r_3)\) with \(r_3 \in \Fp\)---explicitly
\(r_3 = -r_1 - r_2\) by Vieta, the depressed cubic having no \(x^2\)
term. Nonsingularity says \(f\) has no repeated root even over
\(\overline{\Fp}\) (\cref{ellipticcurves:prop-smooth}(2)), so
\(r_3 \notin \{r_1, r_2\}\), and \(f\) has exactly three roots
(\cref{polynomials:thm:root-bound} caps the count). Hence
\(N \in \{0, 1, 3\}\), matching the trichotomy of the statement.
Together with \(\mathcal{O}\) the order-\(2\) points form the
rational \(2\)-torsion subgroup \(E(\Fp)[2]\), which is accordingly
trivial, \(\Z/2\Z\), or \(\Z/2\Z \times \Z/2\Z\).

For the parity claim, consider negation \(P \mapsto -P\), an
\emph{involution}---a self-inverse map---of the finite set
\(E(\Fp)\). Its fixed points are exactly
\(\mathcal{O}\) and the points \((x_0, 0)\); every other point pairs
off with its distinct negative into a \(2\)-element orbit. Counting the
set by orbits,
\[
  \#E(\Fp) \equiv 1 + N \pmod{2},
\]
where \(N \in \{0, 1, 3\}\) is the number of roots of \(f\) in \(\Fp\).
Hence \(\#E(\Fp)\) is even iff \(N\) is odd, i.e.\ iff \(N \geq 1\),
i.e.\ iff \(f\) has a root in \(\Fp\). (In general, an involution of a
finite set determines the cardinality of the set modulo \(2\) from its
fixed points alone.)
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[No two-torsion on the Pasta curves]
\label{ellipticcurves:rem-pasta-odd}
For \(y^2 = x^3 + 5\) over \(\Fp\), the curve has a point of order
\(2\) iff \(x^3 + 5 = 0\) has a solution, i.e.\ iff \(-5\) is a cube in
\(\Fp\). For the Pasta primes the order \(\#E_p(\Fp) = q\) is odd (it
is prime), so there are \emph{no} rational \(2\)-torsion points;
consistently, \(-5\) is not a cube in \(\Fp\).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Base fields, scalar fields, and the Pasta cycle}
\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-basescalar}

An elliptic-curve computation involves two prime fields. Every operation
of the group law on \(E(\Fp)\)---the slope
\(\lambda = (y_2 - y_1)(x_2 - x_1)^{-1}\), the squarings, and the
inversions needed to add and to double---is arithmetic in \(\Fp\), the
\emph{base field} (or coordinate field), performed with the prime-field
algorithms of \cref{finitefields:sub:inversion}. Keys and signatures
reside in the subgroup of large prime order \(r\)
(\cref{ellipticcurves:def-cofactor}), and a scalar multiplication
\(P \mapsto [s]P\) on a point of order \(r\) depends only on
\(s \bmod r\), since \([r]P = \mathcal{O}\)
(\cref{groups:prop-order-divides}); scalars are therefore residues modulo
\(r\), and since \(r\) is prime, they form a \emph{field}
(\cref{ringsfields:cor-fpfield}).

\begin{definition}[Base field and scalar field]
\label{ellipticcurves:def-basescalar}
For an elliptic curve used in cryptography, with a prime-order subgroup
of order \(r\):
\begin{itemize}
  \item the \emph{base field} \(\Fp\) is the field over which the curve
    is defined and in which point coordinates lie;
  \item the \emph{scalar field} \(\Fr = \Z/r\Z\) is the field of
    scalars by which one multiplies points, i.e.\ the integers modulo
    the subgroup order \(r\).
\end{itemize}
Both are prime fields, but in general \(p \neq r\).
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Two fields at once, and the Pasta cycle]
\label{ellipticcurves:rem-pasta}
Scalar arithmetic runs in \(\Fr\) and coordinate arithmetic in \(\Fp\).
Halo~2's Pallas and Vesta curves (the ``Pasta'' pair) form a
\emph{cycle of curves}: the base field of one is the scalar field of
the other and vice versa,
\[
  \#E_p(\Fp) = q, \qquad \#E_q(\Fq) = p,
\]
so the scalar field of Pallas is \(\Fq\), the base field of Vesta, and
symmetrically (\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#pallasandvesta}{\S5.4.9.6}
of the Zcash protocol
specification). This lets a proof system express the verifier of one
curve's arithmetic \emph{natively} in the field of the other, which
enables the recursive proof composition of Halo~2. Both
primes are chosen \(\equiv 1 \pmod{2^{32}}\), so by
\cref{finitefields:cor:rootsofunity} each field contains a
multiplicative subgroup of order \(2^{32}\), furnishing the high-order
roots of unity that the number-theoretic transform of
\cref{sec:rootsofunity} needs for fast polynomial multiplication.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The elliptic-curve discrete logarithm problem}
\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-ecdlp}

The discrete logarithm problem of \cref{numbertheory:def:dlp}, stated
there for an abstract cyclic group, reads as follows for elliptic curves
in additive notation.

\begin{definition}[ECDLP]\label{ellipticcurves:def-ecdlp}
Let \(P \in E(\Fp)\) be a point of large prime order \(r\), generating
\(G = \langle P \rangle\). The \emph{elliptic-curve discrete logarithm
problem} (ECDLP) is: given \(P\) and a point \(Q \in G\), find the
unique integer \(n \in \{0, 1, \ldots, r - 1\}\) with \(Q = [n]P\). One
writes \(n = \log_P Q\).
\end{definition}

The map \(n \mapsto [n]P\) is a group isomorphism
\(\Z/r\Z \to G\) (\cref{groups:thm-cyclic-classification}), so a
discrete logarithm always exists and is unique; the difficulty of the
problem is purely \emph{computational}. The forward map is fast by
double-and-add (\cref{ellipticcurves:prop-dbladd}); on a well-chosen
curve the best known algorithms to invert it are the two generic attacks,
which transfer verbatim from \cref{numbertheory:rem:dlp-generic} to the
additive setting.

\begin{proposition}[Square-root attacks]
\label{ellipticcurves:prop-ecdlp-sqrt}
Baby-step giant-step solves the ECDLP in a group of prime order \(r\)
in \(O(\sqrt{r})\) group operations and \(O(\sqrt{r})\) space.
Pollard's rho method matches the \(O(\sqrt r)\) bound in expectation
with only \(O(1)\) space, under a modelling assumption on its
pseudo-random walk that is stated explicitly in the proof and is
unproved for every concrete walk.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For baby-step giant-step, set \(m = \ceil{\sqrt{r}}\) and write the
unknown logarithm as \(n = im + j\) with \(0 \leq i, j < m\); since
\(m^2 \geq r\), every \(n \in \{0, \ldots, r-1\}\) has such a
decomposition. Tabulate the \emph{baby steps} \([j]P\) for
\(j = 0, \ldots, m - 1\) (a table of \(m\) points, built with \(m - 1\)
additions), compute \(S = [m]P\) once, and walk the \emph{giant steps}
\(Q - [i]S\) for \(i = 0, 1, 2, \ldots\), testing each against the
table. At \(i = \floor{n/m}\) the walk hits
\(Q - [im]P = [n - im]P = [j]P\), a table entry; the collision reveals
\(i\) and \(j\), hence \(n = im + j\). Both the table and the walk cost
\(O(m) = O(\sqrt{r})\) group operations, and the table holds
\(O(\sqrt{r})\) points.

Pollard's rho eliminates the table, at the price of an explicitly
heuristic analysis. The algorithm takes a pseudo-random walk on \(G\)
whose every position is maintained in the form \([a]P + [b]Q\) with
known coefficients \(a, b\), started from such a combination with
random coefficients; the walk's next step depends only on the current
point, so once the walk revisits a point it cycles from then on. A repeat
is therefore detected with \(O(1)\) memory: run a second copy of the
walk at double speed, and the two copies meet at a common point inside
the cycle within a constant factor of the first repeat time.
\emph{Modelling assumption}: the successive positions of the walk
behave like independent uniform samples from \(G\). No proof of this
is known for any concrete walk---the assumption is what
``well-designed'' means, and experiment supports it---and the rest of
the argument is conditional on it. Under the assumption, the first
\(k\) positions are pairwise distinct with probability at most
\(e^{-k(k-1)/(2r)}\) by the birthday bound (a forward reference:
\cref{probability:prop-birthday} belongs to the closing section), so
the number \(T\) of steps to the first repeat
satisfies \(\Pr[T > k] \le e^{-k(k-1)/(2r)}\). The tail-sum formula
(\cref{probability:lem-tailsum}) converts the tail bound into an
expectation bound: with \(m = \ceil{\sqrt r}\) and \(r \ge 4\),
\[
  \E[T] = \sum_{k \ge 1} \Pr[T \ge k]
  \le m \sum_{j \ge 0} \Pr[T > jm]
  \le m \Bigl(1 + \sum_{j \ge 1} e^{-j/4}\Bigr)
  = O(\sqrt{r}) ,
\]
where the middle step groups the indices \(k\) into blocks of length
\(m\), bounding each block by its first term, and the last uses
\(jm(jm-1) \ge j^2\, m(m-1) \ge j^2 (r - \sqrt r) \ge j^2 r/2\) for
\(j \ge 1\), so that \(\Pr[T > jm] \le e^{-j^2/4} \le e^{-j/4}\), a
convergent geometric tail. A repeat presents one group element with
two representations, \([a]P + [b]Q = [a']P + [b']Q\). If
\(b' \not\equiv b \pmod r\), this rearranges to
\([a - a']P = [b' - b]Q = [(b' - b)\,n]P\), whence
\[
  n \equiv (a - a')(b' - b)^{-1} \pmod{r},
\]
the inverse existing because \(r\) is prime. If instead
\(b' \equiv b\), then \([a]P = [a']P\) forces \(a \equiv a'\): the two
representations coincide, the collision reveals nothing, and the walk
is restarted with fresh random starting coefficients; that such
degenerate collisions are rare is a further part of the heuristic,
borne out in practice. Both algorithms run in time exponential in the
bit length \(\log_2 r\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Best known attacks on well-chosen curves]
\label{ellipticcurves:rem-ecdlp-hardness}
For well-chosen curves, no unrestricted classical attack faster than
\(O(\sqrt{r})\) is known; this is not an unconditional lower bound for
all classical algorithms. In the classical generic-group model, however,
Shoup's matching lower bound is proved
(\cref{numbertheory:rem:dlp-generic}). For
\(r \approx 2^{254}\)---the \(255\)-bit Pasta orders---this
is about \(2^{127}\) operations, infeasible. Curves with special
structure can be weaker, and the known weak classes are avoided by
design. \emph{Anomalous} curves, those with \(\#E(\Fp) = p\), admit a
polynomial-time attack through the formal group. The MOV/Frey--Rück
attack transfers the ECDLP into a finite-field discrete logarithm
when the \emph{embedding degree}---the least \(k \geq 1\) with
\(r \mid p^k - 1\), i.e.\ the degree (\cref{linalg:exa-more};
the dimension of the extension over its base) of the smallest extension
of \(\Fp\) whose multiplicative group contains a subgroup of order
\(r\)---is small, where index calculus applies. Cryptographically
secure curves, the Pasta curves included, are chosen with a large prime
subgroup order, a large embedding degree, and \(\#E(\Fp) \neq p\),
which excludes every known subexponential attack.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The index-calculus contrast]
\label{ellipticcurves:rem-index-calculus}
In \(\Fp^\times\) the index-calculus method solves the discrete logarithm
in subexponential time \(L_p[1/3]\)---shorthand for
\(2^{\Theta(\lambda^{1/3} (\log \lambda)^{2/3})}\) operations in the
bit length \(\lambda = \log_2 p\), a notation defined precisely in
\cref{probability:def-exp-fn} of the closing section---exploiting the
integer arithmetic behind the residues
(\cref{numbertheory:rem:dlp-generic}); those groups must therefore be
large. On a well-chosen elliptic curve no index calculus is
known---the points offer no analogue of ``factoring into small
primes''---so the generic \(O(\sqrt{r})\) bound stands, and much
smaller groups suffice: about \(256\) bits for \(128\)-bit security,
versus thousands of bits for \(\Fp^\times\). Smaller groups mean
faster arithmetic and shorter keys, which is why elliptic curves dominate
modern protocols, including Halo~2.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The \texorpdfstring{\(j\)}{j}-invariant and the GLV
endomorphism}
\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-glv}

The \(j\)-invariant classifies curves up to isomorphism; when it vanishes,
as it does for the Pasta curves by design, the curve has an additional
endomorphism.

\begin{definition}[\(j\)-invariant]\label{ellipticcurves:def-jinvariant}
The \emph{\(j\)-invariant} of the curve \(y^2 = x^3 + ax + b\) (with
\(4a^3 + 27b^2 \neq 0\)) is
\[
  j = 1728\,\frac{4a^3}{4a^3 + 27b^2} \in K ,
\]
where \(1728 = 12^3\) is a normalising constant of historical origin.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{ellipticcurves:prop-jinvariant}
Two elliptic curves over \(\overline{K}\) are isomorphic over
\(\overline{K}\) if and only if they have the same \(j\)-invariant.
The admissible coordinate changes preserving short Weierstrass form are
