\((x, y) \mapsto (u^2 x, u^3 y)\) for \(u \in \overline{K}^\times\),
under which \((a, b) \mapsto (u^{4} a, u^{6} b)\); the combination
\(j\) is invariant.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof (sketch)]
A direct computation shows that the only isomorphisms between short
Weierstrass curves fixing \(\mathcal{O}\) are the scalings
\((x, y) \mapsto (u^2 x, u^3 y)\). Substituting \(x = u^{-2} x'\),
\(y = u^{-3} y'\) into \(y^2 = x^3 + ax + b\) and clearing
\(u^6\) gives \(y'^2 = x'^3 + u^4 a\, x' + u^6 b\), which is the stated
action on coefficients. Under it,
numerator and denominator of \(4a^3/(4a^3 + 27b^2)\) both scale by
\(u^{12}\), so \(j\) is unchanged. Conversely, given two curves with
equal \(j\)-invariants one solves for a suitable \(u\) transforming one
coefficient pair into the other, treating separately the cases \(j = 0\)
(both \(a\) vanish) and \(j = 1728\) (both \(b\) vanish).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The Pasta curves have \(j = 0\)]
\label{ellipticcurves:rem-pasta-j}
For \(y^2 = x^3 + b\) one has \(a = 0\), so \(j = 0\); both Pasta
curves have \(j = 0\). Curves with \(j = 0\) admit an additional
automorphism of order \(3\), which underlies the efficient endomorphism
described next. (In the other exceptional case, \(b = 0\) gives
\(j = 1728\) and an order-\(4\) automorphism.) The Pasta design chooses
\(j = 0\) to make the Gallant--Lambert--Vanstone (GLV) speedup available,
which roughly halves the doubling count in scalar multiplication.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The GLV endomorphism for \(y^2 = x^3 + b\)]
\label{ellipticcurves:prop-glv}
Suppose \(\Fp\) contains a primitive cube root of unity \(\omega\),
i.e.\ \(p \equiv 1 \pmod 3\)
(\cref{finitefields:cor:rootsofunity}), so
\(\omega \in \Fp\) satisfies \(\omega^2 + \omega + 1 = 0\). On
\(E : y^2 = x^3 + b\), the map
\[
  \phi(x, y) = (\omega x,\ y), \qquad \phi(\mathcal{O}) = \mathcal{O},
\]
is a group endomorphism of \(E(\Fp)\). Let \(G = \langle P \rangle\) be
a subgroup of prime order \(r\) with \(r \equiv 1 \pmod 3\) and
\(\phi(G) \subseteq G\). Then \(\phi\) acts on \(G\) as multiplication
by a scalar: there is \(\lambda \in \Z/r\Z\) with
\(\lambda^2 + \lambda + 1 \equiv 0 \pmod r\) and
\[
  \phi(Q) = [\lambda]\,Q \quad\text{for all } Q \in G .
\]
For the Pasta curves the hypothesis \(\phi(G) \subseteq G\) is
automatic, since \(E(\Fp)\) itself is the prime-order group \(G\); and
\(r \equiv 1 \pmod 3\) holds for both orders.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{The map lands on the curve.} If \(y^2 = x^3 + b\) then
\(y^2 = (\omega x)^3 + b\), because \(\omega^3 = 1\); so
\((\omega x, y) \in E(\Fp)\).

\emph{The map is a homomorphism.} We check
\(\phi(P_1 + P_2) = \phi(P_1) + \phi(P_2)\) against the case analysis
of \S\ref{ellipticcurves:subsec-formulas}. The \(\mathcal{O}\) cases
are immediate, and \(\phi(-P) = -\phi(P)\) since negation touches only
\(y\); in particular the inverse case
\(\phi(P) + \phi(-P) = \mathcal{O} = \phi(\mathcal{O})\) is respected,
and \(\phi\) preserves the case split, since it preserves equality of
\(x\)-coordinates. For a chord (\(x_1 \neq x_2\)), the slope
transforms as
\[
  \lambda' = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{\omega x_2 - \omega x_1}
  = \omega^{-1} \lambda = \omega^2 \lambda ,
\]
using \(\omega^{-1} = \omega^2\). Then,
by~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-add} and \(\omega^4 = \omega\),
\[
  x_3' = \lambda'^2 - \omega x_1 - \omega x_2
  = \omega^4 \lambda^2 - \omega x_1 - \omega x_2
  = \omega\,(\lambda^2 - x_1 - x_2) = \omega x_3 ,
\]
\[
  y_3' = \lambda'(\omega x_1 - x_3')
       - y_1
  = \omega^2 \lambda \cdot \omega (x_1 - x_3) - y_1
  = \lambda (x_1 - x_3) - y_1 = y_3 ,
\]
so \(\phi(P_1) + \phi(P_2) = (\omega x_3, y_3) = \phi(P_1 + P_2)\). The
tangent case is identical, with
\(\lambda' = 3(\omega x_1)^2 / (2 y_1) = \omega^2 \lambda\)
in~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-double}.

\emph{The relation \(\phi^2 + \phi + \id = 0\).} Let \(P = (x, y)\)
be affine; we show \(P + \phi(P) + \phi^2(P) = \mathcal{O}\) directly
from the formulas of \S\ref{ellipticcurves:subsec-formulas}.

First let \(x \neq 0\). The points \(P = (x, y)\),
\(\phi(P) = (\omega x, y)\), \(\phi^2(P) = (\omega^2 x, y)\) share
their \(y\)-coordinate and have pairwise distinct \(x\)-coordinates,
since \(1, \omega, \omega^2\) are distinct and \(x \neq 0\). The chord
formula~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-add} applies to \(P + \phi(P)\), with
slope \(\lambda = (y - y)/(\omega x - x) = 0\):
\[
  x_3 = 0 - x - \omega x = -(1 + \omega)\,x = \omega^2 x, \qquad
  y_3 = 0 \cdot (x - x_3) - y = -y ,
\]
using \(1 + \omega + \omega^2 = 0\). Hence
\(P + \phi(P) = (\omega^2 x, -y) = -\phi^2(P)\)
(\cref{ellipticcurves:def-negation}), and
\(P + \phi(P) + \phi^2(P) = \mathcal{O}\).

If instead \(x = 0\), the three points coincide:
\(\phi(P) = \phi^2(P) = P\). Here \(y \neq 0\), since \(x = y = 0\) on
the curve would force \(b = 0\), which nonsingularity
\(27b^2 \neq 0\) forbids; so the doubling
formula~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-double} applies, with \(a = 0\):
\[
  \lambda = \frac{3 \cdot 0^2 + 0}{2y} = 0, \qquad
  x_3 = 0 - 2 \cdot 0 = 0, \qquad
  y_3 = 0 \cdot (0 - 0) - y = -y ,
\]
so \([2]P = (0, -y) = -P\), hence
\(P + \phi(P) + \phi^2(P) = [3]P = \mathcal{O}\) in this case too.
(The three points \(P\), \(\phi(P)\), \(\phi^2(P)\) are exactly the
intersections of the horizontal line through \(P\) with the curve, the
roots of \(X^3 = y^2 - b\) being \(x\), \(\omega x\), \(\omega^2 x\);
the computation above proves the rule ``three points of a line sum to
\(\mathcal{O}\)'' for this line, including the triple intersection at
\(x = 0\).)

With \(\phi(\mathcal{O}) = \mathcal{O}\) the identity holds on all of
\(E(\Fp)\); as an identity of endomorphisms,
\(\phi^2 + \phi + \id = 0\). Moreover \(\phi^3 = \id\), since
\(\omega^3 = 1\); and whenever \(\#E(\Fp) > 3\)---as always at
cryptographic sizes, the Pasta curves included---\(\phi\) has order
exactly \(3\), because at most two affine points, \((0, \pm y_0)\)
with \(y_0^2 = b\), share the coordinate \(x = 0\), so some point of
\(E(\Fp)\) has \(x \neq 0\) and \(\phi\) moves it. The proviso is
necessary: \(y^2 = x^3 + 4\) over \(\F_7\) has only the three points
\(\mathcal{O}\), \((0, 2)\), \((0, 5)\), and there \(\phi = \id\).

\emph{Action on \(G\) as a scalar.} The subgroup
\(G \cong \Z/r\Z\) is cyclic with generator \(P\), and
\(\phi(G) \subseteq G\) by hypothesis, so
\(\phi(P) = [\lambda]P\) for some \(\lambda \in \Z/r\Z\); then for any
\(Q = [m]P \in G\), the homomorphism property gives
\(\phi(Q) = [m]\phi(P) = [m\lambda]P = [\lambda]Q\). (This is the
statement \(\Hom(\Z/r\Z, \Z/r\Z) \cong \Z/r\Z\): an endomorphism of a
cyclic group is determined by, and equal to multiplication by, the
image of a generator.) Applying the endomorphism relation to \(P\),
\[
  [\lambda^2 + \lambda + 1]P = \mathcal{O}
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  \lambda^2 + \lambda + 1 \equiv 0 \pmod r ,
\]
since \(P\) has order \(r\). Consistently, this congruence is solvable
exactly when \(\Fr\) contains a primitive cube root of unity, i.e.\
when \(r \equiv 1 \pmod 3\) (\cref{finitefields:cor:rootsofunity}
again)---the hypothesis of the statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[How GLV halves the doubling count]
\label{ellipticcurves:rem-glv-speedup}
Computing \(\phi\) costs a single field multiplication
\(x \mapsto \omega x\), negligible against a group operation. A scalar
\(n \in \Z/r\Z\) is decomposed as
\[
  [n]P = [n_1]P + [n_2]\,\phi(P), \qquad
  n \equiv n_1 + n_2 \lambda \pmod r ,
\]
where \(n_1, n_2\) can each be taken about \(\tfrac{1}{2}\log_2 r\)
bits long; such a pair is found by lattice
reduction in the lattice of pairs \((c_1, c_2)\) with
\(c_1 + c_2 \lambda \equiv 0 \pmod r\). The two half-length scalar
multiplications are then run \emph{simultaneously}, sharing their
doublings (Straus--Shamir's trick): one double-and-add loop of half the
length, adding \(P\), \(\phi(P)\), or both according to the bits of
\(n_1\) and \(n_2\). The result costs about half the doublings of a
single full-length double-and-add; this saving is one reason the Pasta
curves take the form \(y^2 = x^3 + b\) over primes
\(p \equiv 1 \pmod 3\).
\end{remark}

\subsection{A worked toy example}\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-toy}

\begin{example}[The curve \(y^2 = x^3 + 5\) over \(\F_{11}\)]
\label{ellipticcurves:exa-toy}
Take \(p = 11\): then \(p > 3\), and
\(4 \cdot 0 + 27 \cdot 5^2 = 675 \equiv 4 \not\equiv 0 \pmod{11}\), so
the curve \(E : y^2 = x^3 + 5\) is nonsingular over \(\F_{11}\)
(\cref{ellipticcurves:def-curve}). Squaring the residues
\(0, 1, \ldots, 10\) yields the squares
\(\{0, 1, 3, 4, 5, 9\}\), so the nonzero quadratic residues are
\(\{1, 3, 4, 5, 9\}\). Tabulating \(f(x) = x^3 + 5 \bmod 11\) and
reading off the \(y\)-values:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|l}
  x & x^3 \bmod 11 & f(x) = x^3 + 5 & y \text{ with } y^2 = f(x) \\
  \hline
  0 & 0 & 5  & \pm 4 \ (4^2 = 16 \equiv 5) \\
  1 & 1 & 6  & \text{none} \ (6 \notin \mathrm{QR}) \\
  2 & 8 & 2  & \text{none} \\
  3 & 5 & 10 & \text{none} \\
  4 & 9 & 3  & \pm 5 \ (5^2 = 25 \equiv 3) \\
  5 & 4 & 9  & \pm 3 \\
  6 & 7 & 1  & \pm 1 \\
  7 & 2 & 7  & \text{none} \\
  8 & 6 & 0  & 0 \\
  9 & 3 & 8  & \text{none} \\
  10 & 10 & 4 & \pm 2 \\
\end{array}
\]
Counting: the five values \(x \in \{0, 4, 5, 6, 10\}\) contribute two
points each (\(10\) points), \(x = 8\) contributes the single point
\((8, 0)\), and \(\mathcal{O}\) is the remaining point:
\[
  \#E(\F_{11}) = 10 + 1 + 1 = 12 .
\]
Hasse's bound holds: \(p + 1 = 12\), so the trace is \(t = 0\), and
\(\abs{t} = 0 \leq 2\sqrt{11} \approx 6.63\). The point
\((8, 0)\) has order \(2\), consistent with
\cref{ellipticcurves:prop-twotorsion} and the even order \(12\); here
\(-5 \equiv 6\) \emph{is} a cube, namely \(8^3 = 512 \equiv 6\).

\smallskip
\emph{An addition.} Let \(P = (0, 4)\) and \(Q = (5, 3)\). Since
\(x_1 = 0 \neq 5 = x_2\), the chord
formula~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-add} applies:
\[
  \lambda = \frac{3 - 4}{5 - 0} = (-1) \cdot 5^{-1}
  = (-1) \cdot 9 = -9 \equiv 2 \pmod{11},
\]
using \(5^{-1} = 9\) (check: \(5 \cdot 9 = 45 \equiv 1\)). Then
\[
  x_3 = \lambda^2 - x_1 - x_2 = 4 - 0 - 5 = -1 \equiv 10, \qquad
  y_3 = \lambda(x_1 - x_3) - y_1 = 2\,(0 - 10) - 4 = -24 \equiv 9 ,
\]
so \(P + Q = (10, 9)\). The result is on the curve:
\(9^2 = 81 \equiv 4\) and \(10^3 + 5 = 1005 \equiv 4 \pmod{11}\), as
required.

\smallskip
\emph{A doubling.} Take \(P = (0, 4)\). With \(a = 0\),
formula~\eqref{ellipticcurves:eq-double} gives
\[
  \lambda = \frac{3 x_1^2 + a}{2 y_1} = \frac{0}{8} = 0, \qquad
  x_3 = \lambda^2 - 2 x_1 = 0, \qquad
  y_3 = \lambda(x_1 - x_3) - y_1 = -4 \equiv 7 ,
\]
so \([2](0, 4) = (0, 7) = -(0, 4)\). Hence
\([3](0, 4) = \mathcal{O}\): the point \((0, 4)\) has order \(3\).

\smallskip
\emph{The GLV endomorphism.} Since \(11 \equiv 2 \pmod 3\), the field
\(\F_{11}\) contains \emph{no} primitive cube root of unity
(\cref{finitefields:cor:rootsofunity} requires \(3 \mid p - 1\)), so
the GLV map is not available over \(\F_{11}\). The Pasta primes do
satisfy \(p \equiv q \equiv 1 \pmod 3\), so GLV applies there. For a
small GLV-friendly illustration take instead
\(p = 13 \equiv 1 \pmod 3\), where \(\omega = 3\) satisfies
\(3^2 + 3 + 1 = 13 \equiv 0 \pmod{13}\); then
\(\phi(x, y) = (3x, y)\) is a nontrivial endomorphism of
\(y^2 = x^3 + b\) over \(\F_{13}\).
\end{example}

\subsection{Pallas and Vesta assembled}\label{ellipticcurves:subsec-pasta}

\begin{example}[The Pasta curves]\label{ellipticcurves:exa-pasta}
For the running example \(y^2 = x^3 + 5\), with \(p\) and \(q\) the
\(255\)-bit primes of the section opening:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{Pallas}, over \(\Fp\): the order is
    \(\#E_p(\Fp) = q\), a prime, so the group is cyclic,
    \(E_p(\Fp) \cong \Z/q\Z\) (\cref{groups:cor-prime-cyclic}), with
    cofactor \(h = 1\) (\cref{ellipticcurves:def-cofactor}). The trace
    is \(t = p + 1 - q\), an \(87\)-bit (negative) integer, far inside
    the Hasse bound \(2\sqrt{p} \approx 2^{128}\) of
    \cref{ellipticcurves:thm-hasse}.
  \item \emph{Vesta}, over \(\Fq\): the order is
    \(\#E_q(\Fq) = p\), a prime, so
    \(E_q(\Fq) \cong \Z/p\Z\), again with cofactor \(1\).
  \item Both curves have \(j = 0\)
    (\cref{ellipticcurves:rem-pasta-j}), and both primes satisfy
    \(p \equiv q \equiv 1 \pmod 3\), so each curve admits the
    order-\(3\) endomorphism \(\phi(x, y) = (\omega x, y)\) of
    \cref{ellipticcurves:prop-glv} and supports the GLV speedup of
    \cref{ellipticcurves:rem-glv-speedup}.
  \item Both fields have two-adicity \(32\) in the sense of
    \cref{rootsofunity:rem-twoadic}: \(2^{32}\) divides \(p - 1\) and
    \(q - 1\), so each field carries the multiplicative subgroups of
    order \(2^{32}\) asserted in
    \cref{ellipticcurves:rem-pasta}, the evaluation domains of the
    fast polynomial arithmetic of \cref{sec:rootsofunity}.
  \item The cycle property \(\#E_p(\Fp) = q\) and
    \(\#E_q(\Fq) = p\) means the scalar field of each curve is the base
    field of the other (\cref{ellipticcurves:def-basescalar}): the base
    field of Pallas is \(\Fp\) and its scalar field is \(\Fq\),
    matching the base field of Vesta, and symmetrically. This lets
    recursive proof systems chain proofs across the two curves without
    expensive non-native field arithmetic
    (\cref{ellipticcurves:rem-pasta}). The cycle is deployed: the
    \texttt{pasta\_curves} crate defines the scalar field of Pallas to
    be \(\Fq\) and that of Vesta to be \(\Fp\), as specified in
    \href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#pallasandvesta}{\S5.4.9.6} of the Zcash
    protocol specification.
\end{itemize}
The discrete logarithm problem in \(E_p(\Fp)\) and \(E_q(\Fq)\) is
believed to require about \(2^{127}\) operations---\(\sqrt{r}\) for the
\(255\)-bit orders \(r \approx 2^{254}\)
(\cref{ellipticcurves:prop-ecdlp-sqrt})---with no faster attack known
(\cref{ellipticcurves:rem-ecdlp-hardness}), slightly below the \(128\)-bit
security level targeted by Halo~2 and Zcash Orchard.
\end{example}

Each Pasta curve is thus a cyclic group of prime \(255\)-bit order
(\cref{ellipticcurves:thm-hasse} and \cref{groups:cor-prime-cyclic}),
with a fast forward map (\cref{ellipticcurves:prop-dbladd}, accelerated
by \cref{ellipticcurves:rem-glv-speedup}) whose inversion, the ECDLP,
resists every known attack except the generic \(O(\sqrt{r})\) attacks of
\cref{ellipticcurves:prop-ecdlp-sqrt}. Later volumes build on this pair.

\section{Probability, asymptotics, and computation}\label{sec:probability}

This section fixes the language of security claims: probability,
asymptotic notation, and efficient computation. It proves the union and
birthday bounds, the properties of statistical distance, the bias bound
for modular reduction, Wald's identity, Chernoff bounds for the arrival
counts of exponential clocks, and the closure of negligible functions
under addition and multiplication by polynomials. The birthday bound
sets hash output lengths, and the bias bound justifies the sampling of
Orchard scalars; later volumes build their block-discovery and
extraction analyses on the waiting-time and clock results. A signature
scheme advertised as offering ``128-bit security'' cannot exclude
forgery: a forger that guesses the secret key, a finite string,
succeeds with positive probability, so the claim bounds the success
probability of every attacker within stated resources;
\S\ref{probability:sub-secparam} states what it asserts. Probability
spaces are finite until \S\ref{probability:sub-beyond}; finite spaces
suffice for keys, bounded-length messages, group elements, and the coins of
an algorithm with a fixed running-time bound. The prerequisites are finite sets,
sums, and elementary calculus: limits, the exponential function, and,
for the density-defined laws of \S\ref{probability:sub-beyond}, the
improper Riemann integral.

\subsection{Finite probability spaces and events}

\begin{definition}[Finite probability space]\label{probability:def-space}
A \emph{finite probability space} is a pair \((\Omega, \Pr)\) where
\(\Omega\) is a nonempty finite set, called the \emph{sample space},
and \(\Pr\colon \Omega \to [0,1]\) is a function, called the
\emph{probability mass function}, satisfying the normalisation
condition
\[
\sum_{\omega \in \Omega} \Pr[\omega] = 1 .
\]
An element \(\omega \in \Omega\) is an \emph{outcome} (or
\emph{elementary event}).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Event]\label{probability:def-event}
An \emph{event} is a subset \(A \subseteq \Omega\). Its
\emph{probability} is
\[
\Pr[A] = \sum_{\omega \in A} \Pr[\omega],
\]
with the convention \(\Pr[\varnothing] = 0\) (an empty sum). The event
\(A\) \emph{occurs} on outcome \(\omega\) if \(\omega \in A\).
\end{definition}

Because \(\Omega\) is finite, every subset is an event. On an
uncountable sample space the general theory specifies a family of
admissible events together with its probability assignment;
Remark~\ref{probability:rem-extension} marks the one place this series
requires measure theory. The following facts are used without
further citation.

\begin{proposition}[Basic axioms]\label{probability:prop-axioms}
For any finite probability space \((\Omega, \Pr)\) and events
\(A, B \subseteq \Omega\):
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(0 \le \Pr[A] \le 1\), \(\Pr[\varnothing] = 0\), and
  \(\Pr[\Omega] = 1\).
\item \emph{(Complement.)} \(\Pr[\Omega \setminus A] = 1 - \Pr[A]\).
\item \emph{(Monotonicity.)} If \(A \subseteq B\) then
  \(\Pr[A] \le \Pr[B]\).
\item \emph{(Inclusion--exclusion for two events.)}
  \(\Pr[A \cup B] = \Pr[A] + \Pr[B] - \Pr[A \cap B]\).
\item \emph{(Finite additivity.)} If \(A_1, \dots, A_n\) are pairwise
  disjoint then
  \(\Pr\!\left[\bigcup_{i=1}^n A_i\right] = \sum_{i=1}^n \Pr[A_i]\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every claim follows by manipulating the defining sums. For (1), each
summand \(\Pr[\omega]\) lies in \([0,1]\) and the total is \(1\) by
normalisation; an event's probability is a partial sum of nonnegative
terms, hence lies in \([0,1]\). For (2), the sets \(A\) and
\(\Omega \setminus A\) partition \(\Omega\), so
\(\Pr[A] + \Pr[\Omega \setminus A] = \sum_{\omega \in \Omega}
\Pr[\omega] = 1\). For (3), splitting the sum over \(B\) gives
\(\Pr[B] = \sum_{\omega \in A} \Pr[\omega] +
\sum_{\omega \in B \setminus A} \Pr[\omega] \ge \Pr[A]\), the second
term being a sum of nonnegative numbers. For (5), disjointness means
each \(\omega\) in the union lies in exactly one \(A_i\), so the
double sum \(\sum_i \sum_{\omega \in A_i} \Pr[\omega]\) counts each
\(\omega \in \bigcup_i A_i\) exactly once. For (4), write
\(A \cup B = A \sqcup (B \setminus A)\) (disjoint), so by (5)
\(\Pr[A \cup B] = \Pr[A] + \Pr[B \setminus A]\); also
\(B = (A \cap B) \sqcup (B \setminus A)\), giving
\(\Pr[B \setminus A] = \Pr[B] - \Pr[A \cap B]\). Substituting yields
the claim.
\end{proof}

The distribution used most often in cryptography is the uniform
distribution.

\begin{definition}[Uniform distribution]\label{probability:def-uniform}
Let \(S\) be a nonempty finite set. The \emph{uniform distribution} on
\(S\) is the probability space \((S, \Pr)\) with
\(\Pr[\omega] = 1/\abs{S}\) for every \(\omega \in S\). The notation
\(x \gets S\) (or \(x \xleftarrow{\$} S\)) means that \(x\) is sampled
according to the uniform distribution on \(S\). Under the uniform
distribution, for any event \(A \subseteq S\),
\[
\Pr[A] = \frac{\abs{A}}{\abs{S}} .
\]
\end{definition}

\subsection{Conditional probability and independence}

Conditioning updates the probability of one event on the information
that another has occurred.

\begin{definition}[Conditional probability]\label{probability:def-cond}
Let \(A, B\) be events with \(\Pr[B] > 0\). The \emph{conditional
probability of \(A\) given \(B\)} is
\[
\Pr[A \mid B] = \frac{\Pr[A \cap B]}{\Pr[B]} .
\]
When \(\Pr[B] = 0\) the quantity is left undefined.
\end{definition}

Conditioning on \(B\) restricts the sample space to \(B\) and
renormalises so that \(B\) has probability \(1\): the map
\(\omega \mapsto \Pr[\{\omega\} \mid B]\) is itself a probability mass
function on \(B\), and \(\Pr[A \mid B]\) is the probability it assigns
to \(A \cap B\).

\begin{proposition}[Chain rule and total probability]
\label{probability:prop-chain}
Let \(A_1, \dots, A_n\) be events with
\(\Pr[A_1 \cap \cdots \cap A_{n-1}] > 0\). Then
\[
\Pr[A_1 \cap \cdots \cap A_n]
= \Pr[A_1]\,\Pr[A_2 \mid A_1]\,\Pr[A_3 \mid A_1 \cap A_2]
  \cdots \Pr[A_n \mid A_1 \cap \cdots \cap A_{n-1}] .
\]
Moreover, if \(B_1, \dots, B_k\) partition \(\Omega\) (pairwise
disjoint with union \(\Omega\)) and each \(\Pr[B_i] > 0\), then for
any event \(A\),
\[
\Pr[A] = \sum_{i=1}^{k} \Pr[A \mid B_i]\,\Pr[B_i] .
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The chain rule follows by telescoping: each factor
\(\Pr[A_j \mid A_1 \cap \cdots \cap A_{j-1}]\) equals
\(\Pr[A_1 \cap \cdots \cap A_j] / \Pr[A_1 \cap \cdots \cap A_{j-1}]\)
by \cref{probability:def-cond}, and the product collapses to
\(\Pr[A_1 \cap \cdots \cap A_n]/1\). (Monotonicity ensures that all
the intermediate intersections have positive probability, so every
factor is defined.) For total probability, the events \(A \cap B_i\)
are pairwise disjoint with union \(A\) (since the \(B_i\) partition
\(\Omega\)), so finite additivity and
\(\Pr[A \cap B_i] = \Pr[A \mid B_i]\,\Pr[B_i]\) give
\(\Pr[A] = \sum_i \Pr[A \cap B_i]
= \sum_i \Pr[A \mid B_i]\,\Pr[B_i]\).
\end{proof}

Independence is the formal statement that one event carries no
information about another.

\begin{definition}[Independence of events]\label{probability:def-indep}
Two events \(A, B\) are \emph{independent} if
\[
\Pr[A \cap B] = \Pr[A]\,\Pr[B] .
\]
A family \(\{A_i\}_{i \in I}\) of events is \emph{mutually
independent} if for every finite subset \(J \subseteq I\),
\[
\Pr\Bigl[\,\bigcap_{i \in J} A_i\Bigr] = \prod_{i \in J} \Pr[A_i] .
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}\label{probability:rem-indep}
When \(\Pr[B] > 0\), independence of \(A\) and \(B\) is equivalent to
\(\Pr[A \mid B] = \Pr[A]\). Mutual independence is strictly stronger than
pairwise independence. The standard counterexample consists of two
fair coin tosses, with \(A\) ``the first is heads'', \(B\) ``the
second is heads'', and \(C\) ``the two coins differ''. Each pair is
independent, yet \(\Pr[A \cap B \cap C] = 0 \neq \tfrac18 =
\Pr[A]\,\Pr[B]\,\Pr[C]\), so the three are not mutually independent.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Random variables and expectation}

\begin{definition}[Random variable]\label{probability:def-rv}
Let \((\Omega, \Pr)\) be a finite probability space and \(T\) any set.
A \emph{(\(T\)-valued) random variable} is a function
\(X\colon \Omega \to T\). When \(T \subseteq \R\), we call \(X\)
\emph{real-valued}. For \(t \in T\),
\[
\Pr[X = t] = \Pr[\{\omega \in \Omega : X(\omega) = t\}],
\]
and more generally \(\Pr[X \in B]\) denotes the probability that \(X\)
takes a value in \(B \subseteq T\). The function
\(t \mapsto \Pr[X = t]\) on the finite image of \(X\) is the
\emph{distribution} (or \emph{law}) of \(X\).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Independent random variables]
\label{probability:def-indep-rv}
Random variables \(X_1, \dots, X_n\) (with values in sets
\(T_1, \dots, T_n\)) are \emph{mutually independent} if for all
\(t_1 \in T_1, \dots, t_n \in T_n\),
\[
\Pr[X_1 = t_1, \dots, X_n = t_n] = \prod_{i=1}^n \Pr[X_i = t_i] .
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Expectation]\label{probability:def-exp}
Let \(X\colon \Omega \to \R\) be a real-valued random variable on a
finite space. Its \emph{expectation} (or \emph{mean}) is
\[
\E[X] = \sum_{\omega \in \Omega} X(\omega)\,\Pr[\omega]
      = \sum_{t \in \img(X)} t \cdot \Pr[X = t],
\]
where \(\img(X)\) denotes the (finite) image of \(X\). The two
expressions agree by grouping outcomes according to their
\(X\)-value.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Indicator]\label{probability:def-indicator}
For an event \(A\), its \emph{indicator random variable}
\(\indicator_A\colon \Omega \to \{0,1\}\) takes the value
\(\indicator_A(\omega) = 1\) if \(\omega \in A\) and \(0\) otherwise.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Indicator expectation]
\label{probability:prop-indicator}
For any event \(A\), \(\E[\indicator_A] = \Pr[A]\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By \cref{probability:def-exp},
\(\E[\indicator_A] = \sum_{\omega} \indicator_A(\omega)\,\Pr[\omega]
= \sum_{\omega \in A} \Pr[\omega] = \Pr[A]\), since the indicator
vanishes off \(A\).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Linearity of expectation]\label{probability:thm-linearity}
Let \(X, Y\colon \Omega \to \R\) be random variables on a finite space
and \(a, b \in \R\). Then
\[
\E[aX + bY] = a\,\E[X] + b\,\E[Y] .
\]
More generally, for random variables \(X_1, \dots, X_n\) and scalars
\(a_1, \dots, a_n\),
\[
\E\Bigl[\,\sum_{i=1}^n a_i X_i\Bigr] = \sum_{i=1}^n a_i\,\E[X_i] .
\]
This holds whether or not the \(X_i\) are independent.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Directly from the definition,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\E[aX + bY]
&= \sum_{\omega \in \Omega} \bigl(aX(\omega) + bY(\omega)\bigr)
  \Pr[\omega]
\\
&= a \sum_{\omega} X(\omega)\Pr[\omega]
  + b \sum_{\omega} Y(\omega)\Pr[\omega]
\\
&= a\,\E[X] + b\,\E[Y],
\end{aligned}
\]
splitting the finite sum and extracting the constants. The \(n\)-term
statement follows by induction on \(n\).
\end{proof}

The following consequence underlies every averaging (``heavy-row'')
argument in the series.

\begin{proposition}[Markov's inequality]\label{probability:prop-markov}
Let \(X \ge 0\) be a real-valued random variable and \(a > 0\). Then
\(\Pr[X \ge a] \le \E[X]/a\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every outcome contributes nonnegatively to \(\E[X]\), and each outcome
with \(X(\omega) \ge a\) contributes at least \(a \Pr[\omega]\); hence
\(\E[X] \ge a \Pr[X \ge a]\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{The union bound and a birthday calculation}

This subsection proves the union bound, the inequality used most often
in cryptographic proofs; it requires no independence. It gives the
upper half of the \emph{birthday bound}, which governs how soon
collisions appear under uniform sampling: among roughly \(\sqrt{N}\)
uniform samples from a set of size \(N\), two are likely to coincide.
The birthday bound sets the output lengths of cryptographic hash
functions.

\begin{theorem}[Union bound / Boole's inequality]
\label{probability:thm-union}
For any events \(A_1, \dots, A_n\) in a finite probability space,
\[
\Pr\Bigl[\,\bigcup_{i=1}^n A_i\Bigr] \le \sum_{i=1}^n \Pr[A_i] .
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Disjointify the union into ``first occurrence'' events: \(B_1 = A_1\)
and \(B_i = A_i \setminus (A_1 \cup \cdots \cup A_{i-1})\) for
\(i \ge 2\). The \(B_i\) are pairwise disjoint, \(B_i \subseteq A_i\),
and \(\bigcup_i B_i = \bigcup_i A_i\). By finite additivity and
monotonicity (\cref{probability:prop-axioms}),
\[
\Pr\Bigl[\,\bigcup_i A_i\Bigr] = \Pr\Bigl[\,\bigcup_i B_i\Bigr]
= \sum_i \Pr[B_i] \le \sum_i \Pr[A_i] . \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

The ``first occurrence'' disjointification recurs, in a telescoping
form for increasing chains, in the proof of the continuity theorem of
\S\ref{probability:sub-beyond}. Taking complements in the union bound
gives the corresponding inequality for conjunctions.

\begin{corollary}[Union bound for the good event]
\label{probability:cor-union}
If each event \(A_i\) holds except with probability at most
\(\varepsilon_i\) (that is,
\(\Pr[\Omega \setminus A_i] \le \varepsilon_i\)), then all of them
hold simultaneously except with probability at most
\(\sum_i \varepsilon_i\):
\[
\Pr\Bigl[\,\bigcap_{i=1}^n A_i\Bigr]
\ge 1 - \sum_{i=1}^n \varepsilon_i .
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By De Morgan,
\(\Omega \setminus \bigcap_i A_i = \bigcup_i (\Omega \setminus A_i)\).
Apply the union bound to the complements and take the complement of
both sides.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Birthday bound]\label{probability:prop-birthday}
Sample \(x_1, \dots, x_k\) independently and uniformly from a set of
size \(N\), and let \(C\) be the event that some two of them collide,
i.e.\ \(x_i = x_j\) for some \(i < j\). Then
\[
\Pr[C] \le \binom{k}{2}\frac{1}{N} = \frac{k(k-1)}{2N},
\]
and, on the other side,
\[
\Pr[C] = 1 - \prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\Bigl(1 - \frac{i}{N}\Bigr)
\ge 1 - e^{-k(k-1)/(2N)} .
\]
In particular a collision becomes likely once
\(k = \Theta(\sqrt{N})\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Upper bound.} For \(i < j\) let \(A_{ij}\) be the event
\(x_i = x_j\). Since \(x_i\) and \(x_j\) are independent and uniform,
\(\Pr[A_{ij}] = \sum_{v} \Pr[x_i = v]\,\Pr[x_j = v]
= N \cdot (1/N)^2 = 1/N\). Now \(C = \bigcup_{i<j} A_{ij}\), a union
of \(\binom{k}{2}\) events, one per two-element subset of the indices
(\cref{finitefields:lem:binomcount}), so the union bound
(\cref{probability:thm-union}) gives
\(\Pr[C] \le \binom{k}{2}/N\).

\emph{Lower bound.} The complement \(\overline{C}\) is the event that
all \(k\) samples are distinct. If \(k > N\), the pigeonhole principle
forces a collision, so \(\Pr[\overline{C}] = 0\); the product also
vanishes, through its factor \(1 - N/N\), so the stated equality holds
and the exponential bound is trivial. Let then \(k \le N\), and for
\(0 \le i \le k\) let \(D_i\) be the event that the first \(i\)
samples are pairwise distinct (\(D_0 = D_1 = \Omega\), and
\(D_k = \overline{C}\)). We show
\(\Pr[D_i] = \prod_{j=0}^{i-1}(1 - j/N)\) by induction on \(i \le k\);
every factor is positive, since \(j \le k - 1 \le N - 1\), so in
particular each \(D_i\) has positive probability and conditioning on
it is legitimate (\cref{probability:def-cond}). For the step, the
events pinning the first \(i\) samples to specific distinct values
partition \(D_i\), each with probability \(N^{-i} > 0\) by
independence and uniformity; given any one of them, the next sample
avoids the \(i\) values taken with probability \(1 - i/N\), again by
independence and uniformity. Averaging over the cells (total
probability, \cref{probability:prop-chain}) gives
\(\Pr[D_{i+1} \mid D_i] = 1 - i/N\), and
\(\Pr[D_{i+1}] = \Pr[D_{i+1} \mid D_i]\,\Pr[D_i]\) completes the
induction. Taking \(i = k\) yields the stated equality, in which every
factor is nonnegative, so the inequality \(1 - x \le e^{-x}\) applies
factor by factor. (For \(0 \le x < 1\) this inequality follows from Bernoulli's
inequality, \((1 - x/n)^n \ge 1 - x\) for \(n > x\), by letting
\(n \to \infty\) in the limit definition of \(e^{-x}\); for
\(x \ge 1\) the left side is nonpositive.) Thus
\[
\Pr[\overline{C}] = \prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\Bigl(1 - \frac iN\Bigr)
\le \prod_{i=0}^{k-1} e^{-i/N}
= \exp\Bigl(-\tfrac1N \sum_{i=0}^{k-1} i\Bigr)
= e^{-k(k-1)/(2N)},
\]
summing the exponents via
\(\sum_{i=0}^{k-1} i = k(k-1)/2\). Taking complements gives
\(\Pr[C] = 1 - \Pr[\overline{C}] \ge 1 - e^{-k(k-1)/(2N)}\).
\end{proof}

% FIGURE (planned, dedicated figures pass): fig:probability-birthday ---
% script-generated collision-probability curve: exact Pr[collision]
% against the sample count k for a fixed set size N, with the two bounds
% of \cref{probability:prop-birthday} overlaid and the sqrt(N) threshold
% marked on the k-axis.

\begin{example}\label{probability:exp-birthday}
With \(N = 365\) and \(k = 23\), the classical birthday problem, the
exact product gives \(\Pr[C] = 0.5073\), a probability above one half,
and the proposition brackets it as
\(0.5000 \le \Pr[C] \le 0.6932\). The threshold appears at small
scale as well: with \(N = 16\), the fifth sample, the first beyond
\(\sqrt{16} = 4\), raises the collision probability above one half,
\(\Pr[C] = 0.5001\) at \(k = 5\).
\end{example}

\begin{remark}\label{probability:rem-birthday}
The threshold \(k \approx \sqrt{N}\) is the reason an \(n\)-bit hash
function (so \(N = 2^n\)) offers only about \(2^{n/2}\) collision
resistance: an adversary expects a collision after roughly
\(2^{n/2}\) random evaluations --- for \(N = 2^{256}\), that is
\(\sqrt{2^{256}} = 2^{128}\). Output lengths are therefore chosen
twice the desired security level. The same square-root
phenomenon recurs in random-walk discrete-log attacks (Pollard's rho,
\cref{ellipticcurves:prop-ecdlp-sqrt}), so the curves used in Halo~2
and Orchard are sized so that \(\sqrt{N}\) itself lies far beyond
feasible computation.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Statistical distance}

Statistical distance measures how far apart two distributions are, such
as a real key-generation procedure and an idealised uniform one; it
formalises ``no statistical test can distinguish them well''.

\begin{definition}[Statistical distance]\label{probability:def-sd}
Let \(P\) and \(Q\) be probability distributions on the same finite
set \(S\) (i.e.\ probability mass functions
\(P, Q\colon S \to [0,1]\)). Their \emph{statistical distance} (or
\emph{total variation distance}) is
\[
\Delta(P, Q) = \frac12 \sum_{s \in S} \bigl|P(s) - Q(s)\bigr| .
\]
For random variables \(X, Y\) with values in \(S\), \(\Delta(X, Y)\)
denotes the statistical distance of their distributions.
\end{definition}

The factor \(\tfrac12\) makes \(\Delta\) range over \([0,1]\) and
makes it equal to the maximum advantage of a distinguisher, as part (3)
of the next theorem establishes.

% FIGURE (planned, dedicated figures pass): fig:probability-sd ---
% statistical distance as area: two overlaid probability mass functions
% on a common finite set drawn as bar charts; the pointwise differences
% shaded, with the excess region S^+ highlighted, total shaded area
% equal to 2*Delta(P,Q) so that Delta is half the L1 difference.

\begin{theorem}[Properties of statistical distance]
\label{probability:thm-sd}
Let \(P, Q, R\) be distributions on a finite set \(S\). Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(0 \le \Delta(P,Q) \le 1\); and \(\Delta(P,Q) = 0\) if and
  only if \(P = Q\).
\item \emph{(Metric.)} \(\Delta\) is symmetric and satisfies the
  triangle inequality
  \(\Delta(P,R) \le \Delta(P,Q) + \Delta(Q,R)\).
\item \emph{(Variational characterisation.)}
  \(\displaystyle \Delta(P,Q)
  = \max_{A \subseteq S}\bigl(P(A) - Q(A)\bigr)
  = \max_{A \subseteq S}\bigl|P(A) - Q(A)\bigr|\), where
  \(P(A) = \sum_{s \in A} P(s)\).
\item \emph{(Data processing / post-processing.)} For any (possibly
  randomised) function \(f\) applied to a sample,
  \(\Delta(f(P), f(Q)) \le \Delta(P, Q)\). In particular no algorithm
  can increase statistical distance.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(1) Nonnegativity holds termwise. For the upper bound, split \(S\) into
\(S^+ = \{s : P(s) \ge Q(s)\}\) and \(S^- = S \setminus S^+\). Since
\(\sum_s P(s) = \sum_s Q(s) = 1\), we have
\(\sum_{s}(P(s) - Q(s)) = 0\), so
\(\sum_{s \in S^+}(P(s) - Q(s))
= \sum_{s \in S^-}(Q(s) - P(s)) =: D \ge 0\). Hence
\(\Delta(P,Q) = \tfrac12\bigl(\sum_{S^+}(P - Q)
+ \sum_{S^-}(Q - P)\bigr) = D\). As
\(D = \sum_{S^+}(P(s) - Q(s)) \le \sum_{S^+} P(s) \le 1\), we get
\(\Delta \le 1\). If \(\Delta = 0\) then every
\(|P(s) - Q(s)| = 0\), so \(P = Q\); the converse is immediate.

(2) Symmetry holds termwise. For the triangle inequality,
\(|P(s) - R(s)| \le |P(s) - Q(s)| + |Q(s) - R(s)|\) termwise by the
triangle inequality on \(\R\); summing over \(s\) and halving gives
the claim.

(3) With \(D\) as above, take \(A = S^+\): then
\(P(A) - Q(A) = \sum_{S^+}(P - Q) = D = \Delta(P,Q)\), so the maximum
is at least \(\Delta\). Conversely, for \emph{any}
\(A \subseteq S\),
\(P(A) - Q(A) = \sum_{s \in A}(P(s) - Q(s))
\le \sum_{s \in A \cap S^+}(P(s) - Q(s)) \le D = \Delta\), since
dropping the negative terms only increases the sum and extending from
\(A \cap S^+\) to all of \(S^+\) increases it further. Thus the
maximum over \(A\) of \(P(A) - Q(A)\) is exactly \(\Delta\); replacing
\(A\) by its complement swaps the sign, giving the same value for
\(\max_A |P(A) - Q(A)|\).

(4) First take \(f\) deterministic, \(f\colon S \to U\). For
\(u \in U\), \(f(P)(u) = P(f^{-1}(u))\). Then
\[
\Delta(f(P), f(Q))
= \tfrac12 \sum_{u} \bigl|P(f^{-1}(u)) - Q(f^{-1}(u))\bigr|
\le \tfrac12 \sum_u \sum_{s \in f^{-1}(u)} |P(s) - Q(s)|
= \Delta(P, Q),
\]
using \(|\sum_s a_s| \le \sum_s |a_s|\) on each fibre, and that the
fibres \(f^{-1}(u)\) partition \(S\). A randomised \(f\) is a
deterministic function of the sample \(s\) together with independent
fresh coins \(r\); apply the deterministic case to the joint variable
\((s, r)\), noting that the two joint distributions
\(P \times U_r\) and \(Q \times U_r\) have the same statistical
distance \(\Delta(P, Q)\), the shared coin distribution contributing
nothing.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Distinguishing interpretation]\label{probability:rem-sd}
Part (3) states that \(\Delta(P,Q)\) is exactly the best advantage of
any test --- even a computationally unbounded one --- that, given a
single sample, must guess whether it came from \(P\) or \(Q\): the
optimal test outputs ``\(P\)'' on the set \(A = S^+\), and its
advantage \(\Pr_{P}[A] - \Pr_{Q}[A]\) equals \(\Delta(P,Q)\). Part (4)
makes statistical distance compose in proofs: if
\(\Delta(P,Q) \le \varepsilon\), then \(P\) and \(Q\) are
interchangeable in any analysis at the cost of an additive
\(\varepsilon\) in every probability. When \(\varepsilon\) is
negligible (\cref{probability:def-negl} below), we call \(P\) and
\(Q\) \emph{statistically indistinguishable}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Uniform sampling and the bias of modular reduction}

The most tractable source of randomness is a stream of independent
uniform bits, whereas cryptography requires uniform elements of other
finite sets: a scalar in \(\Z/q\Z\) (\cref{sec:intmod}), a field
element of \(\Fp\) (\cref{sec:finitefields}), a point in a group. If
the target set \(S\) has \(\abs{S} = 2^\ell\), reading \(\ell\)
uniform bits as a number in \(\{0, \dots, 2^\ell - 1\}\) and indexing
into \(S\) is exact. When
\(\abs{S}\) is not a power of two --- most importantly when
\(S = \Z/q\Z\) for a prime \(q\), the scalar field of an elliptic
curve (\cref{sec:ellipticcurves}) --- a common method is
\emph{reduce-modulo}: sample a long uniform integer and reduce it mod
\(q\). Some residues then receive one more preimage than
others; the next proposition bounds the resulting bias.

\begin{proposition}[Bias of modular reduction]
\label{probability:prop-modbias}
Let \(q \ge 2\) be an integer and let \(K = 2^L\) for a nonnegative
integer \(L\). Sample \(U\) uniformly from \(\{0, 1, \dots, K-1\}\)
(equivalently, read \(L\) uniform bits as an integer), and set
\(R = U \bmod q \in \{0, 1, \dots, q-1\}\). Let \(\mathcal{U}_q\)
denote the uniform distribution on \(\{0, \dots, q-1\}\). Then the
statistical distance of \(R\) from uniform satisfies
\[
\Delta(R, \mathcal{U}_q) \le \frac{q}{K} = \frac{q}{2^{L}} .
\]
Consequently, if \(q < 2^n\) and one takes \(L = n + s\) bits, then
\(\Delta(R, \mathcal{U}_q) < 2^{-s}\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write \(K = mq + t\) with \(m = \floor{K/q}\) and remainder
\(t = K \bmod q\), so \(0 \le t < q\). For a residue
\(r \in \{0, \dots, q-1\}\), the number of integers in
\(\{0, \dots, K-1\}\) congruent to \(r\) modulo \(q\) is \(m+1\) if
\(r < t\) and \(m\) if \(r \ge t\). Hence
\[
\Pr[R = r] =
\begin{cases}
(m+1)/K, & r < t,\\[2pt]
m/K, & r \ge t.
\end{cases}
\]
Comparing with the target probability \(1/q\) and using
\(m/K \le 1/q \le (m+1)/K\) (since \(mq \le K \le (m+1)q\)),
\[
\Bigl|\Pr[R = r] - \tfrac1q\Bigr|
\le \frac{m+1}{K} - \frac{m}{K} = \frac1K
\qquad\text{for every } r .
\]
Therefore
\[
\Delta(R, \mathcal{U}_q)
= \frac12 \sum_{r=0}^{q-1} \Bigl|\Pr[R = r] - \tfrac1q\Bigr|
\le \frac12 \cdot q \cdot \frac1K = \frac{q}{2K} \le \frac{q}{K} .
\]
(The computation yields the sharper bound \(q/(2K)\);
\(q/K\) is the usual stated form.) For the final claim, \(q < 2^n\)
and \(K = 2^{n+s}\) give \(q/K < 2^n/2^{n+s} = 2^{-s}\).
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{probability:exp-modbias}
Let \(L = 6\) uniform bits be reduced modulo \(q = 5\). Then
\(K = 64 = 12 \cdot 5 + 4\), so the four residues \(r < 4\) receive
\(13\) preimages each and the residue \(r = 4\) receives \(12\); the
statistical distance from uniform is exactly \(1/80\),
within the guarantee \(2^{-3}\) of the proposition (as \(q < 2^3\)
and \(s = 6 - 3 = 3\)).
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Sampling scalars in practice]\label{probability:rem-modbias}
By the proposition, a scalar modulo a \({\sim}256\)-bit prime \(q\)
with statistical distance below \(2^{-128}\) from uniform is obtained
by reducing \(256 + 128 = 384\) uniform bits modulo \(q\). The bias
\(2^{-s}\), with the slack \(s\) set to the security
parameter, is negligible (\cref{probability:def-negl}), so the
reduce-modulo sample is statistically indistinguishable from a
perfectly uniform scalar, and by the data-processing inequality
(\cref{probability:thm-sd}(4)) substituting one for the other
anywhere costs at most \(2^{-128}\) per use. An alternative,
\emph{rejection sampling}, draws \(\ceil{\log_2 q}\) bits, accepts if
the result is \(< q\), and otherwise retries; it gives \emph{exactly}
uniform output at the cost of a variable but (with overwhelming
probability) small number of retries. For this series the negligible
bias of reduce-modulo is acceptable and simpler to analyse. The Orchard
specification employs this reduce-modulo sampling when
deriving scalars from hash outputs: its \(\mathrm{ToScalar}\) reduces
the full \(512\)-bit \(\mathsf{PRFexpand}\) output modulo the
\(255\)-bit group order, a slack of \(257\) bits, well
beyond the \(128\)-bit slack computed above (Zcash protocol specification
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#orchardkeycomponents}{\S4.2.3}
and \href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#concreteprfs}{\S5.4.2}).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Beyond finite spaces: countable additivity, limits, and
densities}\label{probability:sub-beyond}

Two uses require more than a finite sample space. A process observed
until an event occurs, such as an unbounded retry loop or a random walk
run until it first hits a barrier, has no finite horizon, even when it
terminates with probability one; a waiting time measured in continuous
units has no finite sample space. This subsection adds countable
additivity, continuity along monotone events, and laws defined by
densities, and states in Remark~\ref{probability:rem-extension} the
measure-theoretic results it assumes.

\begin{definition}[Countable probability space]
\label{probability:def-countable}
A \emph{countable probability space} is a pair \((\Omega, \Pr)\) in
which \(\Omega\) is a countable set and
\(\Pr\colon \Omega \to [0,1]\) satisfies
\(\sum_{\omega \in \Omega} \Pr[\omega] = 1\); the sum is independent
of the enumeration order because its terms are nonnegative. Events are
arbitrary subsets \(A \subseteq \Omega\), with
\(\Pr[A] = \sum_{\omega \in A} \Pr[\omega]\), and
Definitions \ref{probability:def-event}--\ref{probability:def-exp}
carry over with ``finite'' read as ``countable'' (a random variable's
image may now be countably infinite), with one exception:
\cref{probability:def-uniform} does not --- equal weights cannot sum
to \(1\) on a countably infinite set, so the uniform distribution
remains a strictly finite notion. Expectation additionally requires
its defining sum to converge absolutely.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Countable additivity]
\label{probability:prop-countadd}
Let \((\Omega, \Pr)\) be a countable probability space and
\(A_1, A_2, \ldots\) pairwise disjoint events. Then
\[
\Pr\Bigl[\,\bigcup_{n \ge 1} A_n\Bigr] = \sum_{n \ge 1} \Pr[A_n],
\]
and in particular the basic axioms of
\cref{probability:prop-axioms} hold unchanged.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every outcome of the union lies in exactly one \(A_n\), so both sides
sum the same nonnegative terms \(\Pr[\omega]\), grouped differently;
a series of nonnegative terms may be summed in any order, or in
groups, without changing its value.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Continuity along monotone events]
\label{probability:thm-continuity}
Let \(\Pr\) be a probability assignment, on any sample space, defined
for a family of events closed under complement and countable union,
and countably additive on it. If
\(A_1 \subseteq A_2 \subseteq \cdots\) is an increasing chain of
events, then
\[
\Pr\Bigl[\,\bigcup_{n \ge 1} A_n\Bigr] = \lim_{n \to \infty} \Pr[A_n],
\]
and dually \(\Pr[\bigcap_n B_n] = \lim_n \Pr[B_n]\) for a decreasing
chain \(B_1 \supseteq B_2 \supseteq \cdots\). On a countable space the
hypothesis holds automatically, by
\cref{probability:prop-countadd}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Disjointify by telescoping: set \(D_1 = A_1\) and
\(D_n = A_n \setminus A_{n-1}\) for \(n \ge 2\). The \(D_n\) are
pairwise disjoint, \(\bigcup_{k \le n} D_k = A_n\), and
\(\bigcup_n D_n = \bigcup_n A_n\). Countable additivity gives
\[
\Pr\Bigl[\,\bigcup_n A_n\Bigr]
= \sum_{n \ge 1} \Pr[D_n]
= \lim_{n \to \infty} \sum_{k=1}^{n} \Pr[D_k]
= \lim_{n \to \infty} \Pr[A_n],
\]
the final step by finite additivity. The decreasing case follows by
complementation.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Infinite sequence of independent trials]
\label{probability:def-trials}
Fix a finite outcome set \(T\) and a distribution \(p\) on \(T\). An
\emph{infinite sequence of independent trials} with law \(p\) is a
sequence of random variables \(X_1, X_2, \ldots\) such that every
prefix \((X_1, \ldots, X_n)\) consists of \(n\) mutually independent
\(p\)-distributed trials in the sense of
\cref{probability:def-indep-rv} --- equivalently, the prefix's law is
that of the finite probability space \(T^{n}\) carrying the product
weights \(\Pr[(t_1, \ldots, t_n)] = p(t_1) \cdots p(t_n)\). An event
is \emph{finitely determined} if membership in it is decided by some
fixed finite prefix; its probability is the finite-space value. An
event of the form ``some prefix satisfies \(P\)'' is the union of an
increasing chain of finitely determined events, and
\cref{probability:thm-continuity} assigns it the limit of the prefix
probabilities.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Assumed measure-theoretic results]\label{probability:rem-extension}
\Cref{probability:def-trials} presumes that a single, countably
additive probability assignment on infinite sequences exists that
restricts correctly to every finite prefix at once. That existence
statement is the Kolmogorov extension theorem, and its proof belongs
to measure theory, which lies off this series' critical path: the
space of infinite sequences over at least two possible outcomes is
uncountable, so it escapes \cref{probability:def-countable}, and the
extension specifies the admissible events. This series therefore takes
it, with its countable additivity and hence
\cref{probability:thm-continuity}, as an assumption rather than a
theorem. On infinite-sequence spaces, only finitely determined events
and their monotone limits appear in this series, and for those the
values are determined by the finite theory together with continuity;
the density-defined laws of \cref{probability:def-density} rest on the
analogous assumption, their interval and joint-event probabilities
being taken to cohere as the stated integrals. The independent
families of \cref{probability:def-density-family} extend that
assumption in two ways:
joint events of several density-defined quantities are taken to be
iterated integrals, in whichever order of integration is convenient
--- the exchange of order for nonnegative integrands is the
Fubini--Tonelli theorem, likewise measure-theoretic --- and the gap
sequences of \cref{probability:def-clock} are infinite families, of
which only finite prefixes enter any event this series uses, except
through countable unions over the values of an arrival count ---
\(N(t)\) of \cref{probability:def-clock}, or the count \(X\) of
\cref{probability:thm-negbin}, each event ``count \(= x\)'' being a
joint event of finitely many gaps. For any set \(B\) of values, the
event ``count \(\in B\)'' --- among them the event that the count is
finite, and the tail events of \cref{probability:thm-chernoff} --- is
taken to have probability \(\sum_{x \in B} \Pr[\text{count} = x]\),
by the countable additivity assumed above.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Law defined by a density]
\label{probability:def-density}
A real-valued random quantity \(X\) has \emph{density}
\(f\colon \R \to \R_{\ge 0}\) if
\(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} f(t)\,dt = 1\) and
\[
\Pr[a \le X \le b] = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt
\qquad \text{for all } a \le b;
\]
improper Riemann integrals suffice throughout this series. The
\emph{distribution function} is
\(F(t) = \Pr[X \le t] = \int_{-\infty}^{t} f\), so
\(\Pr[X > t] = 1 - F(t)\), and single points carry probability zero.
The expectation is \(\E[X] = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} t\,f(t)\,dt\)
when this integral converges absolutely. Two density-defined
quantities \(X, Y\) are \emph{independent} if
\(\Pr[X \in I,\ Y \in J] = \Pr[X \in I]\,\Pr[Y \in J]\) for all
intervals \(I, J\); probabilities of joint events are then taken to
factor through iterated integrals of the product
\(f_X(s)\,f_Y(t)\).
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{Waiting times: tail sums, geometric trials, and Wald's identity}

The results of this unit need only countable expectation and finitely
determined events. For a variable with values in
\(\N \cup \{\infty\}\), the defining sum of the expectation has
nonnegative terms, and the value \(+\infty\) is permitted when it
diverges.

\begin{lemma}[Tail-sum formula]\label{probability:lem-tailsum}
Let \(T\) be a random variable with values in \(\N \cup \{\infty\}\)
and \(\Pr[T = \infty] = 0\). Then
\(\E[T] = \sum_{k \ge 1} \Pr[T \ge k]\), either side being infinite
exactly when the other is.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Expanding \(\Pr[T \ge k] = \sum_{m \ge k} \Pr[T = m]\) and regrouping
the doubly indexed nonnegative series, each term \(\Pr[T = m]\) is
counted once for every \(k \le m\), that is, \(m\) times; nonnegative
series may be regrouped freely.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Geometric waiting time]
\label{probability:prop-geometric}
In an infinite sequence of independent trials, each succeeding with
probability \(\varepsilon > 0\), let \(T\) be the index of the first
success. Then \(\Pr[T \ge k] = (1-\varepsilon)^{k-1}\) and
\(\E[T] = 1/\varepsilon\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The event \(T \ge k\) says the first \(k-1\) trials all fail --- a
finitely determined event of probability \((1-\varepsilon)^{k-1}\);
in particular
\(\Pr[T = \infty] = \lim_k (1-\varepsilon)^{k-1} = 0\) by continuity
(\cref{probability:thm-continuity}). The tail-sum formula then gives
\(\E[T] = \sum_{k \ge 1} (1-\varepsilon)^{k-1} = 1/\varepsilon\), a
geometric series.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Wald's identity]\label{probability:prop-wald}
Let \(X_1, X_2, \ldots\) be an infinite sequence of independent
trials with common law, each \(X_i \ge 0\) with finite mean, and let
\(T\) be a \emph{stopping rule}: a random index such that, for every
\(k\), whether \(T = k\) is decided by the first \(k\) trials alone.
If \(\E[T] < \infty\), then
\[
\E\Bigl[\,\sum_{i=1}^{T} X_i\Bigr] = \E[T] \cdot \E[X_1] .
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write \(S = \sum_{i=1}^{T} X_i\) and \(S_k = \sum_{i=1}^{k} X_i\), so
that \(S = S_k\) on the event \(T = k\). The hypothesis
\(\E[T] < \infty\) forces \(\Pr[T = \infty] = 0\), by the convention
preceding \cref{probability:lem-tailsum}. The variable \(S\) takes
countably many values --- on \(\{T = k\}\) it agrees with \(S_k\),
whose values run over a finite set, the trials having finitely many
outcomes --- and each event \(\{S = s\}\) is the disjoint countable
union \(\bigcup_k \{T = k,\ S_k = s\}\) of finitely determined events,
so its probability is assigned by countable additivity
(\cref{probability:rem-extension}). By \(\E[S]\) we mean
\(\sum_s s \Pr[S = s]\), the expectation of this countably supported
distribution, a sum of nonnegative terms.

Expanding each \(\Pr[S = s]\) and regrouping the doubly indexed
nonnegative series,
\[
\E[S]
= \sum_s s \sum_{k \ge 1} \Pr[T = k,\ S_k = s]
= \sum_{k \ge 1} \E[S_k \indicator_{T = k}] ,
\]
each inner expectation living on the finite prefix space of the first
\(k\) trials (\cref{probability:def-trials}), where linearity
(\cref{probability:thm-linearity}) expands it as
\(\sum_{i=1}^{k} \E[X_i \indicator_{T = k}]\). Regrouping the
nonnegative double series once more,
\[
\E[S]
= \sum_{i \ge 1} \sum_{k \ge i} \E[X_i \indicator_{T = k}] .
\]
Fix \(i\) and let \(n \ge i\). The events \(T = k\) (for
\(k \le n\)), \(T > n\), and \(T \ge i\) are all decided by the first
\(n\) trials, and on that prefix space the pointwise identity
\(\indicator_{T \ge i}
= \sum_{k=i}^{n} \indicator_{T = k} + \indicator_{T > n}\)
holds, so linearity gives
\[
\sum_{k=i}^{n} \E[X_i \indicator_{T = k}]
= \E[X_i \indicator_{T \ge i}] - \E[X_i \indicator_{T > n}] .
\]
The trials take finitely many values, so \(X_i \le M\) for a constant
\(M\), and
\(0 \le \E[X_i \indicator_{T > n}] \le M \Pr[T > n] \to 0\) as
\(n \to \infty\): the terms \(\Pr[T \ge k]\) of the convergent series
\(\E[T]\) tend to zero. Hence
\(\sum_{k \ge i} \E[X_i \indicator_{T = k}]
= \E[X_i \indicator_{T \ge i}]\).

The remaining step is a factorisation. The event \(T \ge i\) --- the
negation of \(T \le i - 1\) --- is decided by the first \(i - 1\) trials, so its
indicator is a function \(g(t_1, \dots, t_{i-1})\) of the first
\(i - 1\) trial values, and on the prefix space of the first \(i\)
trials the expectation splits along the product weights of
\cref{probability:def-trials}: with \(p\) the common law,
\[
\E[X_i \indicator_{T \ge i}]
= \sum_{t_1, \dots, t_i} g(t_1, \dots, t_{i-1})\; t_i\;
  p(t_1) \cdots p(t_i)
= \Pr[T \ge i] \cdot \E[X_1] ,
\]
the double sum factoring into the sum over
\((t_1, \dots, t_{i-1})\), which totals \(\Pr[T \ge i]\), times
\(\sum_{t_i} t_i\, p(t_i) = \E[X_1]\). Assembling the pieces and
applying the tail-sum formula (\cref{probability:lem-tailsum}),
\[
\E[S]
= \sum_{i \ge 1} \E[X_1] \Pr[T \ge i]
= \E[X_1] \cdot \E[T] . \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Exponential clocks, Poisson counts, and the Chernoff method}

Block discovery is modelled by \emph{clocks}: each party's next block
arrives after a random delay whose remaining law does not depend on how
long the delay has already run, and two parties race. A light client
that samples a chain needs a tail bound on the probability that an
adversary has found \(cL\) blocks while the honest chain found \(L\),
and a wallet that spaces its network queries needs a delay law with a
prescribed mean and no memory. This unit constructs these clocks and
proves the tail bounds from density-defined laws
(\cref{probability:def-density}) and Markov's inequality with a free
parameter.

\begin{definition}[Exponential law]\label{probability:def-exponential}
A random quantity \(X\) is \emph{exponential with rate}
\(\lambda > 0\), written \(X \sim \mathrm{Exp}(\lambda)\), if it has
the density \(f(t) = \lambda e^{-\lambda t}\) for \(t \ge 0\) and
\(f(t) = 0\) for \(t < 0\).
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Tail, mean, and memorylessness]
\label{probability:lem-exp-tail}
Let \(X \sim \mathrm{Exp}(\lambda)\). Then
\(\Pr[X > t] = e^{-\lambda t}\) for all \(t \ge 0\),
\(\E[X] = 1/\lambda\), and
\[
\Pr[X > s + t \mid X > s] = \Pr[X > t]
\qquad \text{for all } s, t \ge 0 ,
\]
conditional probability being the ratio of
\cref{probability:def-cond}: a wait without an arrival leaves the law
of the remaining wait unchanged. Conversely, a nonnegative quantity
with \(\Pr[X > t] = e^{-\lambda t}\) for all \(t \ge 0\) is
exponential with rate \(\lambda\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The density integrates to \(1\), and
\(\Pr[X > t] = 1 - \int_0^t \lambda e^{-\lambda s}\,ds
= e^{-\lambda t}\). Integrating by parts,
\[
\E[X] = \int_0^\infty t\,\lambda e^{-\lambda t}\,dt
= \bigl[-t e^{-\lambda t}\bigr]_0^\infty
  + \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}\,dt
= \frac{1}{\lambda} .
\]
Since \(X > s + t\) implies \(X > s\), the conditional probability is
\(e^{-\lambda(s+t)}/e^{-\lambda s} = e^{-\lambda t}\). For the
converse, the events \(X > a\) and \(X > b\) (\(a \le b\)) differ by
\(a < X \le b\), so
\(\Pr[a < X \le b] = e^{-\lambda a} - e^{-\lambda b}
= \int_a^b \lambda e^{-\lambda t}\,dt\); and the point \(a\) carries
probability at most \(e^{-\lambda(a - \delta)} - e^{-\lambda a}\) for
every \(\delta > 0\), hence zero, so \(\Pr[a \le X \le b]\) is the
same integral.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Exponential clocks as limits of geometric waiting times]
\label{probability:rem-geometric-limit}
The exponential law is the continuous limit of the geometric waiting
time (\cref{probability:prop-geometric}). If trials succeed with a
small probability \(\varepsilon\) and each occupies time
\(\varepsilon/\lambda\), the first success arrives at time
\(\varepsilon T/\lambda\), and for integer-valued \(T\)
\[
\Pr[\varepsilon T/\lambda > t]
= \Pr\bigl[T \ge \floor{\lambda t/\varepsilon} + 1\bigr]
= (1 - \varepsilon)^{\floor{\lambda t/\varepsilon}}
\longrightarrow e^{-\lambda t}
\qquad (\varepsilon \to 0) ,
\]
because \((1-\varepsilon)^{1/\varepsilon} \to e^{-1}\) and the floor
alters the exponent by less than one. Whether a proof of work
satisfies this model, with each candidate independent of the last,
depends on the trials; the volumes on consensus treat that question.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[A jittered schedule]\label{probability:exp-jitter}
% Source: .wip/rewrite/math-guide/compute/clocks.py
%   jitter: n=3 -> T = 28800 s = 8 h; Pr[Delta > 24 h] = e^-3 = 0.0498; Pr[Delta > 2T] = e^-2 = 0.1353
A wallet that must poll each of \(n\) addresses about once a day
waits \(\Delta \sim \mathrm{Exp}(1/T)\) between polls, with
\(T = 86400/n\) seconds: \(\E[\Delta] = T\) by
\cref{probability:lem-exp-tail}, one expected poll per address per
day. For \(n = 3\), \(T = 28\,800\) seconds, eight hours; the gap
exceeds a full day with probability \(e^{-3} = 0.0498\), and exceeds
twice its mean with probability \(e^{-2} = 0.1353\) whatever
\(T\) is. By memorylessness, an observer who has seen no poll for some
time obtains no information about the time of the next one.
\end{example}

Clock races and clock restarts both involve joint events of
several density-defined quantities, which
\cref{probability:def-density} provides only for pairs.

\begin{definition}[Independent family of density-defined quantities]
\label{probability:def-density-family}
Density-defined quantities \(X_1, \dots, X_n\) with densities
\(f_1, \dots, f_n\) form an \emph{independent family} if
\(\Pr[X_1 \in I_1, \dots, X_n \in I_n] = \prod_i \Pr[X_i \in I_i]\)
for all intervals \(I_1, \dots, I_n\). As in
\cref{probability:def-density}, the probability of a joint event
\((X_1, \dots, X_n) \in R\), for a region \(R \subseteq \R^n\) cut out
by finitely many inequalities, is then taken to be the iterated
integral of \(f_1(s_1) \cdots f_n(s_n)\) over \(R\), in whichever
order of integration is convenient (\cref{probability:rem-extension}).
In particular a quantity that is a function of one block of the
family is independent of a quantity that is a function of a disjoint
block: integrate the two blocks separately. A sequence
\(X_1, X_2, \ldots\) is an independent family if every finite prefix
is one, in the manner of \cref{probability:def-trials}.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Integrating out one quantity]
\label{probability:lem-integrate-out}
Let \(U\) be a density-defined quantity, with density \(f_U\), that
is a function of the first \(k\) members of an independent family
\(X_1, \dots, X_n\), and write \(W = (X_{k+1}, \dots, X_n)\). Let
\(R\) be a region in the \((U, W)\)-coordinates whose section
\(\{s : (s, w) \in R\}\) is an interval for every fixed value \(w\)
of \(W\). Then
\[
\Pr[(U, W) \in R]
= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} f_U(s)\,\Pr[W \in R_s]\,ds ,
\qquad R_s = \{w : (s, w) \in R\} ,
\]
the inner probability being a joint event of
\(X_{k+1}, \dots, X_n\) alone.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Integrate the product density over the first \(k\) coordinates
first, holding \(w\) fixed: the result is the probability that \(U\)
lies in the interval \(\{s : (s, w) \in R\}\), which by
\cref{probability:def-density} equals
\(\int f_U(s)\,\indicator[(s, w) \in R]\,ds\). What remains is the
integral of \(f_{k+1} \cdots f_n\) against this function of \(w\);
exchanging the order and integrating over \(w\) first leaves, for
each \(s\), the inner probability \(\Pr[W \in R_s]\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Density of a sum]\label{probability:lem-convolution}
Let \(U, V\) be independent density-defined quantities, nonnegative
(their densities vanish on the negative axis). Then \(U + V\) is
density-defined, with density the \emph{convolution}
\((f_U * f_V)(x) = \int_0^x f_U(u)\, f_V(x - u)\,du\) for \(x \ge 0\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For \(0 \le a \le b\), the event \(a \le U + V \le b\) is the region
\(\{(u, v) : u, v \ge 0,\ a \le u + v \le b\}\), so
\[
\Pr[a \le U + V \le b]
= \int_0^\infty f_U(u) \int_{\max(a - u,\,0)}^{\max(b - u,\,0)}
  f_V(v)\,dv\,du
= \int_0^\infty f_U(u) \int_{\max(a,\,u)}^{\max(b,\,u)}
  f_V(x - u)\,dx\,du ,
\]
substituting \(x = u + v\) in the inner integral. Exchanging the
order, the region becomes \(a \le x \le b\), \(0 \le u \le x\), and
the right-hand side is \(\int_a^b (f_U * f_V)(x)\,dx\). With
\(a = 0\) and \(b = \infty\) the left-hand side is \(1\), so
\(f_U * f_V\) is a density.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Two-clock race]\label{probability:lem-race}
Let \(X \sim \mathrm{Exp}(\lambda)\) and \(Y \sim \mathrm{Exp}(\mu)\)
be independent. Then, for every \(u \ge 0\),
\[
\Pr[X < Y,\ X \le u]
= \frac{\lambda}{\lambda + \mu}\bigl(1 - e^{-(\lambda + \mu)u}\bigr) ,
\]
symmetrically with the roles of \((X, \lambda)\) and \((Y, \mu)\)
exchanged, and \(\Pr[X = Y] = 0\). Consequently
\(\Pr[X < Y] = \lambda/(\lambda + \mu)\); the winning time
\(\min(X, Y)\) is exponential with rate \(\lambda + \mu\); and the
winner is independent of the winning time,
\[
\Pr[X < Y,\ \min(X, Y) \le u]
= \Pr[X < Y] \cdot \Pr[\min(X, Y) \le u]
\qquad \text{for all } u \ge 0 .
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The event is the region \(0 \le s \le u\), \(t > s\) in the
\((X, Y)\)-plane. Integrating over \(t\) first
(\cref{probability:def-density-family}),
\[
\Pr[X < Y,\ X \le u]
= \int_0^u \lambda e^{-\lambda s}\, \Pr[Y > s]\,ds
= \int_0^u \lambda e^{-(\lambda + \mu)s}\,ds
= \frac{\lambda}{\lambda + \mu}\bigl(1 - e^{-(\lambda + \mu)u}\bigr) .
\]
The diagonal \(s = t\) has inner integral zero, so
\(\Pr[X = Y] = 0\), and \(u = \infty\) gives \(\Pr[X < Y]\). On the
event \(X < Y\) the winning time is \(X\), so the displayed formula
is \(\Pr[X < Y,\ \min(X, Y) \le u]\); adding its mirror for
\(Y < X\) gives \(\Pr[\min(X, Y) \le u] = 1 - e^{-(\lambda + \mu)u}\),
whence \(\min(X, Y)\) is exponential with rate \(\lambda + \mu\) by
the characterisation in \cref{probability:lem-exp-tail}, and the
formula is exactly the product
\(\Pr[X < Y] \cdot \Pr[\min(X, Y) \le u]\). Differencing at two
values of \(u\) extends the factorisation from half-lines to
intervals.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Exponential clock process]
\label{probability:def-clock}
An \emph{exponential clock of rate} \(\lambda\) --- a \emph{Poisson
process}, by \cref{probability:thm-poisson-count} --- is
an independent family \(G_1, G_2, \ldots\) of
\(\mathrm{Exp}(\lambda)\) quantities, the \emph{gaps}. Its
\emph{arrival times} are the partial sums
\(S_n = G_1 + \cdots + G_n\), with \(S_0 = 0\), and its \emph{arrival
count} up to time \(t \ge 0\) is \(N(t) = \sup\{n : S_n \le t\}\),
the number of arrivals in \([0, t]\), a value in \(\N \cup \{\infty\}\)
that \cref{probability:thm-poisson-count} shows finite with
probability one. The event \(N(t) = n\) is
\(S_n \le t < S_{n+1}\), a joint event of the first \(n + 1\) gaps.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Arrival-time density]\label{probability:lem-erlang}
The \(n\)-th arrival time \(S_n\) of an exponential clock of rate
\(\lambda\) has density
\(f_n(s) = \lambda^n s^{n-1} e^{-\lambda s}/(n-1)!\) for \(s \ge 0\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Induction on \(n\): \(f_1\) is the exponential density, and
\(S_{n+1} = S_n + G_{n+1}\) with \(S_n\) a function of the first
\(n\) gaps, hence independent of \(G_{n+1}\)
(\cref{probability:def-density-family}), so
\cref{probability:lem-convolution} gives
\[
f_{n+1}(s)
= \int_0^s \frac{\lambda^n u^{n-1} e^{-\lambda u}}{(n-1)!}\,
  \lambda e^{-\lambda(s - u)}\,du
= \frac{\lambda^{n+1} e^{-\lambda s}}{(n-1)!} \int_0^s u^{n-1}\,du
= \frac{\lambda^{n+1} s^{n} e^{-\lambda s}}{n!} . \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Poisson law]\label{probability:def-poisson}
A random variable \(K\) with values in \(\N\) is \emph{Poisson with
mean} \(\nu > 0\) if \(\Pr[K = k] = e^{-\nu}\nu^k/k!\) for every
\(k \ge 0\). The weights sum to \(1\) by the exponential series
\(e^{\nu} = \sum_{k \ge 0} \nu^k/k!\), and
\(\E[K] = \sum_{k \ge 1} k\, e^{-\nu}\nu^k/k!
= \nu e^{-\nu} \sum_{k \ge 1} \nu^{k-1}/(k-1)! = \nu\), so the
parameter is the mean.
\end{definition}

An arrival count is a function of the gaps of a clock family, so it
lives on no finite or countable space, and the expectation of
\cref{probability:def-exp} does not literally apply to it. The
following convention covers \(N(t)\) above and the count \(X\) of
\cref{probability:thm-negbin} below. Let
\(X\) be a variable with values in \(\N \cup \{\infty\}\) that is a
function of the gaps, each event \(X = x\) with \(x\) finite being a
joint event of finitely many gaps, with the probability of
\cref{probability:def-density-family}. Events \(X \in B\), for sets
\(B\) of finite values, have the probabilities
\(\sum_{x \in B} \Pr[X = x]\) (\cref{probability:rem-extension}), and
when \(X\) is finite with probability \(1\) its \emph{mean} is
defined by the law,
\[
\E[X] = \sum_{x \ge 0} x\,\Pr[X = x] ,
\]
a sum of nonnegative terms, permitted to be \(+\infty\) as in the
convention preceding \cref{probability:lem-tailsum}. On a finite or
countable space this sum is the second expression of
\cref{probability:def-exp}, the expectation regrouped by value
(\cref{probability:def-countable}), so the two definitions agree
whenever both apply.

\begin{theorem}[Poisson arrival counts]
\label{probability:thm-poisson-count}
For an exponential clock of rate \(\lambda\) and every \(t \ge 0\),
the arrival count \(N(t)\) is Poisson with mean \(\lambda t\):
\[
\Pr[N(t) = n] = e^{-\lambda t}\frac{(\lambda t)^n}{n!},
\qquad n = 0, 1, 2, \ldots
\]
In particular \(N(t)\) is finite with probability \(1\) and
\(\E[N(t)] = \lambda t\): a rate-\(\lambda\) clock delivers
\(\lambda\) arrivals per unit time on average.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(n = 0\) the event is \(G_1 > t\), of probability
\(e^{-\lambda t}\). For \(n \ge 1\) the event
\(S_n \le t < S_n + G_{n+1}\) is, in the \((S_n, G_{n+1})\)-coordinates,
the region \(s \le t\), \(g > t - s\), whose section at fixed \(g\)
is the interval \((t - g, t]\); the quantity \(S_n\) is a function of
the first \(n\) gaps with the density of
\cref{probability:lem-erlang}, so \cref{probability:lem-integrate-out}
gives
\[
\Pr[N(t) = n]
= \int_0^t \frac{\lambda^n s^{n-1} e^{-\lambda s}}{(n-1)!}\,
  \Pr[G_{n+1} > t - s]\,ds
= \frac{\lambda^n e^{-\lambda t}}{(n-1)!} \int_0^t s^{n-1}\,ds
= e^{-\lambda t}\frac{(\lambda t)^n}{n!} .
\]
The event \(N(t) < \infty\) is the disjoint union of the events
\(N(t) = n\), so its probability is the sum of the weights
(\cref{probability:rem-extension}), which is \(1\)
(\cref{probability:def-poisson}); and the mean --- the sum over the
law, by the convention above --- is the one computed in
\cref{probability:def-poisson}.
\end{proof}

Markov's inequality applied to \(X\) itself gives a bound decaying only
like \(1/a\); applied to \(e^{tX}\), it gives a bound decaying
exponentially, with the free parameter \(t\) optimised for the
threshold. This is the Chernoff method.

\begin{definition}[Moment generating function]\label{probability:def-mgf}
For a real-valued random variable \(X\) with finite or countable
image --- one on a finite or countable space, or an arrival count of a
clock family in the sense of the convention preceding
\cref{probability:thm-poisson-count}, restricted to its finite values
(the value \(\infty\) has probability zero there), whose law is
countably additive over its values:
\(\Pr[X \in B] = \sum_{x \in B} \Pr[X = x]\) for
every set \(B\) of values (\cref{probability:rem-extension}) --- and a
real \(t\), the \emph{moment generating function} is
\[
M_X(t) = \sum_{x} e^{tx}\,\Pr[X = x] \in (0, \infty] ,
\]
the sum running over the finite values of \(X\); its terms are positive,
so
it either converges or is \(+\infty\). On a countable space it is
\(\E[e^{tX}]\), the expectation regrouped by value, and
\(M_X(0) = \sum_x \Pr[X = x] = 1\).
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The Chernoff method]\label{probability:thm-chernoff}
Let \(X\) be as above, \(a\) real, and \(t > 0\) with
\(M_X(t) < \infty\). Then
\[
\Pr[X \ge a] \le e^{-ta} M_X(t) ,
\]
and hence \(\Pr[X \ge a] \le \inf_t e^{-ta} M_X(t)\), the infimum
over the \(t > 0\) at which \(M_X\) is finite.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The events \(X = x\) are disjoint, so
\(\Pr[X \ge a] = \sum_{x \ge a} \Pr[X = x]\) by countable additivity
(\cref{probability:prop-countadd}, or the clause of
\cref{probability:def-mgf} for a clock family). Since \(t > 0\), each
term with \(x \ge a\) satisfies
\(\Pr[X = x] \le e^{t(x - a)}\,\Pr[X = x]\), and summing gives
\(\Pr[X \ge a] \le e^{-ta} \sum_x e^{tx}\,\Pr[X = x]\), the terms with
\(x < a\) being nonnegative. This is Markov's inequality
(\cref{probability:prop-markov}) applied to \(e^{tX}\), written on the
law of \(X\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Chernoff bound for a Poisson variable]
\label{probability:cor-chernoff-poisson}
Let \(K\) be Poisson with mean \(\nu\) and let \(a > \nu\). Then
\(M_K(t) = \exp\bigl(\nu(e^t - 1)\bigr)\) for every real \(t\), and
\[
\Pr[K \ge a] \le e^{a - \nu}\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{a}\Bigr)^{a} .
\]
In particular, for \(\nu = \mu L\) and \(a = cL\) with \(c > \mu\),
the bound reads \(e^{L(c - \mu)}\,(c/\mu)^{-cL}\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Expanding,
\(M_K(t) = \sum_k e^{tk} e^{-\nu}\nu^k/k!
= e^{-\nu}\sum_k (\nu e^t)^k/k! = e^{-\nu} e^{\nu e^t}\). The
exponent \(\phi(t) = -ta + \nu(e^t - 1)\) of the Chernoff bound has
\(\phi'(t) = -a + \nu e^t\) and \(\phi''(t) = \nu e^t > 0\), so it
is minimised at \(e^{t} = a/\nu\), a positive \(t\) exactly when
\(a > \nu\); there \(\phi = -a\log(a/\nu) + a - \nu\), with \(\log\)
the natural logarithm --- base-free \(\log\) is natural throughout,
base two being written \(\log_2\) --- and exponentiating gives the
bound. Substituting \(\nu = \mu L\) and \(a = cL\) gives
\(e^{cL - \mu L}(\mu/c)^{cL}\).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Negative-binomial arrival count]
\label{probability:thm-negbin}
Let two exponential clocks be independent --- their gaps together
form one independent family --- with rates \(\lambda\), the
\emph{honest} clock, and \(\mu\), the \emph{adversary} clock, and let
\(X\) be the number of adversary arrivals strictly before the
\(L\)-th honest arrival, for a fixed \(L \ge 1\). Then \(X\) is
finite with probability \(1\), with the \emph{negative binomial} law
\[
\Pr[X = m] = \binom{m + L - 1}{m}\, p^{L} q^{m},
\qquad p = \frac{\lambda}{\lambda + \mu},\quad
q = \frac{\mu}{\lambda + \mu},
\qquad m = 0, 1, 2, \ldots,
\]
of mean \(Lq/p = L\mu/\lambda\), and, for every real \(t\) with
\(\mu e^{t} < \lambda + \mu\),
\[
M_X(t) = \Bigl(\frac{\lambda}{\lambda + \mu - \mu e^{t}}\Bigr)^{L} ;
\]
with the honest rate normalised to \(1\) this is
\(M_X(t) = \bigl(1/(1 + \mu - \mu e^t)\bigr)^{L}\). Only the ratio
\(\mu/\lambda\) enters.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write \(S_L\) for the \(L\)-th honest arrival time, a function of
the honest gaps with the density \(f_L\) of
\cref{probability:lem-erlang}, and \(N_A\) for the adversary's
arrival count. The event \(X = m\) differs from \(N_A(S_L) = m\)
only on the events \(S_L = A_1 + \cdots + A_m\) and
\(S_L = A_1 + \cdots + A_{m+1}\), with \(A_i\) the adversary gaps;
each of these, in the coordinates \((S_L, A_1, \dots, A_{m+1})\), has
a single point as its section at fixed adversary gaps, hence
probability zero (\cref{probability:def-density}), so
\(\Pr[X = m] = \Pr[N_A(S_L) = m]\). The latter event is the region
\(A_1 + \cdots + A_m \le S_L < A_1 + \cdots + A_{m+1}\), whose
section at fixed adversary gaps is an interval. By
\cref{probability:lem-integrate-out} and
\cref{probability:thm-poisson-count},
\begin{align*}
\Pr[X = m]
&= \int_0^\infty f_L(s)\,\Pr[N_A(s) = m]\,ds
= \frac{\lambda^L \mu^m}{(L-1)!\,m!}
  \int_0^\infty s^{m+L-1} e^{-(\lambda + \mu)s}\,ds \\
&= \frac{\lambda^L \mu^m}{(L-1)!\,m!}\cdot
  \frac{(m+L-1)!}{(\lambda + \mu)^{m+L}} ,
\end{align*}
using \(\int_0^\infty s^{k} e^{-\alpha s}\,ds = k!/\alpha^{k+1}\)
for \(\alpha > 0\), which \(k\) integrations by parts establish.
Rearranging the factorials gives the binomial coefficient and the
powers of \(p\) and \(q\).

For the generating function, set \(x = q e^{t}\), so that
\(0 \le x < 1\) exactly when \(\mu e^t < \lambda + \mu\). Then
\(M_X(t) = p^L \sum_{m \ge 0} \binom{m+L-1}{m} x^m\), and we claim
the series is \((1 - x)^{-L}\), by induction on \(L\). For \(L = 1\)
it is the geometric series. For the step, write
\(T_L(n) = \sum_{m=0}^{n} \binom{m+L-1}{m} x^m\) for the partial
sums. Pascal's rule
\(\binom{m+L}{m} = \binom{m+L-1}{m-1} + \binom{m+L-1}{m}\) --- sort
the \(m\)-subsets of \(m + L\) items, which the coefficients count
(\cref{finitefields:lem:binomcount}), by whether they contain a
distinguished item --- gives, on subtracting \(x\,T_{L+1}(n)\) from
\(T_{L+1}(n)\) term by term,
\[
(1 - x)\,T_{L+1}(n) = T_L(n) - \binom{n+L}{n} x^{n+1} .
\]
Hence \(T_{L+1}(n) \le T_L(n)/(1 - x) \le (1 - x)^{-L-1}\) by the
induction hypothesis: the partial sums \(T_{L+1}(n)\) increase and
are bounded, so the series converges, and its terms
\(\binom{n+L}{n} x^{n}\) tend to zero. Letting \(n \to \infty\) in
the display gives
\((1 - x) \sum_{m \ge 0} \binom{m+L}{m} x^m = (1 - x)^{-L}\), the
claim for \(L + 1\). Hence
\(M_X(t) = \bigl(p/(1 - q e^t)\bigr)^L\), which is the displayed form
after multiplying numerator and denominator by \(\lambda + \mu\). At
\(t = 0\) this gives \(\sum_m \Pr[X = m] = 1\); the event
\(X < \infty\) is the disjoint union of the events \(X = m\), so its
probability is this sum (\cref{probability:rem-extension}), and \(X\)
is finite with probability \(1\). For the mean --- the sum over the
law, by the convention preceding \cref{probability:thm-poisson-count}
--- \(m\binom{m+L-1}{m} = L\binom{m+L-1}{m-1}\), so
\(\E[X] = Lq\,p^L \sum_{k \ge 0} \binom{k+L}{k} q^k
= Lq\,p^L (1 - q)^{-L-1} = Lq/p\) by the same series with \(L + 1\)
in place of \(L\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The coin-sequence form]
\label{probability:rem-negbin-coins}
The same law arises without clocks. In an infinite sequence of
independent trials (\cref{probability:def-trials}) succeeding with
probability \(p\), the number of failures strictly before the
\(L\)-th success equals \(m\) exactly when the \((m + L)\)-th trial
succeeds and, among the first \(m + L - 1\), precisely \(m\) fail:
\(\binom{m+L-1}{m}\) orders, one per choice of the \(m\) failing
positions (\cref{finitefields:lem:binomcount}), each of probability
\(p^{L-1} q^m \cdot p\). The two-clock race and memorylessness
(\cref{probability:lem-race} and \cref{probability:lem-exp-tail})
identify the clock model's attribution sequence --- which clock
fires next --- with such a coin sequence of success probability
\(\lambda/(\lambda + \mu)\); later volumes carry out that
identification.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Chernoff bound for the arrival count]
\label{probability:thm-chernoff-negbin}
In the setting of \cref{probability:thm-negbin} with honest rate
\(1\) and adversary rate \(\mu\), for every \(c > \mu\),
\[
\Pr[X \ge cL]
\le \Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{1 + c}{1 + \mu}\Bigr)^{1 + c}
          \Bigl(\frac{\mu}{c}\Bigr)^{c}\Bigr]^{L} ,
\]
the Chernoff bound optimised at
\(e^{t} = c(1 + \mu)/(\mu(1 + c))\). The base in brackets is
strictly less than \(1\), so the bound decays exponentially in
\(L\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The count \(X\) is finite with probability one with values in \(\N\)
(\cref{probability:thm-negbin}) and has a law countably additive over
its values (\cref{probability:rem-extension}), so
\cref{probability:thm-chernoff} applies: with
\cref{probability:thm-negbin},
\(\Pr[X \ge cL] \le e^{-tcL} M_X(t) = e^{L\phi(t)}\) with
\(\phi(t) = -tc - \log(1 + \mu - \mu e^{t})\), for
\(0 < t < \log((1 + \mu)/\mu)\). On that interval
\[
\phi'(t) = -c + \frac{\mu e^t}{1 + \mu - \mu e^t},
\qquad
\phi''(t) = \frac{\mu e^t (1 + \mu)}{(1 + \mu - \mu e^t)^2} > 0 ,
\]
so \(\phi'\) is strictly increasing (positive derivative) and
vanishes at most once, namely where \(\mu e^t (1 + c) = c(1 + \mu)\),
that is, at \(e^{t^*} = c(1 + \mu)/(\mu(1 + c))\); this lies in the
interval because \(c > \mu\) gives \(e^{t^*} > 1\) and
\(c/(1 + c) < 1\) gives \(e^{t^*} < (1 + \mu)/\mu\). Thus \(\phi\)
decreases before \(t^*\) and increases after it, and \(\phi(t^*)\) is
its minimum on the interval. At \(t^*\),
\(1 + \mu - \mu e^{t^*} = (1 + \mu)/(1 + c)\), so
\(e^{\phi(t^*)} = (e^{t^*})^{-c}\,(1 + c)/(1 + \mu)\), which is the
bracketed base. Finally \(\phi(0) = 0\) and \(\phi\) decreases on
\([0, t^*]\), since \(\phi'(0) = \mu - c < 0\), so \(\phi(t^*) < 0\):
the base is below \(1\).
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[The Poisson expression is not a bound for the
arrival count]\label{probability:exp-negbin-tail}
% Source: .wip/rewrite/math-guide/compute/clocks.py
%   mu=1/2, c=3/4, L=40: exact tail = 5.0512e-02, clock bound = 2.5324e-01, Poisson expression = 1.1487e-01
%   mu=1/2, c=3/4, L=200: exact tail = 1.0859e-04, clock bound = 1.0416e-03, Poisson expression = 2.0000e-05  [Poisson expression BELOW the exact tail]
%   mu=1/2, c=3/4, L=400: exact tail = 8.2274e-08, clock bound = 1.0849e-06, Poisson expression = 4.0001e-10  [Poisson expression BELOW the exact tail]
%   mu=1/2, c=3/4: clock-bound base = 0.966248, Poisson-expression base = 0.947338
With the honest rate \(1\), the arrival count \(X\) has mean
\(\mu L\), the same as the Poisson variable of
\cref{probability:cor-chernoff-poisson} with \(\nu = \mu L\), which
counts adversary arrivals in a window of fixed length \(L\); the two
laws differ because the window here ends at the random time
\(S_L\). Substituting one for the other is an error. At
\(\mu = 1/2\) and \(c = 3/4\), the exact tail \(\Pr[X \ge cL]\)
(summing \cref{probability:thm-negbin}), the bound of
\cref{probability:thm-chernoff-negbin}, and the Poisson expression
\(e^{L(c - \mu)}(c/\mu)^{-cL}\) are
\[
\begin{array}{r@{\qquad}lll}
L & \text{exact tail} & \text{clock bound} & \text{Poisson expression} \\
40  & 5.05 \cdot 10^{-2} & 2.53 \cdot 10^{-1} & 1.15 \cdot 10^{-1} \\
200 & 1.09 \cdot 10^{-4} & 1.04 \cdot 10^{-3} & 2.00 \cdot 10^{-5} \\
400 & 8.23 \cdot 10^{-8} & 1.08 \cdot 10^{-6} & 4.00 \cdot 10^{-10}
\end{array}
\]
At \(L = 200\) and \(L = 400\) the Poisson expression lies
\emph{below} the exact tail, so it is not an upper bound for the
arrival count; its base per unit of \(L\) is \(0.9473\), against
\(0.9662\) for the valid bound.
\end{example}

Later volumes build block-discovery and extraction analyses on these
results: block clocks are the exponential clocks above, their races and
arrival counts the lemmas of this unit, block-attribution sequences are
infinite sequences of independent trials, extractor running times are
the waiting times of the previous unit (the geometric waiting time and
Wald's identity), and the limit arguments instantiate
\cref{probability:thm-continuity}.

\subsection{Asymptotic notation}

Efficiency and hardness are stated by comparing how functions of an
integer parameter grow as that parameter tends to infinity, in the
Bachmann--Landau notation. Throughout,
functions map \(\N\) (or a cofinite subset of it) to the nonnegative
reals \(\R_{\ge 0}\), and ``for sufficiently large \(n\)'' means
``for all \(n\) beyond some threshold \(n_0\)''.
